# ETH-W Pre-writes

I-CRUPAC paragraphs to adapt and retype in the exam. The bold part labels (**I**, **C**,
**R**, **P**, **A**, **C**) are for finding your place only — don't type them. Swap the
square-bracket placeholders for the facts. The **Application** part is a pattern (which
facts trigger the rule and how to run it), not a worked answer.

The **Rule** part sets out each rule in its own words, with every limb, exception,
definition and case factor the answer may need. When retyping, keep the operative words
and only the limbs, exceptions and factors the facts engage.

The *Source note* under each Pre-write is for checking only and is never retyped. Source
aliases (PCR, LPA, SYL, SQ, L1–L10, PYP21–23, PS1–3, PD x) are defined in `outline.md`.
"Cyril" = `Bar Exam/Seniors Notes/Cyril - Part B/For exam/Exam files/04122025 ETH Thursday.docx`
(secondary; course materials win). Lecture slides are cited only as where a point was
found, never as authority in the text.

**Opening line for any answer:** A legal practitioner's duty to the client is subject only
to the paramount duty to the court, and must be fulfilled in a way that upholds the
standing and integrity of the legal profession (r 4(a)–(b) PCR).
*Source note: PCR p9.*

**Closing line for any breach:** Such a breach may amount to improper conduct or
misconduct unbefitting an advocate and solicitor, which is due cause for discipline under
s 83(2)(b) and (h) of the Legal Profession Act 1966.
*Source note: LPA p217–218; full Pre-write at 0-35.*

**Answer shape:** issue → rule, PD/GN → cases → competing considerations and exceptions →
apply to facts → conclude whether there is a breach (and, for conflicts, whether the
lawyer may act and with what safeguards).
*Source note: INTRO2 p25–26.*

**Which conflict rule?** Two or more clients in one matter → r 20 (0-7, 0-8). Former
client and their confidential information → r 21 (0-11). The lawyer's, their family's or
the firm's own interest → r 22 (0-13, 0-14). Lawyer will be a material witness → r 11(3)
(0-22, 0-23). Firm drafted the disputed document for both sides → r 20(8) (0-9).
Opponent demands that the firm withdraw → the court's power to restrain (0-12).
Information barriers → 0-10.

Contents: Regulation 35–36 · Client relationship 1–6, 37–39 · Conflicts 7–14, 40–43 ·
Retainer end 15–16 · Court 17–21, 44–52 · Witness-lawyer 22–23 · Other lawyers and persons
24–25, 53–63 · Staff 26–28, 64–65 · Publicity 29–31, 66–67 · Fees and client money 32–34, 68–70.

## Regulation of the profession

### eth-w-0-35 — Why ethics matters; character; due cause and sanctions (s 83 LPA)
*Keywords: character, integrity, honesty, admission, fit and proper, due cause, s 83 LPA, s 83(1), s 83(2)(a), s 83(2)(b), s 83(2)(h), s 83(2)(i), struck off, suspension, penalty, censure, personal misconduct, misconduct unbefitting, grossly improper conduct, Ahmad Khalis, Rasif David, Tay Quan Li Leon, Sean Wong, Gabriel Silas Tang Rafferty, Udeh Kumar, Chia Choon Yang*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer]'s conduct shows due cause for discipline under s 83 of the Legal Profession Act 1966 and what sanction may follow. **C** [Lawyer]'s conduct [is / is likely to be] due cause under s 83(2)[(b) / (h)] LPA, which covers [fraudulent or grossly improper conduct in the discharge of professional duty, or a breach of the PCR amounting to improper conduct / misconduct unbefitting an advocate and solicitor].
**R** All advocates and solicitors are subject to the control of the Supreme Court and on due
cause shown may be struck off, suspended for up to five years, ordered to pay a penalty of up
to $100,000, or censured (s 83(1) LPA). Due cause includes a conviction implying a defect of
character, fraudulent or grossly improper conduct in professional duty or a breach of the
rules amounting to improper conduct (s 83(2)(a)–(b) LPA), paid procurement of work
(s 83(2)(d)–(f)), misconduct unbefitting an advocate and solicitor (s 83(2)(h)), and an
incompatible business (s 83(2)(i)).

> **Rule in full**
> - Section 83(1) LPA applies, which states that all advocates and solicitors are subject to the control of the Supreme Court and are liable on due cause shown
>   - (a) to be struck off the roll of advocates and solicitors;
>   - (b) to be suspended from practice for a period not exceeding 5 years;
>   - (c) to pay a penalty of not more than $100,000;
>   - (d) to be censured; or
>   - (e) to suffer the penalty in (c) in addition to the punishment in (b) or (d).
> - Section 83(2) states that due cause may be shown by proof that an advocate and solicitor
>   - (a) has been convicted of a criminal offence, implying a defect of character which makes him or her unfit for his or her profession;
>   - (b) has been guilty of fraudulent or grossly improper conduct in the discharge of his or her professional duty, or guilty of such a breach of
>     - (i) any usage or rule of conduct made by the Professional Conduct Council under s 71 or by the Council under the Act,
>     - (ii) Part 5A or any rules made under s 70H, or
>     - (iii) any rules made under s 36M(2)(r), as amounts to improper conduct or practice as an advocate and solicitor;
>   - (c) has been adjudicated bankrupt and has been guilty of any of the acts or omissions listed in s 394(5) of the Insolvency, Restructuring and Dissolution Act 2018;
>   - (d) has tendered or given or consented to retention, out of any fee payable to him or her for his or her services, of any gratification for having procured the employment in any legal business of himself or herself or of any other advocate and solicitor;
>   - (e) has, directly or indirectly, procured or attempted to procure the employment of himself or herself or of any advocate and solicitor through or by the instruction of any person to whom any remuneration for obtaining such employment has been given by him or her or agreed or promised to be so given;
>   - (f) has accepted employment in any legal business through a person who has been proclaimed a tout under any written law;
>   - (h) has been guilty of such misconduct unbefitting an advocate and solicitor as an officer of the Supreme Court or as a member of an honourable profession;
>   - (i) carries on by himself or herself, or any person in his or her employment, any trade, business or calling that detracts from the profession of law or is in any way incompatible with it, or is employed in any such trade, business or calling;
>   - (j) has contravened any of the provisions of the Act if the contravention warrants disciplinary action; or
>   - (k) has been disbarred, struck off, suspended, ordered to pay a penalty, censured or reprimanded as a legal practitioner in any other jurisdiction.
> - The PCR are made by the Professional Conduct Council under s 71(2) LPA, so a breach of the PCR falls within s 83(2)(b) when it amounts to improper conduct.

**P** The standard is objective and set by the court, and there is a paramount public interest in deterring the lawyer and others like-minded, and in protecting public confidence in the administration of justice (*Law Society of Singapore v Ahmad Khalis bin Abdul Ghani* [2006] 4 SLR(R) 308 at [2]–[5], [81]–[82]). Honesty and integrity are required in a lawyer's personal as well as professional life (*Law Society of Singapore v Rasif David* [2008] 2 SLR(R) 955 at [50]–[60]). Lawyers are "ministers in the temple of justice", so admission requires good character, not just exam passes (*Re Tay Quan Li Leon* [2022] SGHC 133 at [1], [34], [46]); cheating in the Bar examinations shows a character deficit (*Re Sean Wong Wai Loong* [2022] SGHC 237 at [76]); and so does a lack of candour in the admission process (*Re Gabriel Silas Tang Rafferty* [2024] SGHC 82 at [1]–[2], [38]–[39]). Misconduct involving dishonesty will almost invariably lead to striking off where it reveals a character defect that makes the lawyer unsuitable for the profession (*Law Society of Singapore v Udeh Kumar s/o Sethuraju* [2017] SGHC 141 at [25]–[30]; *Law Society of Singapore v Chia Choon Yang* [2018] SGHC 174 at [44]–[55]).

**A** First, ask whether [Lawyer]'s [conduct] was in the discharge of professional duty. If it was, s 83(2)(b) asks whether it was fraudulent or grossly improper, or a breach of [r X PCR] serious enough to amount to improper conduct: here [facts]. If the conduct was personal, s 83(2)(h) still catches it as misconduct unbefitting a member of an honourable profession: here [facts]. A conviction implying a defect of character engages s 83(2)(a); paying for referrals engages s 83(2)(d)–(e); an incompatible business engages s 83(2)(i). Second, on sanction, ask whether [Lawyer]'s conduct was dishonest. If so, striking off is the likely outcome under s 83(1)(a); if it shows misjudgement rather than a lack of integrity, suspension under s 83(1)(b) or a penalty under s 83(1)(c) is more likely. **C** [Lawyer]'s conduct is due cause under s 83(2)[(b) / (h)] LPA, and the likely sanction is [striking off / suspension / a penalty].

*Source note: s 83(1)–(2) LPA p217–219; s 71(2) as the PCR's enabling power, PCR p4; Ahmad Khalis, Rasif David, Tay Quan Li Leon, Sean Wong, Gabriel Silas Tang Rafferty pinpoints SYL p1; Udeh Kumar 2017, Chia Choon Yang pinpoints SYL p2; summaries Cyril §I.1.1–1.2 (secondary). This also serves as the stock closing line in the preamble.*

### eth-w-0-36 — Who the PCR apply to; interpretive principles
*Keywords: r 3, application, practising certificate, s 15 admission, regulated foreign lawyer, registered foreign lawyer, law practice, Joint Law Venture, Formal Law Alliance, Qualifying Foreign Law Practice, r 4, principles, spirit of the rules, paramount duty, access to justice, keep up to date, Then Khek Khoon*

**I** The issue is whether the PCR [Part X / r X] apply to [person / law practice] and how they are read. **C** [Part X / r X] [applies / does not apply] to [person], because r 3 PCR applies Part [X] to [category].
**R** Part 2 (rules 5 to 8A) applies to every
solicitor with a practising certificate, every person admitted under s 15 of the Legal
Profession Act 1966 and every regulated foreign lawyer; Part 3 (practice of Singapore law and
in Singapore courts) applies to solicitors with practising certificates and s 15 admittees,
with limited extension to registered foreign lawyers; Parts 4 and 5 apply to law practices
and, for publicity, practitioners (r 3(1)–(7) PCR). The Rules are read in light of r 4: a
paramount duty to the court; a duty to the client subject only to that; honour and integrity
to tribunals, clients, the public and other lawyers; upholding the law; access to justice;
fairness and courtesy to every person; and keeping up to date with the law (r 4(a)–(h) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 3 PCR applies.
> - Rule 3(1) states that Part 2 (rules 5–8A: the client, other legal practitioners and other persons) applies to
>   - (a) every solicitor who has in force a practising certificate or is registered under s 36F of the Act;
>   - (b) every person admitted under s 15 of the Act; and
>   - (c) every regulated foreign lawyer.
> - Rule 3(2) states that Division 1 of Part 3 (rules 9–15B, the administration of justice), insofar as it relates to any relevant proceedings or relevant appeal, applies to
>   - (a) every solicitor who has in force a practising certificate;
>   - (b) every person admitted under s 15; and
>   - (c) every regulated foreign lawyer registered under s 36P.
> - Rule 3(3) states that Division 1 of Part 3, insofar as it relates to any other proceedings before a court, applies to
>   - (a) every solicitor who has in force a practising certificate; and
>   - (b) every person admitted under s 15.
> - Rule 3(4) states that, except as provided in paragraphs (2) and (3), Part 3 (rules 9–34) applies to
>   - (a) every solicitor who has in force a practising certificate or is registered under s 36F;
>   - (b) every person admitted under s 15; and
>   - (c) every foreign lawyer registered under s 36B.
> - Rule 3(5) states that Division 2 of Part 3 (rules 16–26) applies
>   - (a) to every Singapore law practice; and
>   - (b) in connection with the practice of Singapore law, to every Joint Law Venture, Formal Law Alliance, Qualifying Foreign Law Practice and licensed foreign law practice.
> - Rule 3(6) states that Part 4 (management, rules 35–36) applies to every law practice.
> - Rule 3(7) states that Part 5 (touting and publicity, rules 37–49) applies
>   - (a) to every solicitor who has in force a practising certificate or is registered under s 36F, every person admitted under s 15 and every foreign lawyer registered under s 36B;
>   - (b) to every Singapore law practice; and
>   - (c) in connection with the practice of Singapore law, to every Joint Law Venture, Formal Law Alliance, Qualifying Foreign Law Practice and licensed foreign law practice.
> - Rule 3(8) applies Part 5A (third-party funding) to the same practitioners as r 3(1), every Singapore law practice and every other licensed law practice.
> - Rule 4 PCR states that the following principles guide the interpretation of the Rules:
>   - (a) a legal practitioner has a paramount duty to the court, which takes precedence over the duty to the client;
>   - (b) the duty to the client is subject only to the duty to the court, and must at all times be fulfilled in a manner that upholds the standing and integrity of the Singapore legal system and the legal profession in Singapore;
>   - (c) a legal practitioner has a duty to discharge honourably and with integrity all responsibilities to any tribunal before which the legal practitioner appears, the legal practitioner's clients, the public and other members of the legal profession;
>   - (d) a legal practitioner must uphold the laws of Singapore in practice;
>   - (e) a legal practitioner must facilitate the access of members of the public to justice;
>   - (f) a legal practitioner must be fair and courteous towards every person in respect of the legal practitioner's professional conduct;
>   - (g) a legal practitioner must ensure that his or her professional conduct is always consistent with the values of the legal profession in each jurisdiction in which he or she is qualified, and, when giving advice in any other jurisdiction, with the values of the profession there; and
>   - (h) a legal practitioner must keep up to date with all pertinent developments in the law in his or her area of practice.

**P** It is the spirit and intent of the rules, not just their wording, that set the standard for assessing conduct, so the rules are read with a proper appreciation of the mischief to be averted rather than in a strict textual fashion (*Then Khek Khoon v Arjun Permanand Samtani* [2012] 2 SLR 451 at [47], quoting *Law Society of Singapore v Tan Phuay Khiang* [2007] 3 SLR(R) 477 at [100]). A lawyer without a valid practising certificate was still disciplined for prohibited borrowing (*Law Society of Singapore v Chia Chwee Imm Helen* [2022] SGHC 214), and personal misconduct is caught by s 83(2)(h) LPA in any event.

**A** Here [Lawyer] [has no current practising certificate / is a foreign lawyer registered under s 36B / is admitted under s 15 for one case / acted personally, not professionally]. Take the category from r 3, then the Part the issue falls in: a conflict under r 20–22 is in Division 2 of Part 3, so r 3(4)–(5) governs; a publicity issue is in Part 5, so r 3(7) governs. Then read the rule purposively in light of r 4: [the relevant principle, e.g. r 4(b), duty to client subject only to duty to court]. **C** [Part X / r X] [applies] to [Lawyer] under r 3([n]) PCR, read purposively under r 4.

*Source note: r 3 PCR p7–9; r 4 PCR p9; Part and Division ranges from the PCR arrangement of provisions p1–3; Then Khek Khoon pinpoint SYL p6 (listed under r 11); "spirit and intent" is at [47] of Then Khek Khoon ([2012] SGHC 17), not [30]–[36], read from the judgment on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026); Chia Chwee Imm Helen SYL p4, Cyril §II.5 (secondary).*

## Client relationship

### eth-w-0-1 — Who is a client; implied retainer
*Keywords: client, implied retainer, s 2(1) LPA, contentious business, non-contentious business, liable to pay costs, non-client, duty of care, Ahmad Khalis, Anwar Patrick Adrian, Lee Suet Fern, Uthayasurian Sidambaram, AEL, objective test, putative client*

**I** The issue is whether [X] is [Lawyer]'s client, so that the duties of loyalty, confidentiality and care under the PCR are owed to [X]. **C** [X] [is / is not] [Lawyer]'s client under s 2(1) LPA, which defines a client to include anyone who retains or is about to retain a solicitor or who is liable to pay the solicitor's costs, [and an implied retainer arose / did not arise].
**R** Under s 2(1) of the Legal Profession Act 1966, a
"client" includes any person who retains or employs, or is about to retain or employ, a
solicitor, and any person liable to pay the solicitor's costs. A retainer need not be in
writing: it may be implied where, viewed objectively, an intention to form a
solicitor-client relationship ought fairly to be imputed to both parties (*Law Society of
Singapore v Ahmad Khalis bin Abdul Ghani* [2006] 4 SLR(R) 308 at [64]–[73]).

> **Rule in full**
> - Section 2(1) LPA applies, which states that "client" includes
>   - (a) in relation to contentious business, any person who, as a principal or on behalf of another person, retains or employs, or is about to retain or employ, a solicitor, and any person who is or may be liable to pay the costs of a solicitor, law corporation or limited liability law partnership; and
>   - (b) in relation to non-contentious business,
>     - (i) any person who, as a principal or on behalf of another, or as a trustee, an executor or an administrator, or in any other capacity, has power (express or implied) to retain or employ, and retains or employs or is about to retain or employ, a solicitor, law corporation or limited liability law partnership; and
>     - (ii) any person for the time being liable to pay a solicitor, law corporation or limited liability law partnership for its services any costs.
> - The label the parties use does not matter; the question is whether the relationship bore the hallmarks of a solicitor-client relationship, and a client may act in a representative capacity (*Law Society of Singapore v Uthayasurian Sidambaram* [2009] 4 SLR(R) 674 at [39]–[44]).
> - A retainer need not be in writing: it may be implied where, on an objective consideration of all the circumstances, an intention to enter into such a contractual relationship ought fairly and properly to be imputed to the parties, and the implication must be so clear that the solicitor ought to have appreciated it; the lawyer's own view is not conclusive, and the question is whether it was reasonable, on the objective facts, for each side to reach the conclusion it did (*Law Society of Singapore v Ahmad Khalis bin Abdul Ghani* [2006] 4 SLR(R) 308 at [64], [66]).
> - The court considers a non-exhaustive list of factors:
>   - (1) who is paying the putative solicitor's fees;
>   - (2) who is providing instructions;
>   - (3) whether a contractual relationship existed between them in the past;
>   - (4) whether express advice was given by the putative solicitor and, if so, whether it was relied on;
>   - (5) the nature of any such advice, including whether it was merely preparatory to explaining a legal document or was substantive;
>   - (6) whether the putative solicitor asked the putative client to seek independent advice; and
>   - (7) whether any advice was given without qualification (*Law Society of Singapore v Lee Suet Fern* [2020] SGHC 255 at [69]); no single factor is determinative and the assessment is holistic (at [69]).

**P** In *Ahmad Khalis* the solicitor tried to allay the beneficiaries' fears about his client being sole administrator and advised them on the cost and delay of a co-administrator, and a retainer was implied. In *Anwar Patrick Adrian v Ng Chong & Hue LLC* [2014] 3 SLR 761 at [49]–[61], the solicitor's signing of official documents showed he thought he had authority to act. In *Lee Suet Fern* no retainer was found, but the conduct was still misconduct unbefitting an advocate and solicitor. Even without a retainer, a duty of care in tort may be owed to a third party, such as intended beneficiaries of a will (*AEL v Cheoh Yeoh & Associates LLC* [2014] 3 SLR 1231; *Anwar Patrick Adrian*).

**A** Walk the factors. (1) [X] [paid / did not pay] the fees. (2) [X] [gave / did not give] instructions. (3) There [was / was no] past relationship. (4)–(5) [Lawyer] [gave substantive advice on what [X] should do next, which [X] relied on / merely pointed [X] to existing contract terms]. (6) [Lawyer] [did / did not] tell [X] to get independent advice. (7) The advice was [unqualified / qualified]. On balance an intention to retain [ought / ought not] fairly to be imputed, and [Lawyer] [ought / ought not] to have appreciated it. **C** Therefore [X] is [not] a client, and the conflict and confidentiality rules [do / do not] apply to [Lawyer]'s dealings with [X].

*Source note: s 2(1) LPA p15, L1 p5; Uthayasurian, Ahmad Khalis, Anwar, Lee Suet Fern pinpoints SYL p2, L1 p6; Ahmad Khalis test at [64], [66] ([2006] SGHC 143) and the seven Lee Suet Fern factors at [69] read from the judgments on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026); L1 p7 (AEL, Anwar duty to third parties). Lee Suet Fern outcome L3 p24.*

### eth-w-0-2 — Limiting or excluding liability to the client
*Keywords: limitation of liability, exclusion clause, negligence, fraud, reckless disregard, PI insurance minimum, $1 million, $2 million, s 112(5) LPA, s 111 LPA, s 113(4) LPA, PD 7.1.2, Unfair Contract Terms Act, reasonableness, engagement letter, in writing*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] may limit or exclude liability to the client as the engagement terms propose. **C** The clause [breaches / complies with] PD 7.1.2, which allows a law practice to limit but not exclude its liability to clients, and never below the professional indemnity minimum.
**R** A
law practice may limit, but not exclude, its civil liability to clients, and may not limit
it below the minimum cover required by the Legal Profession (Professional Indemnity
Insurance) Rules, currently $1 million per claim for a law firm (PD 7.1.2). Liability for
fraud or reckless disregard of professional obligations cannot be limited at all, and
courts will not enforce an unfair agreement in the lawyer's favour (PD 7.1.2). In an
agreement as to costs for contentious business, a term that the solicitor is not liable
for negligence, or is relieved of professional responsibility, is wholly void (s 112(5)
Legal Profession Act 1966).

> **Rule in full**
> - PD 7.1.2 applies, which states that although it is not acceptable for law practices and advocates and solicitors to attempt to exclude by contract all liability to their clients, the Council has no objection to their seeking to limit liability provided the limit is not below the minimum cover required by the Legal Profession (Professional Indemnity Insurance) Rules.
> - That minimum is, for each and every claim, $1 million for a lawyer practising in a law firm; $1 million for a law corporation with only one director and $2 million for any other law corporation; and $2 million for a limited liability law partnership.
> - The PD states that this is subject to the law, and that
>   - (a) liability for fraud or reckless disregard of professional obligations cannot be limited;
>   - (b) existing legal principles and restraints cannot be overridden, and in particular the courts will not enforce an unfair agreement with the client in the lawyer's favour;
>   - (c) under s 112(5) LPA a provision in an agreement as to costs for contentious business that the lawyer shall not be liable for negligence, or shall be relieved from any responsibility to which the lawyer would otherwise be subject, is void;
>   - (d) under s 2(2) of the Unfair Contract Terms Act, a term excluding liability is of no effect except so far as it satisfies the s 11 requirement of reasonableness, that is, that it is fair and reasonable having regard to the circumstances known to or in the contemplation of the parties when the contract was made;
>   - (e) under s 11(4) of that Act, where a term restricts liability to a specified sum, reasonableness takes into account the resources the lawyer could expect to have to meet the liability and how far the lawyer could cover it by insurance; and
>   - (f) where foreign law may affect the retainer, the applicable law may need to be considered.
> - Finally, any limitation must be brought clearly to the attention of the client and be understood and accepted by the client, and the client's acceptance should be evidenced in or confirmed by writing.
> - Section 112(5) LPA itself states that a provision in an agreement under s 111 (a written, client-signed agreement as to costs for contentious business) that the solicitor
>   - (a) is not liable for negligence; or
>   - (b) is relieved from any responsibility to which the solicitor would otherwise be subject as a solicitor, is wholly void.

**P** Under s 113(4) LPA, if the terms of a s 111 agreement are deemed by the court to be unfair or unreasonable, the agreement may be declared void.

**A** First, the cap of [$X] [is / is not] below the minimum for a [law firm / law corporation with one director / other law corporation / LLP], which is [$1 million / $2 million] per claim. Second, the clause [does / does not] purport to cover fraud or reckless disregard of professional obligations, which PD 7.1.2(a) forbids. Third, if it is in a contentious costs agreement and excludes negligence or professional responsibility altogether, it is wholly void under s 112(5). Fourth, the clause must be reasonable under UCTA, taking into account the firm's PI cover. Fifth, [Lawyer] [did / did not] explain it clearly to the client and get written acceptance. A cap at or above the PI minimum, covering negligence only, explained to the client and accepted in writing, would be acceptable. **C** The clause [is unenforceable as drafted and offering it breaches PD 7.1.2 / complies with PD 7.1.2]; [Lawyer] should redraft it to a lawful cap and obtain the client's written acceptance.

*Source note: PD 7.1.2 p1–2; s 111 LPA p270; s 112(5) LPA p271; s 113(4) LPA p272; PD 7.1.2 listed SYL p2. Tested PYP21 Q3.4.*

### eth-w-0-3 — Honesty with the client
*Keywords: honesty, dishonesty, r 5(2)(a), r 5(1)(a), false pretext, deceive client, striking off, character defect, Udeh Kumar, Chia Choon Yang, Ng Bock Hoh Dixon, Ng Chee Sing*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] breached the duty of honesty to the client by [act]. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 5(2)(a) PCR, which requires a legal practitioner to be honest in all dealings with his or her client.
**R** A legal practitioner must be honest in all
dealings with the client (r 5(1)(a), r 5(2)(a) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 5(2)(a) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must be honest in all the legal practitioner's dealings with his or her client; it is guided by the principle that the lawyer-client relationship imports a duty to be honest in all dealings with the client (r 5(1)(a)). On sanction, misconduct involving dishonesty will almost invariably lead to striking off where the dishonesty reveals a character defect rendering the lawyer unsuitable for the profession, which is so in three categories:
>   - (1) where the dishonesty was integral to a criminal offence of which the lawyer was convicted;
>   - (2) where the lawyer failed to deal appropriately with client money or the firm's accounts, or the dishonesty violated the relationship of trust and confidence inherent in the solicitor-client relationship; and
>   - (3) where the lawyer dealt fraudulently with the court or breached the duty of candour to it (*Law Society of Singapore v Udeh Kumar s/o Sethuraju* [2017] SGHC 141 at [105]–[107]; *Law Society of Singapore v Chia Choon Yang* [2018] SGHC 174 at [39]), and in those categories striking off follows save in the most exceptional circumstances (*Udeh Kumar* at [109]).
> - Outside those categories the court considers
>   - (1) the real nature of the wrong and the interest implicated;
>   - (2) the extent and nature of the deception;
>   - (3) the motivations behind the dishonesty, and whether it shows a fundamental lack of integrity or a misjudgement;
>   - (4) whether the lawyer benefited from it; and
>   - (5) whether it caused actual harm, or had the potential to cause harm that the lawyer ought to have recognised or did recognise (*Chia Choon Yang* at [40]); these factors are not exhaustive and are weighed holistically (at [41]).

**P** Creating a false document in practice is dishonest even if done to protect a position (*Law Society of Singapore v Ng Bock Hoh Dixon* [2011] SGHC 242 at [31]–[35]). Inexperience is no mitigation for dishonesty (*Law Society of Singapore v Ng Chee Sing* [2000] 1 SLR(R) 466). In *Chia Choon Yang* a notary's false attestation that he had witnessed a signature, with no deception of the court and no breach of the client's trust, was a lapse of judgment rather than a character defect and led to 15 months' suspension rather than striking off (at [44], [54]).

**A** Here [Lawyer] [gave a false reason — e.g. "required for insurers" — to get the client to sign / misstated the position], intending the client to act on it, which is not honest dealing under r 5(2)(a). Note that r 6(3)(d) only permits disclosure *to* an insurer; it creates no reporting duty on the client. On sanction, the dishonesty [falls in category (2), because it abused the client's trust / falls outside the categories], and on the factors it [benefited [Lawyer] / caused or risked harm to the client / was a misjudgement]. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 5(2)(a), which is likely grossly improper conduct under s 83(2)(b) LPA, and the likely sanction is [striking off / suspension].

*Source note: r 5(1)–(2) PCR p10; Udeh Kumar 2017, Chia Choon Yang, Ng Bock Hoh Dixon listed SYL p2 (SYL's ranges [25]–[30], [102]–[109] and [44]–[55] are wider than the passages used); three categories Udeh Kumar [105]–[107], [109] and Chia Choon Yang [39], factors [40]–[41], facts and outcome [44], [54], all read from the judgments on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026); Ng Chee Sing L1 p21 (which cites Ng Bock Hoh Dixon as [2012] 1 SLR 348). Tested PYP21 Q1.3.*

### eth-w-0-4 — Duty to inform and advise the client
*Keywords: duty to inform, r 5(2)(b), material information, overriding confidentiality, client agreed in writing, advise on risks, r 17(2)(c), r 17(2)(f), r 10(2), informed decision, settlement proposal, client instructions, Su Ah Tee*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] failed to tell the client [information / risk]. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 5(2)(b) PCR, which requires a legal practitioner, when advising, to tell the client all information known to the lawyer that may reasonably affect the client's interests in the matter.
**R** When advising, a lawyer must tell the client all
information known to the lawyer that may reasonably affect the client's interests in the
matter, unless another duty of confidentiality prevents it or the client agreed in writing
not to be told (r 5(2)(b) PCR). The lawyer must also advise on the legal issues so the
client can make an informed decision (r 17(2)(f) PCR) and tell a client in proceedings of
their duties to the court (r 10(2) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 5(2)(b) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must, when advising the client, inform the client of all information known to the legal practitioner that may reasonably affect the interests of the client in the matter, other than
>   - (i) any information that the legal practitioner is precluded, by any overriding duty of confidentiality, from disclosing to the client; and
>   - (ii) any information that the client has agreed in writing need not be disclosed to the client.
> - Rule 17(2)(c) states that a legal practitioner must explain clearly and properly to the client a proposal of amicable resolution, or any other offer or position taken by any other party, which affects the client, and r 17(2)(f) that the legal practitioner must advise the client on the relevant legal issues in a matter, to enable the client to make an informed decision about how to act.
> - Rule 10(2) states that a legal practitioner whose client is a party to proceedings before a court or tribunal must inform the client of the client's responsibilities to the court and every other party, including the client's duties
>   - (a) to be truthful towards the court or tribunal; and
>   - (b) to comply with every legal requirement concerning the conduct and presentation of the client's case.

**P** A lawyer who fails to explain material risks to the client breaches this duty (*Su Ah Tee v Allister Lim and Thrumurgam* [2014] SGHC 159).

**A** First, [Lawyer] knew [e.g. that disclosure was legally required / there was no evidence for an element of the application / an undertaking had been given affecting the client]. Second, that information may reasonably affect the client's interests because [reason]. Third, neither exception applies: no other client's confidentiality prevented disclosure, and the client never agreed in writing not to be told. The duty is not displaced by fear of displeasing the client; the lawyer must give the advice and, if the client insists on an improper course, decline to act on it. [If the omission was an offer from the other side, r 17(2)(c) is also breached.] **C** [Lawyer] breached r 5(2)(b) [and r 17(2)(c) / (f)], and should now advise the client fully in writing.

*Source note: r 5(2)(b) PCR p10, L1 p20; r 17(2)(c), (f) PCR p31; r 10(2) PCR p20; Su Ah Tee L1 p22 (no pinpoint in sources). Tested PYP21 Q2, PYP22 (c).*

### eth-w-0-5 — Competence, diligence and records
*Keywords: competence, diligence, r 5(2)(c), r 5(2)(d), r 5(2)(k), r 5(3), contemporaneous records, attendance notes, terms of engagement, conflict check, reasonably competent solicitor, vulnerable client, Jayakumar Naidu, Lie Hendri Rusli*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] acted with reasonable competence and diligence [and kept proper records]. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 5(2)(c)–(d) [and (k)] PCR, which require a legal practitioner to act with reasonable diligence and competence and to have and apply the knowledge and skills each matter requires.
**R** A lawyer must act with reasonable diligence and competence, must have the
knowledge and skills each matter requires, and must keep proper contemporaneous records
of instructions received and advice given (r 5(2)(c), (d), (k) PCR). Compliance is judged
in light of the terms of engagement (r 5(3) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 5(2) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must
>   - (c) act with reasonable diligence and competence in the provision of services to the client;
>   - (d) ensure that the legal practitioner has the relevant knowledge, skills and attributes required for each matter undertaken on behalf of the client, and apply them in a manner appropriate to that matter; and
>   - (k) keep proper contemporaneous records of all instructions received from, and all advice rendered to, the client.
> - Rule 5(3) states that the circumstances, nature and terms of engagement of the legal practitioner by the client are to be taken into account when considering whether the legal practitioner has complied with paragraph (2).
> - The test is what a reasonably competent solicitor would have done having regard to normal standards of practice, and the lawyer must do more where there are special risks, the client is vulnerable or unfamiliar with the transaction (*Law Society of Singapore v K Jayakumar Naidu* [2012] 4 SLR 1232 at [85]–[91]).

**P** Gross incompetence or indifference can amount to misconduct, and pressure of practice is no excuse (*Jayakumar Naidu* at [85]–[91]). Without contemporaneous notes, the solicitor may be handicapped when the credibility of his evidence is assessed; records matter most where several parties are involved (*Lie Hendri Rusli v Wong Tan & Molly Lim* [2004] 4 SLR(R) 594 at [36], [42]–[45], [63]–[64]).

**A** First, under r 5(3), what did the engagement cover? Here [the client chose a limited scope, e.g. "no due diligence" / the retainer covered X]. Second, under r 5(2)(c), did [Lawyer] do what a reasonably competent solicitor would within that scope, e.g. [run a proper firm-wide conflict check / act on warning signs]? Third, under r 5(2)(d), did [Lawyer] have the knowledge needed, or [advise on foreign rules saying "I have no idea what they say"]? Fourth, under r 5(2)(k), did [Lawyer] make attendance notes of [the meeting / the advice]? A lawyer who works within the agreed scope and runs a reasonable firm-wide check is unlikely to be in breach. **C** Therefore [Lawyer] [did not breach / breached] r 5(2)(c)–(d), and the [presence / absence] of contemporaneous notes [supports / weakens] [Lawyer]'s position under r 5(2)(k).

*Source note: r 5(2)–(3) PCR p10–11; Jayakumar Naidu pinpoint SYL p2, L1 p23; reasonably-competent-solicitor test and special-risk points Cyril §II.2.3 (secondary); Lie Hendri Rusli [42]–[45] SYL p2 and [36], [63]–[64] L1 p25; handicap-on-credibility point Cyril §II.2.8 (secondary). Tested PYP21 Q1.1, PS2 Q1, PYP22, PYP23 Q2.*

### eth-w-0-6 — Confidentiality
*Keywords: confidentiality, r 6, r 6(2), r 6(3), disclose, exceptions, consent, required by law, court order, insurer, defend complaint, r 21(1), after retainer, conflict check, reason for declining, Ravi Madasamy, Wee Shuo Woon, privileged and confidential, privilege*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] may disclose [information] to [person]. **C** [Lawyer] may not disclose it, because r 6(2) PCR forbids a legal practitioner to knowingly disclose information confidential to the client and acquired in the engagement, and no r 6(3) exception applies.
**R** A lawyer must not knowingly
disclose information confidential to the client and acquired in the course of the
engagement, from the client or anyone else (r 6(2) PCR). Disclosure is allowed only if the
client authorises it, the law or a court requires it, it is made in confidence to get
advice on the lawyer's own obligations or to the lawyer's insurer, or it is needed to
defend a complaint (r 6(3)(a)–(e) PCR). The duty survives the end of the retainer
(r 21(1) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 6(2) PCR applies, which states that, subject to paragraph (3) and any rules made under s 136, 150 or 166 of the Act, a legal practitioner must not knowingly disclose any information which
>   - (a) is confidential to his or her client; and
>   - (b) is acquired by the legal practitioner (whether from the client or from any other person) in the course of the legal practitioner's engagement.
> - There are five exceptions.
> - Rule 6(3) states that the legal practitioner may disclose such information if
>   - (a) the client authorises the disclosure;
>   - (b) the legal practitioner is permitted or required by law, by an order of court, or by a tribunal to make the disclosure;
>   - (c) the legal practitioner discloses the information in confidence, for the sole purpose of obtaining advice in connection with the legal practitioner's legal or ethical obligations;
>   - (d) the legal practitioner discloses the information in confidence to a provider or broker of the legal practitioner's professional indemnity insurance, in connection with any claim or potential claim, or any complaint or potential complaint, against the legal practitioner; or
>   - (e) the legal practitioner discloses the information for the sole purpose of responding to or defending any charge or complaint about his or her conduct brought in court, before a Review Committee, an Inquiry Committee or a Disciplinary Tribunal, before a complaints committee under s 36S(5), or before a foreign professional disciplinary body where the lawyer is authorised to practise.
> - The duties of loyalty and confidentiality continue after the retainer ends (r 21(1)(a)–(b) PCR).

**P** The duty attaches only to information that is confidential in nature; hearing dates that are public are not (*Law Society of Singapore v Ravi s/o Madasamy* [2015] 3 SLR 1187 at [33]). Marking material "privileged and confidential" puts the recipient on notice of its confidential nature (*HT S.R.L. v Wee Shuo Woon* [2016] SGHC 15).

**A** First, the [identity of the other client / fact that a bidder approached the firm / client's minimum price / will's contents] is confidential in nature, not public. Second, it came to [Lawyer] in the course of the engagement [from the client / from a third party]. Third, run the exceptions: [client] has not authorised disclosure (a); no law or order requires it (b); the disclosure is not in confidence to get ethics advice or to the PI insurer (c)–(d); and there is no complaint to defend (e). In a conflict situation this means the firm may have to decline [Y] without giving the reason, and must get [client A]'s consent before telling [client B] that A has approached the firm. **C** [Lawyer] must not disclose [it] without [client]'s consent, and should say only that the firm is unable to act; disclosing it would breach r 6(2).

*Source note: r 6(2)–(3) PCR p12–13, L1 p27; r 21(1) PCR p39; Ravi at [33] SYL p3, L1 p28; Wee Shuo Woon L1 p28 (no pinpoint in sources); consent before disclosure SQ p111. Tested PYP21 Q1.2, PYP23 Q1.2.*

### eth-w-0-37 — Instructions through an agent; authority; warrant to act
*Keywords: agent, third party instructions, authority, r 5(5), confirm instructions, warrant to act, letter of engagement, PD 7.4.3, verify identity, referral, r 39(2)(g), r 7(8), r 7(9), Fong Maun Yee, Tung Hui Mannequin, Udeh Kumar, breach of warranty of authority, wasted costs*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] could act on instructions from [agent] on [client]'s behalf. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 5(5) PCR, which requires a legal practitioner given instructions purportedly on the client's behalf to ensure the person has authority or, absent evidence of authority, to obtain the client's confirmation within a reasonable time.
**R** When given
instructions purportedly on the client's behalf, a lawyer must ensure the person giving them
has authority, or, if there is no evidence of authority, obtain the client's confirmation
within a reasonable time (r 5(5) PCR). Oral instructions should be confirmed in a written
warrant to act, and the identity of the client verified under the anti-money-laundering rules
(PD 7.4.3).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 5(5) PCR applies, which states that when a legal practitioner is given instructions purportedly on behalf of his or her client, the legal practitioner must
>   - (a) ensure that the person giving those instructions has the authority to give those instructions on behalf of the client; or
>   - (b) if there is no evidence of such authority, obtain the client's confirmation of those instructions within a reasonable time after receiving them.
> - PD 7.4.3 Part D para 1 states that the lawyer must first verify the identity of the client or principal client under PD 3.2.1 on anti-money laundering; that after that the lawyer may accept instructions from the client or from an agent of a principal client, but must then ensure that the agent has authority and, absent evidence of it, confirm the instructions with the principal client within a reasonable time (para 1(a)); that it is in the interests of both lawyer and client to obtain written instructions, and oral instructions must be confirmed in a written Warrant to Act, the absence of which is prima facie evidence of no authority if authority is disputed (para 1(a), citing O 64 r 7 of the Rules of Court 2014); that where a third party refers the client, the lawyer must comply with r 39(2), must communicate directly with the client to obtain or confirm instructions, and must not accept instructions from the third party (para 1(a)); and that the lawyer should explain the nature, contents and scope of the Warrant to Act to the client personally, not through staff, should keep attendance notes of that explanation, must not leave blank Warrants to Act with a referrer, and should have the client sign in the lawyer's presence (para 1(b)).
> - Under r 7(8)–(9) PCR and PD 7.4.3 Part C, another lawyer may ask whether the lawyer has authority to act, and must accept a written representation that the lawyer is authorised unless there is good reason to suspect it is false.

**P** Where instructions come from a third party claiming to represent the client, the lawyer should obtain the client's written instructions or, if in doubt, see the client; failing to verify breached the duty of care in *Fong Maun Yee v Yoong Weng Ho Robert* [1997] 1 SLR(R) 751, where a property agent produced forged board resolutions. Acting without authority risks damages for breach of warranty of authority, wasted costs and disciplinary action (*Tung Hui Mannequin Industries v Tenet Insurance Co Ltd* [2005] 3 SLR(R) 184). Leaving all communication with a referred client to a third party is a dereliction of duty (*Law Society of Singapore v Udeh Kumar s/o Sethuraju* [2013] 3 SLR 875).

**A** First, did [agent — a relative / director / property agent / personal assistant] have authority, e.g. [a power of attorney / board resolution]? Here [there was none / it was doubtful]. Second, if there was no evidence of authority, did [Lawyer] confirm with [client] within a reasonable time? Here [Lawyer] never [spoke to / obtained written confirmation from] [client]. [Facts suggesting doubt: the client is elderly, overseas, or the agent stands to gain] made confirmation essential. Third, did [Lawyer] verify [client]'s identity and get a signed Warrant to Act, explained personally? **C** [Lawyer] breached r 5(5) and should confirm instructions directly with [client] in writing before acting further.

*Source note: r 5(5) PCR p11–12; r 7(8)–(9) PCR p14; PD 7.4.3 Part C p1, Part D para 1(a)–(b) p2–3; Fong Maun Yee SYL p2, facts Cyril §II.1.2 (secondary); Tung Hui Mannequin L1 p11–12, SYL p8; Udeh Kumar 2013 L9 p20, facts Cyril §VI.2.2 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-38 — Keeping the client informed; timeliness; following instructions
*Keywords: r 5(2)(e), r 5(2)(f), r 5(2)(g), r 5(2)(h), r 5(2)(i), r 5(2)(j), r 17(2)(b), r 17(2)(c), keep informed, progress, respond promptly, appointments, timely advice, follow instructions, unless order, settlement proposal, mediation, r 26(1), Chiong Chin May Selena, Ooi Oon Tat, Ezekiel Peter Latimer*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] kept [client] informed and acted promptly. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 5(2)[(e) / (h) / (i)] PCR, which requires a legal practitioner to [keep the client reasonably informed of progress / provide timely advice / follow the client's lawful, proper and reasonable instructions].
**R** A lawyer must keep the client reasonably informed of
progress, respond promptly to the client's communications where practicable, keep
appointments, give timely advice, follow all lawful, proper and reasonable instructions, and
use all legal means to advance the client's interests so far as reasonably expected (r 5(2)(e)–
(j) PCR). These duties continue until the retainer ends (r 26(1) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 5(2) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must
>   - (e) keep the client reasonably informed of the progress of the client's matter;
>   - (f) where practicable, promptly respond to the client's communications;
>   - (g) keep appointments with the client;
>   - (h) provide timely advice to the client;
>   - (i) follow all lawful, proper and reasonable instructions that the client is competent to give; and
>   - (j) use all legal means to advance the client's interests, to the extent that the legal practitioner may reasonably be expected to do so.
> - Rule 17(2) states that a legal practitioner
>   - (b) must at all times use his or her best endeavours to complete any work for the client as soon as is reasonably possible; and
>   - (c) must explain clearly and properly to the client a proposal of amicable resolution, or any other offer or position taken by any other party, which affects the client. These duties continue until the retainer ends (r 26(1) PCR).

**P** A lawyer who made up excuses for inaction, failed to tell her client of the other side's proposal to mediate, and delayed filing showed a pattern of "chronic irresponsibility" (*Law Society of Singapore v Chiong Chin May Selena* [2013] SGHC 5). Failing to respond to discovery requests despite having the client's documents was a breach (*Law Society of Singapore v Ooi Oon Tat* [2022] SGHC 185 at [2]–[4]), as was failing to tell clients of an unless order before its deadline (*Law Society of Singapore v Ezekiel Peter Latimer* [2024] SGHC 90 at [36]–[50]).

**A** Take each limb in turn. (e) [Lawyer] [did not tell the client of the hearing date / the unless order]. (f)–(g) [Lawyer] [did not reply for X weeks / missed the meeting]. (h) The advice on [issue] came [too late to act on]. (i) [Lawyer] [did not act on the instruction to file], which was lawful, proper and reasonable. (j) [Lawyer] [did not pursue the available step of X]. Under r 17(2)(c), the [settlement offer / mediation proposal] had to be explained to the client. Pressure of work is no excuse. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 5(2)[(e) / (h) / (i)] [and r 17(2)(c)], and should update the client in writing and take the outstanding step at once.

*Source note: r 5(2) PCR p10–11, L1 p20; r 17(2)(b)–(c) PCR p31; r 26(1) PCR p48; Chiong Chin May Selena SYL p2, L1 p24, facts Cyril §II.2.5 (secondary); Ooi Oon Tat and Ezekiel Peter Latimer [2024] pinpoints SYL p2, facts Cyril §II.2.3–2.6 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-39 — Privilege; requests for information from authorities
*Keywords: privilege, legal professional privilege, PD 9.1.3, PD 9.1.1, request for information, Comptroller of Income Tax, Commercial Affairs Department, CAD, Official Assignee, power exists, power validly exercised, check the power, submissions, frivolous argument, fraud exception, crime, waiver, client's privilege, r 6(3)(b)*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] may [hand over documents to authority X / disclose communications], and what [Lawyer] must check first. **C** [Lawyer] must claim privilege under PD 9.1.3 and, under PD 9.1.1, satisfy himself or herself that [authority]'s power exists and has been validly exercised before disclosing anything.
**R** Communications made to a lawyer in the professional capacity and
in the legitimate course of professional employment are privileged, whether or not litigation
is contemplated (PD 9.1.3). Privilege does not attach to information not confidential in
nature, facts patent to the senses (such as the date of instruction or that a deed was
signed), communications the client asked to be passed on, the record of public proceedings, joint-client
communications as between those clients, or communications in furtherance of a fraud or crime
(PD 9.1.3). The privilege is the client's: until waived, the lawyer must claim it, and it lasts
forever (PD 9.1.3). When an authority demands documents, the lawyer must satisfy himself or
herself that the power exists and has been validly exercised, and, absent contrary
instructions, make submissions if there is more than a frivolous argument that it has not
(PD 9.1.1).

> **Rule in full**
> - PD 9.1.3 applies, which states that all oral or written communications are privileged, whether letters, deeds, bills of costs, entries, statements or any other communications made to the legal practitioner in the normal course of professional employment, including information obtained in collecting evidence on behalf of a client.
> - The privilege applies to communications made directly or indirectly by the client, provided they are made to the lawyer in his or her professional capacity and in the legitimate course of professional employment, even if they do not relate to a cause in progress or in contemplation.
> - Privileged information about conveyancing is in the same class as any other.
> - No privilege attaches to
>   - (a) any information which is not confidential in nature;
>   - (b) facts patent to the senses, for example the date on which the lawyer was first instructed, the fact that the client executed a particular deed, or that the lawyer witnessed it;
>   - (c) communications which the client instructed the lawyer to repeat to a third party, provided the communication to the third party was not intended to be confidential;
>   - (d) the record of public proceedings;
>   - (e) where several parties employ a common lawyer, communications made to the lawyer in that common capacity, as between those parties; and
>   - (f) communications made to a lawyer in furtherance of a fraud or crime, even if the lawyer was unaware of the purpose, except that communications for the purpose of a defence in criminal proceedings remain privileged so long as they are not made in furtherance of a criminal purpose.
> - The privilege is the client's, not the lawyer's, so the client can restrain disclosure or waive it; until the client waives it, the lawyer must claim it if asked to disclose; and it lasts forever.
> - PD 9.1.1 applies, which states that various authorities, such as the Comptroller of Income Tax, the Commercial Affairs Department and the Official Assignee, have called on lawyers to supply particulars or produce documents about matters in which they acted for a client, under their powers in the relevant legislation; and that it is the lawyer's ethical duty, where the particulars or documents sought are protected by legal professional privilege,
>   - (1) to satisfy himself or herself that the power invoked exists;
>   - (2) to satisfy himself or herself that it has been validly exercised; and
>   - (3) in the absence of the client's instructions to the contrary, to make the necessary submissions on the client's behalf if there is anything more than a frivolous argument that these conditions are not met.
> - To check whether the power is real and validly exercised, the lawyer should:
>   - (1) ask the authority to identify, in writing, the statutory provision it relies on, and read that provision to confirm it exists and is in force;
>   - (2) check that the provision reaches a lawyer and the kind of material sought, and whether its terms override or preserve legal professional privilege;
>   - (3) check that the person making the request is the authority, or an officer authorised under that statute, to exercise the power;
>   - (4) check that any conditions the statute sets have been met, such as a written notice, a stated purpose, a time limit or a court order;
>   - (5) check that the request is within the scope of the power, limited to the matter, client and period the statute covers;
>   - (6) tell the client, take instructions and, unless the client instructs otherwise, make submissions to the authority if any of these points is more than frivolously arguable; and
>   - (7) if the power is valid, disclose only what it requires, which r 6(3)(b) PCR permits as disclosure "required by law".
> - The powers PD 9.1.1 has in mind are these.
> - The Comptroller of Income Tax acts under s 65B Income Tax Act 1947, by notice requiring information or attendance with documents (s 65B(3), (3B)), normally within 21 days (s 65B(3A)), and s 65B(2)(b) states expressly that a person is not obliged to disclose any information subject to legal privilege.
> - The Commercial Affairs Department uses s 20 Criminal Procedure Code 2010, a written order by a police officer of or above the rank of sergeant, or a person authorised in writing, requiring production of a document necessary or desirable for an investigation, inquiry, trial or other proceeding under the Code (s 20(1), (9)); the power applies despite any provision in any other law relating to production (s 20(5)) and has no express saving for privilege.
> - The Official Assignee acts under s 334 Insolvency, Restructuring and Dissolution Act 2018, by written notice summoning a person believed able to give information about a bankrupt's affairs to be examined on oath and to produce documents relating to them (s 334(1)–(2)); s 334 is silent on privilege, although ss 369–370 let the Official Assignee take possession of the bankrupt's own documents "including any which would be privileged".

**P** Privilege is also statutory: s 128(1) Evidence Act 1893 states that no advocate or solicitor may at any time, without the client's express consent, disclose any communication made to him or her in the course and for the purpose of professional employment, the contents of any document learned of in that way, or any advice given, but s 128(2) does not protect a communication made in furtherance of any illegal purpose, or any fact observed showing that a crime or fraud has been committed since the employment began. This sits alongside the duty of confidentiality in r 6(2) PCR, which yields to disclosure "permitted or required by law, by an order of court, or by a tribunal" (r 6(3)(b) PCR).

**A** First, are the [documents] privileged? Here they are [communications made to [Lawyer] in the course of advising [client]], and no PD 9.1.3 exception applies [or: the fraud exception applies because [facts]]. Second, run the PD 9.1.1 checks: [authority] relies on [provision]; it [does / does not] exist and reach privileged material; the request was made by [officer], who [is / is not] authorised; [the statutory conditions were / were not] met; and the request [is / is not] confined to [the matter]. Third, [client] [has / has not] waived privilege or instructed [Lawyer] not to resist. **C** [Lawyer] should [claim privilege, take the client's instructions and make submissions that the power has not been validly exercised / comply, disclosing only what the valid power requires, as r 6(3)(b) permits].

*Source note: PD 9.1.3 p1; PD 9.1.1 p1; both listed SYL p3, L1 p29; r 6(2)–(3) PCR p12–13; summary Cyril §II.3.2–3.3 (secondary). PD 9.1.1 names the three authorities but not their statutes, and gives only the two conditions ("exists" and "validly exercised"); checks (1)–(7) are practical steps applying those two conditions to the statutory requirements below, not rules from a source. ITA s 65B(2)–(3B), CPC s 20(1), (5), (9), IRDA ss 334, 369–370 and Evidence Act s 128 read on Singapore Statutes Online (current at 3 Nov 2026); whether CPC s 20(5) or IRDA s 334 displaces privilege is not decided in the course sources, so the safe course is to claim privilege and make submissions as PD 9.1.1 directs.*

## Conflicts of interest

### eth-w-0-7 — Acting for two or more parties: r 20 before accepting
*Keywords: r 20, multiple clients, diversity of interests, relevant party, r 20(3), ostensibly disadvantageous transaction, undue influence, informed consent in writing, independent legal advice, written record, commercial conflict, rival bidders, consent before disclosure, r 6(3)(a), Lie Hendri Rusli, Uthayasurian, Mahidon, Kuek Siang Wei, Ezekiel Peter Latimer, Ahmad Khalis*

**I** The issue is whether [Firm] may act for both [A] and [B] in [matter]. **C** Here, r 20 PCR applies, and [Firm] may act for both only if, before accepting instructions, it completes every step in r 20(3) PCR, including obtaining each party's informed consent in writing.
**R** Where a firm intends to act for two or
more parties to a matter and a diversity of interests exists or may reasonably be expected
to exist (r 20(2) PCR), it must first explain directly to each party how their interests
diverge, that it may be unable to share information or give advice prejudicial to the other,
and that it must cease to act if it cannot advise competently, evenly and consistently;
advise independent legal advice and, if refused, keep a written record or confirmation; and
get each party's informed consent in writing (r 20(3)(a)–(e) PCR). It must stay vigilant for
conflicts throughout (r 20(4) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 20(2) PCR states that paragraphs (3), (4) and (7) apply where
>   - (a) a legal practitioner or law practice intends to act for 2 or more different parties (each called a relevant party) to a matter or transaction; and
>   - (b) a diversity of interests exists, or may reasonably be expected to exist, between those parties.
> - Rule 20(3) states that, before accepting any instructions from any relevant party in relation to the matter or transaction, the lawyer
>   - (a) must communicate directly with each relevant party
>     - (i) to explain to that party
>       - (A) how the interests of all or any of the relevant parties diverge or may diverge,
>       - (B) how the lawyer may be prevented from disclosing to a relevant party information obtained from another relevant party, despite the relevance of the information to the matter or transaction, and
>       - (C) how the lawyer may be prevented from giving to a relevant party any advice that is prejudicial to another relevant party;
>     - (ii) to inform that party that the lawyer must cease to act in the matter or transaction if, in the course of the retainer, the lawyer has difficulty in advising on and dealing with the relevant parties' divergent interests competently, evenly and consistently;
>     - (iii) to receive and deal with any queries which that party may have on the matter or transaction, or on the risks of all or any of the relevant parties being jointly represented by the lawyer; and
>     - (iv) to ascertain precisely the intentions of that party;
>   - (b) in the case of a transaction that is ostensibly or potentially disadvantageous to a particular relevant party, must also communicate directly with that party
>     - (i) to explain that party's position before the transaction, and how that position will or may be altered to that party's detriment by the transaction,
>     - (ii) to verify whether any instructions purportedly given on behalf of that party do in fact reflect that party's intentions, and
>     - (iii) to remove any doubt as to whether that party may have been misled by, or may be acting under the undue influence of, another person;
>   - (c) must advise each relevant party to obtain independent legal advice;
>   - (d) if a particular relevant party does not obtain independent legal advice, must obtain a written confirmation from that party, or maintain a written record, that the party declines to do so; and
>   - (e) must obtain each relevant party's informed consent in writing to the lawyer acting for all relevant parties, despite their divergent interests.
> - Rule 20(4) states that the lawyer
>   - (a) must throughout the course of the retainer
>     - (i) continue to be vigilant of any conflict or potential conflict between the interests of any of the relevant parties, and
>     - (ii) inform each relevant party of any conflict or potential conflict that arises or may arise; and
>   - (b) must cease to act if, in the course of the retainer, it has difficulty in advising on and dealing with the relevant parties' divergent interests competently, evenly and consistently.
> - The guiding principle is that the lawyer owes duties of loyalty and confidentiality to each client and must act prudently to avoid any compromise of the lawyer-client relationship by reason of a conflict between the interests of 2 or more clients (r 20(1)).
> - Rule 6(2) adds that a lawyer must not knowingly disclose information which is confidential to the client and acquired in the course of the engagement, unless (among other exceptions) the client authorises the disclosure (r 6(3)(a)).

**P** The standard of skill and care owed to each of several clients must be at least equal to that owed to a single client, and the lawyer cannot prefer one client's interests to another's (*Lie Hendri Rusli v Wong Tan & Molly Lim* [2004] 4 SLR(R) 594 at [48]–[50]). A lawyer may act for more than one client in a commercial deal, but must identify and disclose actual or potential conflicts, obtain informed consent from all, remain impartial, define the scope of each retainer, keep proper records and document critical advice, and must not act as a mere messenger; a firm that mishandles the conflict risks being disqualified from acting for all its clients (*Law Society of Singapore v Uthayasurian Sidambaram* [2009] 4 SLR(R) 674 at [34]–[53]). Menon CJ set out the steps needed to obtain genuinely informed consent (*Mahidon Nichiar bte Mohd Ali v Dawood Sultan Kamaldin* [2015] SGCA 36 at [63]–[80]; *Kuek Siang Wei v Kuek Siew Chew* [2015] 5 SLR 357). A lawyer acting for several parties, even under an implied retainer, must keep each informed of all material developments, explain the legal effect of the relevant documents, and must not put himself where he has to choose between one client's interests and his loyalty to another (*Law Society of Singapore v Ahmad Khalis bin Abdul Ghani* [2006] 4 SLR(R) 308 at [74]). Preferring one client's interests to another's led to suspension in *Law Society of Singapore v Ezekiel Peter Latimer* [2019] SGHC 92.

**A** On r 20(2)(a), [Firm], through [Lawyer 1] and [Lawyer 2], intends to act for [A] and [B], who are parties to the same [transaction]; because both lawyers are in one law practice, the rule applies even though different lawyers would act. On r 20(2)(b), a diversity of interests exists because [they are rival bidders for the same asset, so each wants to beat the other on price and terms]. On r 20(3)(a), each lawyer must explain to each client [how their interests diverge; that the firm may be unable to pass on the other bidder's information or give advice prejudicial to the other; and that the firm must cease to act if it cannot advise both competently, evenly and consistently], answer their queries on joint representation and ascertain their intentions. Rule 20(3)(b) applies only if the transaction is ostensibly or potentially disadvantageous to one party [e.g. one party guarantees the other's debt]; [it does not apply to arm's-length rival bidders]. Under r 20(3)(c)–(e) the firm must advise each to get independent legal advice, keep a written confirmation or record if either declines, and obtain each one's informed consent in writing. Before [Lawyer 1] can even approach [A] for consent, [Lawyer 2] must first get [B]'s consent under r 6(3)(a) to reveal that [B] is an interested party, since that fact is itself confidential. Distinguish a mere commercial conflict (clients who are competitors but not parties to the same matter) from a PCR conflict. Under r 20(4) the firm must put information barriers in place and stay vigilant throughout (see eth-w-0-10). **C** [Firm] may act for both [A] and [B] if the r 20(3) steps are completed and information barriers are in place; acting without them would breach r 20(3).

*Source note: r 20(1)–(4) PCR p35–37; r 6(2)–(3) PCR p12; Lie Hendri Rusli L2 p9, SYL p3; Uthayasurian L2 p10–11, SQ p111; Mahidon SYL p3 (cited there as [2015] SGCA 36 at [63]–[80]; SQ p111 cites the same case as [2015] 5 SLR 62); Kuek Siang Wei and "Menon CJ's observations" SQ p111; Ahmad Khalis L2 p12, SYL p3; Latimer [2019] SQ p111, SYL p3; Latimer facts Cyril Master Notes §II.4.1.1 (secondary); consent before disclosure SQ p111; commercial vs PCR conflict INTRO2 p26. Model: SQ p110–111 (which labels the independent-advice step "r 20(3)(b)"; in the PCR it is r 20(3)(c)). Tested PYP23 Q1.2, PYP21 Q1.1–1.2.*

### eth-w-0-8 — When multiple representation is unworkable: ceasing to act
*Keywords: r 20(4), r 20(5), r 20(6), cease to act, competently evenly and consistently, r 20(7), informed consent in writing, regulator, veto, performance bond, crossroads, Lie Hendri Rusli*

**I** The issue is whether [Firm] can continue, or begin, to act for [C] alongside [A] and [B]. **C** [Firm] should decline [or must cease] to act for [C], because r 20(4)(b) [r 20(6)(b)] PCR requires a firm acting for several parties to cease to act if it has difficulty in advising on and dealing with their divergent interests competently, evenly and consistently.
**R** Throughout the retainer a
firm acting for several parties must stay vigilant for conflicts, tell each party of them,
and must cease to act if it has difficulty advising and dealing with their divergent
interests competently, evenly and consistently (r 20(4), (6) PCR). It may continue for one
party only if it stops acting for all the others whose interests diverge and they all
give informed written consent (r 20(7) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Where the diversity of interests exists, or may reasonably be expected to exist, when the lawyer intends to act (r 20(2)), r 20(4) PCR applies, which states that the lawyer
>   - (a) must throughout the course of the retainer
>     - (i) continue to be vigilant of any conflict or potential conflict between the interests of any of the relevant parties, and
>     - (ii) inform each relevant party of any conflict or potential conflict that arises or may arise between the interests of any of the relevant parties; and
>   - (b) must cease to act in the matter or transaction if, in the course of the retainer, the lawyer has difficulty in advising on and dealing with the relevant parties' divergent interests competently, evenly and consistently.
> - Where the lawyer already acts for 2 or more different parties to a matter or transaction and a diversity of interests arises between them during the retainer (r 20(5)(a)–(b)), r 20(6) imposes the same two duties for the remainder of the retainer:
>   - (a) to stay vigilant of, and inform each relevant party of, any conflict or potential conflict; and
>   - (b) to cease to act if the lawyer has difficulty in advising on and dealing with the divergent interests competently, evenly and consistently.
> - There is one exception.
> - Rule 20(7) states that, despite paragraphs (4)(b) and (6)(b), the lawyer may continue to act for a relevant party if
>   - (a) the lawyer ceases to act for all other relevant parties whose interests diverge from the interests of that relevant party; and
>   - (b) all of those other relevant parties give their informed consent in writing for the lawyer to continue to act in the matter or transaction.
> - Each party must have been told at the outset that the lawyer must cease to act in this event (r 20(3)(a)(ii)).

**P** A lawyer who, objectively, reaches the crossroads where he has difficulty in advising and dealing with multiple clients competently, evenly and consistently ought to discharge himself from further involvement in the matter; the standard owed to each client is at least that owed to a single client (*Lie Hendri Rusli v Wong Tan & Molly Lim* [2004] 4 SLR(R) 594 at [48]–[50]).

**A** First, a diversity of interests exists, or may reasonably be expected, between [C] and each of [A] and [B], because [C can veto any sale / can insist on a $5m on-demand performance bond / may reject a bidder under its benchmarks]. Second, apply the r 20(4)(b) test. If [C] takes a position prejudicial to [A] or [B], they will need [Firm]'s advice on how to negotiate with or even challenge [C], while [C] will need [Firm]'s advice on how to maintain its position and resist the challenge. One firm cannot advise on and deal with both sides of that dispute competently, evenly and consistently, and information barriers do not cure this. Third, if [Firm] is already acting, r 20(7) lets it keep one client only if it stops acting for every party whose interests diverge from that client's and all of them consent in writing. **C** On balance, [Firm] should not act for [C]; if already acting, it must cease to act under r 20(4)(b) [r 20(6)(b)] for [all clients / all but one, with the others' informed written consent under r 20(7)].

*Source note: r 20(3)(a)(ii) PCR p35–36; r 20(4)–(7) PCR p37–38; Lie Hendri Rusli L2 p9, SYL p3; model reasoning SQ p112–113 (SQ p113 cites the test as "Rule 20(3)(ii)"; in the PCR it is r 20(3)(a)(ii) and r 20(4)(b)). Tested PYP23 Q1.3, PYP21 Q1.2.*

### eth-w-0-9 — Document prepared for parties now in dispute: r 20(8)
*Keywords: r 20(8), drafted document, prepared on behalf of, joint venture agreement, shareholders' agreement, settlement agreement, dispute arising from the document, absolute bar, no consent exception*

**I** The issue is whether [Firm] may act in a dispute about a [document] it prepared. **C** [Firm] [is / is not] barred by r 20(8) PCR, which forbids a firm that prepared a document on behalf of 2 or more parties from representing any of them in a dispute arising from it.
**R** Where a firm has prepared a document on behalf of two or more parties
concerning their rights and obligations, and two or more of them become involved in a
dispute arising from the document, it must not represent any disputing party (r 20(8)
PCR). Unlike r 20(3), this bar cannot be cured by consent.

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 20(8) PCR applies, which states that where a legal practitioner or law practice has prepared a document on behalf of 2 or more parties concerning their rights and obligations, and 2 or more of those parties become involved in a dispute arising from the document, the legal practitioner or law practice must not represent any disputing party in the dispute.
> - Unlike r 20(3), r 20(7) and r 21(3), r 20(8) contains no exception for consent: once its conditions are met, the bar is absolute.
> - It rests on the principle that a lawyer owes duties of loyalty and confidentiality to each client (r 20(1)).

**P** The rule reflects the duty of loyalty owed to each party for whom the document was prepared, and the lawyer cannot prefer one such client to another (*Lie Hendri Rusli v Wong Tan & Molly Lim* [2004] 4 SLR(R) 594 at [48]–[50]).

**A** Take each element in turn. (1) Did [Firm] prepare the document? [It drafted the agreement.] (2) Was it prepared on behalf of 2 or more parties? This is usually the key question. If [Lawyer] acted for [one party only] and the other party was unrepresented or separately advised, r 20(8) does not apply, though r 8(2) and r 11(3) may (see eth-w-0-25, eth-w-0-22). If the other side alleges the firm acted for "all the shareholders", test it against the engagement terms and records; a draft for one client, later changed and signed by others the firm never met, is unlikely to have been prepared "on behalf of" them. (3) Does the document concern their rights and obligations? [It sets out their contributions and exit rights.] (4) Are 2 or more of those parties now in a dispute arising from the document? [They dispute the buy-out clause.] **C** Therefore r 20(8) [bars / does not bar] [Firm] from acting for any disputing party.

*Source note: r 20(1), (8) PCR p35, p38; Lie Hendri Rusli L2 p9. Tested PS1 Q2, PYP23 Q2.1 (as an issue to rule out).*

### eth-w-0-10 — Information barriers (Chinese walls)
*Keywords: information barrier, Chinese wall, adequate safeguards, r 21(4)(a), tainted lawyer, physical separation, instructions and acknowledgements, monitoring, Harsha factors, nature of work, timing of barrier, size of firm, physical location, number and seniority of tainted lawyers, same lawyer, iron-tight, Harsha*

**I** The issue is whether information barriers can adequately protect [client]'s confidential information. **C** [Firm] [may / may not] rely on an information barrier, because here it is [likely / unlikely] to be an adequate safeguard in place to protect [client]'s confidential information under r 21(4)(a) PCR.
**R** Where a firm acts despite holding a client's or former client's confidential
information, it must have adequate safeguards in place to protect it (r 21(4)(a) PCR),
and when acting for parties with diverging interests it must avoid any compromise of
loyalty and confidentiality (r 20(1) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 21(4)(a) PCR (and likewise r 21(5)(a)) allows a lawyer to act against a former client, where the former client's informed written consent under r 21(3) cannot be obtained despite reasonable efforts, only if "there are adequate safeguards in place to protect the former client's confidential information".
> - Where a firm acts at the same time for parties whose interests diverge, r 20(1) requires it to act prudently to avoid any compromise of the duties of loyalty and confidentiality owed to each client, r 20(3)(a)(i)(B) requires it to warn each party that it may be prevented from disclosing to one party information obtained from another, and r 6(2) forbids a lawyer to knowingly disclose information confidential to a client and acquired in the course of the engagement.
> - An information barrier is the safeguard that makes these duties workable.

**P** The adequacy of the steps taken to prevent disclosure depends on factors such as (1) the nature of the work done for the former client; (2) the timing of the creation of the information barrier; (3) the size of the law firm; (4) the physical locations of departments within the firm; and (5) the number and seniority of tainted lawyers (*Harsha Rajkumar Mirpuri v Shanti Shewakram Samtani* [2018] SGHC 155; [2018] 5 SLR 894 at [68]). The list is not closed. Effective barriers between the teams may include (a) physical separation; (b) instructions and reminders to lawyers and staff, who are asked to acknowledge their obligations and the procedures; and (c) internal monitoring of whether the barriers are effective. It may be difficult to set up an adequate barrier where the confidential information is held by the same lawyer who would act. The leading commentary says safeguards "must be iron-tight and properly managed in order to be effective" (Pinsler, Legal Profession (Professional Conduct) Rules 2015: A Commentary (2016) at [21.018]).

**A** Take each factor in turn. (1) Nature of the work: [Lawyer] [negotiated the sale and knows the minimum price], which is core, commercially sensitive information. (2) Timing: the barrier [was set up when the retainer began / would only go up now, after the teams have already mixed]. (3) Size: [Firm] is [a small firm where everyone works together / a large firm with separate departments]. (4) Physical location: the teams [sit on the same floor and share staff and IT / are in separate offices with separate IT access]. (5) Tainted lawyers: [one senior partner who supervises both teams / two junior associates who can be walled off]. The barrier should comprise separate teams, physical and IT separation, written instructions and acknowledgements from every lawyer and staff member, and monitoring, all in place from the start. If the lawyer who holds the information is the lawyer who would act, no barrier can separate the information from the work. **C** Therefore [Firm] [may rely on a barrier, provided it is set up immediately and documented / cannot rely on a barrier and should decline].

*Source note: r 6(2) PCR p12; r 20(1), (3)(a)(i)(B) PCR p35–36; r 21(4)–(5) PCR p39–40; Harsha five factors at [68], cited as [2018] 5 SLR 894, SQ p110 (L2 p21 and SYL p3 cite the same case as [2018] SGHC 155, the neutral citation of the same judgment); barrier measures SQ p112; same-lawyer difficulty SQ p110; Harsha factors also Cyril Master Notes §II.4.2 (secondary; it omits size of firm); "iron-tight" is Pinsler's commentary, optional reading cited SQ p110, not case authority. Tested PYP23 Q1.1–1.2, PYP21 Q1.2, PS1 Q2.*

### eth-w-0-11 — Acting against a former client: r 21
