*Keywords: r 21, former client, current client, confidential information, protected by rule 6, adverse interest, material, r 21(3), informed consent in writing, independent legal advice, r 21(4), adequate safeguards, reasonable efforts to notify, r 21(5), illegal to notify, Harsha factors, same lawyer*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] may act for [current client] given [Lawyer]'s past work for [former client]. **C** [Lawyer] must decline to act [did breach r 21(2) PCR by acting], because r 21(2) PCR requires a lawyer to decline to act against a former client whose material confidential information the lawyer holds, unless an exception in r 21(3)–(5) applies.
**R** A lawyer or firm must decline or withdraw if it holds confidential information of a
former client protected by r 6, the current client's interest is or may reasonably be
adverse to the former client's, and that information may reasonably be material to the
current matter (r 21(2) PCR). It may still act if the former client, adequately advised to
get independent legal advice, gives informed written consent (r 21(3) PCR); or, if that
fails despite reasonable efforts, if adequate safeguards protect the information and the
firm makes reasonable efforts to notify the former client of the safeguards and that it
will act (r 21(4) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 21(2) PCR applies, which states that, subject to paragraphs (3), (4) and (5), a legal practitioner or law practice must decline to represent, or must withdraw from representing, a client (the current client) in a matter if
>   - (a) the legal practitioner or the law practice holds confidential information relating to a former client that is protected by rule 6;
>   - (b) the current client has an interest that is, or may reasonably be expected to be, adverse to an interest of the former client; and
>   - (c) that information may reasonably be expected to be material to the representation of the current client in that matter.
> - Information is protected by r 6(2) if it is confidential to the client and acquired by the lawyer, whether from the client or from any other person, in the course of the engagement.
> - There are three exceptions.
> - Rule 21(3) states that paragraph (2) does not prevent the lawyer from acting, or continuing to act, for the current client, subject to any conditions agreed with the former client, if
>   - (a) the lawyer has adequately advised the former client to obtain independent legal advice; and
>   - (b) the former client gives informed consent in writing to the lawyer acting, or continuing to act, for the current client.
> - Rule 21(4) states that where the requirements in paragraph (3)(a) and (b) are not met despite reasonable efforts by the lawyer to meet them, the lawyer may still act, or continue to act, if
>   - (a) there are adequate safeguards in place to protect the former client's confidential information; and
>   - (b) the lawyer has made reasonable efforts to notify the former client
>     - (i) of those safeguards; and
>     - (ii) that the lawyer will act, or continue to act, for the current client.
> - Rule 21(5) states that where it would be illegal for the lawyer to meet the requirements in paragraphs (3)(a) and (4)(b), the lawyer may act, or continue to act, if
>   - (a) there are adequate safeguards in place to protect the former client's confidential information; and
>   - (b) the lawyer ensures that the former client's confidential information is not accessed, used or disclosed without the former client's consent in writing.
> - The guiding principle is that the duties of loyalty and confidentiality continue after the retainer ends (r 21(1)).

**P** Whether safeguards are adequate depends on factors such as (1) the nature of the work done for the former client; (2) the timing of the creation of the information barrier; (3) the size of the law firm; (4) the physical locations of departments within the firm; and (5) the number and seniority of tainted lawyers (*Harsha Rajkumar Mirpuri v Shanti Shewakram Samtani* [2018] SGHC 155; [2018] 5 SLR 894 at [68]). It may be difficult to set up an adequate barrier to satisfy r 21(4) where the confidential information is held by the same lawyer who would act.

**A** On r 21(2)(a), [Lawyer] knows [the former client's minimum sale price / its negotiating history with other buyers / the terms of the draft], acquired while acting for it, which is confidential and protected by r 6. On r 21(2)(b), [current client]'s interest is adverse because [it is a bidder and the former client is the seller / it is suing the former client's nominee]. On r 21(2)(c), the information is plainly material: [knowing the minimum price lets the current client bid at the floor]. So [Lawyer] must decline unless an exception applies. Under r 21(3), [Lawyer] should first advise [former client] to get independent legal advice and ask for its informed written consent, on any agreed conditions. If that fails despite reasonable efforts, r 21(4) requires adequate safeguards and reasonable efforts to notify [former client] of them and that [Lawyer] will act; this is hard to satisfy where [Lawyer] personally holds the information, and the *Harsha* factors [point against / support] adequacy here (see eth-w-0-10). Rule 21(5) applies only if notifying the former client or advising it to get independent advice would be illegal [which is not suggested on the facts]. **C** [Lawyer] may act only with [former client]'s informed written consent under r 21(3); otherwise [Lawyer] should decline, and acting would breach r 21(2).

*Source note: r 6(2) PCR p12; r 21(1)–(5) PCR p39–40, also L2 p18–20; Harsha factors at [68] SQ p110 (cited there as [2018] 5 SLR 894; L2 p21 and SYL p3 as [2018] SGHC 155); same-lawyer difficulty SQ p110; model SQ p109–110. Tested PYP23 Q1.1, PS1 Q2, PYP21 Q1.2.*

### eth-w-0-12 — Court's power to restrain a firm; former prospective clients
*Keywords: injunction to restrain, disqualify, supervisory jurisdiction, fair-minded reasonably informed member of the public, proper administration of justice, integrity of the judicial process, appearance of justice, long history with former client, Lim Oon Kuin, Harsha, prospective client, Harsha test, letter demanding withdrawal*

**I** The issue is whether the court could restrain [Firm] from acting for [client], as [opponent] demands. **C** There is a [real / low] risk that the court would restrain [Firm], because its supervisory jurisdiction applies where a fair-minded, reasonably informed member of the public would conclude that the proper administration of justice requires the firm to be restrained.
**R** Apart from the PCR, the court has a supervisory jurisdiction to restrain lawyers from
acting where a fair-minded, reasonably informed member of the public would conclude that
the proper administration of justice requires it, to protect the integrity and appearance
of the judicial process (*Lim Oon Kuin v Rajah & Tann Singapore LLP* [2022] SGCA 29).

> **Rule in full**
> - The court has a supervisory jurisdiction to restrain lawyers from acting.
> - It is invoked to restrain legal practitioners from acting for a current client, whose interests are adverse to the interests of a former client, if a fair-minded, reasonably informed member of the public would conclude that the proper administration of justice requires that the legal practitioners be restrained from acting, in the interests of the protection of the integrity of the judicial process and the due administration of justice, including the appearance of justice (*Lim Oon Kuin v Rajah & Tann Singapore LLP* [2022] SGCA 29 at [65]).
> - The test is objective, the jurisdiction is exceptional, and due weight must be given to a litigant's interest in keeping the lawyer of his choice (at [65]). Factors such as a long history between the legal practitioners and the former client are relevant in assessing whether an injunction should be granted, even where what the firm knows from that history may not have the quality of confidence (*Lim Oon Kuin* at [72]–[73]).
> - The court also observed that no information barrier can be useful where the very same lawyer acts for both sides (at [52]).
> - For a former prospective client, who only sought advice and never retained the firm, the firm will be restrained from acting in a matter for another client only if it is shown that
>   - (a) the firm was in possession of information which was confidential to him and to the disclosure of which he had not consented;
>   - (b) the information was or might be relevant to the new matter, in which the interest of the other client was or might be adverse to his own; and
>   - (c) there was a risk that the information would come into the possession of those now acting for the defendant [the other client] (*Harsha Rajkumar Mirpuri v Shanti Shewakram Samtani* [2018] SGHC 155 at [47]).
> - Unlike a former client, the former prospective client bears the burden on each limb, and meeting the test does not automatically mean an injunction: the court may restrain only the use of the information (at [47]–[48]).

**P** On the risk in limb (c), whether the firm's safeguards are adequate turns on the *Harsha* factors: (1) the nature of the work done for the former client; (2) the timing of the creation of the information barrier; (3) the size of the law firm; (4) the physical locations of departments within the firm; and (5) the number and seniority of tainted lawyers (*Harsha* [2018] 5 SLR 894 at [68]). It is difficult to wall off information held by the very lawyer who would act. The professional rule in r 21(2) PCR (see eth-w-0-11) runs alongside the court's jurisdiction.

**A** Here [opponent]'s letter [does / does not] identify a rule; [Firm] should still assess it seriously. On *Lim Oon Kuin*: [former client] is a former client whose interests are adverse to [client]'s because [facts]. A fair-minded, reasonably informed observer would note [the firm's long history with the former client / that [Lawyer] drafted the very document in dispute / that [Lawyer] is a likely witness], and would [doubt / accept] that the firm can act without compromising the integrity, and the appearance, of the proceedings. If [opponent] only ever consulted the firm, apply *Harsha*: (a) [opponent] gave [Lawyer] [his settlement position] in confidence and never consented to its disclosure; (b) it is relevant to this dispute, where [client]'s interest is adverse to his; and (c) the risk it reaches the acting team is [high, since the same small team acts / low, given a barrier set up at once in a large firm with separate offices]. **C** [Firm] should [withdraw / respond giving reasons and continue with safeguards], since a later restraining order would prejudice the client.

*Source note: Lim Oon Kuin test and "long history" factor L2 p22, SYL p3, pinpoints [52], [65], [72]–[73] read from the judgment on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026; at [74] the court held only that the ground was not wholly unsustainable); Harsha three-limb test for former prospective clients L2 p21, SYL p3, pinpoint [47]–[48] read from the judgment on eLitigation; Harsha factors at [68] SQ p110; same-lawyer difficulty SQ p110. Cyril's reading (Master Notes §II.4.2) that the same lawyer acting is no adequate safeguard matches Lim Oon Kuin at [52], a remark made under the breach-of-confidence ground. Tested PYP23 Q2.1, PYP22 (b), PS1 Q2.*

### eth-w-0-13 — Lawyer's or firm's own interest against the client's: r 22
*Keywords: r 22, own interest, personal interest, immediate family member, firm's interest, adverse interest, non-adverse interest, full and frank disclosure, independent legal advice, not under an impression, informed consent in writing, r 22(4), Ho Kon Kim, Then Khek Khoon, Tan Phuay Khiang, Tan Chun Chuen Malcolm, Singham Dennis Mahendran, Udeh Kumar, Govindan Balan Nair*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer/Firm] has a personal interest that conflicts with the duty to serve [client]'s best interests. **C** [Lawyer/Firm] [breached / must comply with] r 22 PCR, which forbids acting where there is, or may reasonably be expected to be, a conflict between the duty to serve the client's best interests and the lawyer's or firm's own interests, unless r 22(3) or (4) is complied with.
**R** A lawyer or firm must not act where there is, or may
reasonably be, a conflict between the duty to serve the client's best interests and its
own interests (r 22(2) PCR). If the lawyer, an immediate family member, or the firm has an
adverse interest in the matter, the lawyer must withdraw unless they make full and frank
disclosure, advise independent legal advice, ensure the client does not think the lawyer
is protecting the client's interests if no advice is taken, and obtain the client's
informed written consent (r 22(3)(a), (4)(a) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 22(2) PCR applies, which states that, except as otherwise permitted by r 22, a legal practitioner or law practice must not act for a client if there is, or may reasonably be expected to be, a conflict between
>   - (a) the duty to serve the best interests of the client; and
>   - (b) the interests of the legal practitioner or law practice.
> - Rule 22(3) states that where a legal practitioner, any immediate family member of the legal practitioner, or the law practice in which the legal practitioner practises has an interest in any matter entrusted to the legal practitioner by a client,
>   - (a) in any case where the interest is adverse to the client's interests, the legal practitioner must withdraw from representing the client unless
>     - (i) the legal practitioner makes a full and frank disclosure of the adverse interest to the client;
>     - (ii) the legal practitioner advises the client to obtain independent legal advice;
>     - (iii) if the client does not obtain independent legal advice, the legal practitioner ensures that the client is not under an impression that the legal practitioner is protecting the client's interests; and
>     - (iv) despite (i) and (ii), the client gives informed consent in writing to the legal practitioner acting, or continuing to act, on the client's behalf; or
>   - (b) in any other case, the legal practitioner must withdraw unless
>     - (i) the legal practitioner makes a full and frank disclosure of the interest to the client; and
>     - (ii) despite (i), the client gives informed consent in writing to the legal practitioner acting, or continuing to act, on the client's behalf.
> - Rule 22(4) applies the same two tests, with the same conditions, where the law practice itself has an interest in a matter entrusted to it by its client. "Immediate family member" means a spouse, child, grandchild, sibling, sibling's child, parent or grandparent (r 2(1)).
> - The guiding principle is that the lawyer must act prudently to avoid any compromise of the lawyer-client relationship by reason of a conflict between the client's interests and the lawyer's own (r 22(1)).

**P** Where the dispute could prima facie be said to have been caused by the way the firm handled the transaction, the lawyer and his partner should have discharged themselves and advised the client to seek independent legal advice (*Ho Kon Kim v Betsy Lim Gek Kim* [2001] SGCA 64 at [61]–[63]). The rule protects the client, so it is the client, not the opponent, who must be the aggrieved party (*Then Khek Khoon v Arjun Permanand Samtani* [2012] 2 SLR 451 at [30]–[36]). A lawyer who had the client sign an unusual statutory declaration authorising payments to parties who referred clients to him put their interests and his own ahead of the client's, and was suspended for 2 years (*Law Society of Singapore v Tan Phuay Khiang* [2007] 3 SLR(R) 477). A lawyer who persuaded a client into an investment scheme and to pay money to a company he owned preferred his own interests (*Law Society of Singapore v Tan Chun Chuen Malcolm* [2020] SGHC 166). Personal and sexual relationships between solicitors and their clients are clearly wrong (*Law Society of Singapore v Singham Dennis Mahendran* [2001] 1 SLR(R) 1). Contrast *Law Society of Singapore v Udeh Kumar s/o Sethuraju* [2013] 3 SLR 875, where a lawyer appointed at a moneylender's request, who applied to court to resolve conflicting claims to the sale proceeds, was acquitted because no conflict of interests was found.

**A** First, identify the interest and whose it is: [the firm's own drafting or advice is in issue / a negligence claim is threatened against a firm lawyer / the lawyer's spouse owns the counterparty / the firm wants to keep a lucrative referrer]. It belongs to [the lawyer / an immediate family member / the law practice], so r 22(3) [r 22(4)] applies. Second, is it adverse? [The firm has an interest in defending its own work and avoiding liability, which may diverge from the client's interest in the best outcome], so r 22(3)(a) applies. Third, run the four conditions: [Lawyer] [did / did not] fully and frankly disclose the interest; [did / did not] advise independent legal advice; [did / did not] make sure the client did not think [Lawyer] was protecting the client's interests; and [did / did not] get informed written consent. If the interest is not adverse, only full and frank disclosure and informed written consent are needed (r 22(3)(b)). A merely commercial wish to keep a client is not a PCR conflict, but it must not drive the advice. **C** [Lawyer/Firm] [breached r 22(2) and r 22(3)(a) by continuing to act / may continue only after full and frank disclosure of the interest, advice to seek independent legal advice, ensuring that the client, if not independently advised, is not under the impression that [Lawyer/Firm] is protecting the client's interests, and the client's informed consent in writing (r 22(3)(a)(i)–(iv), (4)(a)(i)–(iv)), or must withdraw].

*Source note: r 2(1) PCR p5; r 22(1)–(4) PCR p40–42; L3 p3–6; Ho Kon Kim at [61]–[63] and Then Khek Khoon at [30]–[36] SYL p6 (r 11 section; SYL p3 gives Ho Kon Kim at [63]), facts L3 p9–10; Tan Phuay Khiang L3 p8; Tan Chun Chuen Malcolm SYL p4 ([2020] SGHC 166; L3 p12 cites it as [2020] 5 SLR 946); Singham Dennis Mahendran L3 p11; Udeh Kumar L3 p7, SYL p3. Tested PYP22 (b), PYP23 Q2.2, PS1 Q2, PYP21 Q1.2.*

### eth-w-0-14 — Concealing own fault; extracting a waiver from the client
*Keywords: waiver, release, deed, own negligence, conceal, r 22(3)(a), r 5(2)(a), r 5(2)(b), honesty, duty to inform, Govindan Balan Nair, exclusion of liability, limitation of liability, PD 7.1.2, s 112(5) LPA, independent legal advice, s 83(2)(b) LPA*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] acted properly in [obtaining a release from the client / continuing to act after a mistake]. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 22(3)(a) PCR, which requires a lawyer with an interest adverse to the client to withdraw unless the lawyer makes full and frank disclosure, advises the client to obtain independent legal advice, ensures that the client, if not independently advised, is not under the impression that the lawyer is protecting the client's interests, and obtains the client's informed consent in writing, and also breached r 5(2)(a) and (b) PCR.
**R** A lawyer must not act where their own interest conflicts with the client's, and
an adverse personal interest requires full and frank disclosure, advice to get independent legal
advice, ensuring the client does not think the lawyer is protecting the client's interests if no
advice is taken, and informed written consent (r 22(2)–(3)(a) PCR). The lawyer must be honest with
the client and tell them everything that may reasonably affect their interests (r 5(2)(a)–(b)
PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 22(2) PCR states that a legal practitioner must not act for a client if there is, or may reasonably be expected to be, a conflict between the duty to serve the best interests of the client and the interests of the legal practitioner or law practice.
> - Rule 22(3)(a) states that where the legal practitioner, an immediate family member or the law practice has an interest in the matter that is adverse to the client's interests, the legal practitioner must withdraw from representing the client unless
>   - (i) the legal practitioner makes a full and frank disclosure of the adverse interest to the client;
>   - (ii) the legal practitioner advises the client to obtain independent legal advice;
>   - (iii) if the client does not obtain independent legal advice, the legal practitioner ensures that the client is not under an impression that the legal practitioner is protecting the client's interests; and
>   - (iv) the client gives informed consent in writing to the legal practitioner acting, or continuing to act.
> - Rule 5(2) states that a legal practitioner must
>   - (a) be honest in all the legal practitioner's dealings with his or her client; and
>   - (b) when advising the client, inform the client of all information known to the legal practitioner that may reasonably affect the interests of the client in the matter, other than
>     - (i) any information that the legal practitioner is precluded, by any overriding duty of confidentiality, from disclosing to the client; and
>     - (ii) any information that the client has agreed in writing need not be disclosed to the client. As to the release itself, s 112(5) of the Legal Profession Act 1966 states that a provision in an agreement as to costs for contentious business under s 111 that the solicitor
>   - (a) is not liable for negligence; or
>   - (b) is relieved from any responsibility to which the solicitor would otherwise be subject as a solicitor, is wholly void.
> - PD 7.1.2 states that it is not acceptable for law practices and lawyers to attempt to exclude by contract all liability to their clients; they may limit liability, but not below the minimum cover required by the Legal Profession (Professional Indemnity Insurance) Rules; liability for fraud or reckless disregard of professional obligations cannot be limited; and the courts will not enforce an unfair agreement with a client in the lawyer's favour.

**P** A lawyer whose negligence led to a default judgment, who did not come clean to the client and carried on acting in trying to set the judgment aside, preferred his own interests to the client's: his negligence gave him an interest in keeping it from the client, because telling the client would expose him to potential liability, disciplinary action or the loss of the client (*Law Society of Singapore v Govindan Balan Nair* [2020] SGHC 174 at [15], [20]); no harm to the client is needed, as r 22 covers potential adverse interests, though truly minor errors generally will not create a conflict (at [22]–[23]). "Adverse" in its natural meaning reflects any situation that would give rise to any disadvantage to the client, and in r 22(3)(a) is read with r 22(2) to include any reason that would detract from the lawyer's duty to serve the client's best interests (*Govindan Balan Nair* at [16]–[19]).

**A** On r 22(3)(a), a release of claims against the firm benefits only the lawyer, so [Lawyer] had an interest adverse to the client's. Here [Lawyer] [drafted the deed without disclosing why / gave a false reason / had it signed through an assistant without explanation / concealed the error], so there was no full and frank disclosure (i); [Lawyer] did not advise independent advice (ii); the client [believed [Lawyer] was still protecting her] (iii); and any written consent was not informed (iv). On r 5(2)(a), [the false reason] was dishonest. On r 5(2)(b), [the mistake and the client's resulting claim against the firm] plainly may reasonably affect the client's interests, and neither exception applies, as no duty of confidentiality bars telling the client and she never agreed in writing not to be told. If the release sits in a costs agreement for contentious business, it is wholly void under s 112(5); in any case PD 7.1.2 means a court will not enforce a total exclusion of liability, or an unfair agreement, in the lawyer's favour. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 22(3)(a) and r 5(2)(a)–(b); the deed is unlikely to be relied on, and the deceit may amount to fraudulent or grossly improper conduct in the discharge of professional duty under s 83(2)(b) LPA.

*Source note: r 5(2) PCR p10–11; r 22(2)–(3) PCR p40–41; Govindan Balan Nair SYL p3 ([2020] SGHC 174), facts L3 p13 (cited there as [2020] 5 SLR 988); breadth of "adverse interest" and pinpoints [15]–[23] read from the judgment on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026; the [2020] 5 SLR 988 report cite is L3's and was not confirmed online); s 112(5) LPA p271; PD 7.1.2 p1; s 83(2)(b) LPA p217–218. Tested PYP21 Q1.3.*

### eth-w-0-40 — Specific conflict situations in the practice directions
*Keywords: PD 7.2.1, public authority, member of a public authority, exceptional circumstances, PD 7.2.2, provisional liquidator, applicant creditor, PD 7.2.3, complainant and accused, police report, PD 7.2.4, debenture holder, receiver, real not fanciful, PD 7.2.5, mortgagor and mortgagee, same transaction, PD 7.2.6, different transaction, current account, mischief*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] may act for [X] given the earlier or concurrent role for [Y]. **C** Under PD [7.2.x], [Lawyer] [should not / may] act for [X].
**R** The Law Society's practice directions settle
several recurring situations. A lawyer who is a member of a public authority, and that
lawyer's partners and assistants, should not act against the authority in proceedings or
matters in which it is directly interested (PD 7.2.1). A firm acting for applicant creditors
should not also act for the provisional liquidator (PD 7.2.2). A lawyer retained by the
complainant in a criminal case cannot later defend the accused in the same case, even if they
only obtained a copy of the police report (PD 7.2.3). Acting for both a debenture holder and
the receiver it appoints is not objectionable in principle, unless a real (not fanciful)
likelihood of conflict exists (PD 7.2.4). A lawyer who acted for both mortgagor and mortgagee
should not act for either in litigation about the same transaction, whether or not the loan is
disbursed or title issued (PD 7.2.5); but may act for the lender in recovering a separate
current-account debt (PD 7.2.6).

> **Rule in full**
> - PD 7.2.1 states that, as a general rule, a solicitor who is a member of a public authority, or any partner of or assistant employed by such solicitor, should not be professionally engaged against such authority in any proceedings to which the authority is a party or in any matter in which the authority is directly interested; if exceptional circumstances justify any departure from this general rule, it is the duty of the solicitor to ensure that the interests of the authority are effectively protected. (Where a lawyer is retained by the Attorney-General in civil proceedings by or against the Government or a public officer, the PD draws attention to s 24(3) of the Government Proceedings Act.)
> - PD 7.2.2 states that when a firm is acting for the applicant creditors and the court appoints a provisional liquidator for the company pending the outcome of the winding-up application, it is undesirable for the lawyers for the applicant creditors to act also on behalf of the provisional liquidator.
> - PD 7.2.3 states that when a lawyer has been retained by the complainant to act for him or her in a criminal case, the lawyer cannot subsequently represent the accused person in his or her defence in the same case, notwithstanding that the lawyer only obtained a certified true copy of the police report and did nothing further for the complainant.
> - PD 7.2.4 states that it is not objectionable in principle for a receiver to use the same lawyer that acts for the appointer, the debenture holder; the receiver, almost invariably a public accountant, should be able to identify a situation likely to give rise to a conflict between the company and the appointer, and if there is a likelihood of conflict the receiver should use different lawyers; the debenture holder's lawyers, if they identify a conflict or likelihood of conflict, can be expected to tell the receiver and advise him or her to engage different lawyers; and the likelihood of conflict should be real and not fanciful, so "potential" conflict, which includes a remote possibility, is not the true test.
> - PD 7.2.5 states that a lawyer who has previously acted for both the mortgagor and the mortgagee should refrain from acting for either of them in litigation pertaining to the same transaction, irrespective of whether
>   - (a) the loan has been fully disbursed;
>   - (b) separate title has been issued for the property; and
>   - (c) the transfer in favour of the purchaser/mortgagor has been perfected.
> - PD 7.2.6 qualifies this: the Council ruled it entirely proper for a lawyer who had acted for the borrowers and the bank in securing banking facilities by a mortgage to act for the bank in recovering the outstanding balance of the borrower's current account, after the property had long been sold and the mortgage discharged, because that claim was not litigation pertaining to the mortgage transaction; there is no general rule that a lawyer who has acted for a person, before or after litigation began, can never act for the opposite party, and in each case the court has to be satisfied that mischief would result.

**P** These directions apply the general rules on multiple and former clients: a lawyer owes duties of loyalty and confidentiality to each client (r 20(1) PCR), those duties continue after the retainer ends (r 21(1)), and r 21(2) bars acting against a former client whose material confidential information the lawyer holds, unless r 21(3)–(5) applies (see eth-w-0-11).

**A** Identify which direction fits. Here [Lawyer] [sits on the public authority / acted for the applicant creditors / was retained by the complainant, even if only to obtain the police report / acts for the debenture holder / acted for both mortgagor and mortgagee in the loan now in default]. The new matter [is against the authority, in which it is directly interested / is for the provisional liquidator / is the defence of the accused in the same case / is for the receiver, with a real (not fanciful) likelihood of conflict because [facts] / is litigation pertaining to the same mortgage transaction / concerns a separate current-account debt]. **C** Under PD [7.2.x], [Lawyer] [must decline / may act, watching for any real conflict and any confidential information protected by r 21].

*Source note: PD 7.2.1–7.2.6 (each p1); r 20(1) and r 21 PCR p35, p39–40; table Cyril Master Notes §II.4.1.3 (secondary). L2 p15–16 summarises PD 7.2.5 as a general bar on acting against a former client in substantially related matters without consent, PD 7.2.6 as a bar on acting for both mortgagor and mortgagee, and PD 7.2.1 as a call for caution; the PD texts themselves (followed here) say otherwise in each case.*

### eth-w-0-41 — Borrowing from clients; welfare assistance
*Keywords: prohibited borrowing transaction, r 23, associated party, immediate family member, excepted person, client includes investor, independent advice, certified in writing, full disclosure, trustee transaction, Council determination, loan from client, welfare assistance, GN 7.4.2, foreign workers, creditor/debtor relationship, maintenance, champerty, s 107(3) LPA, Yap Bock Heng, Thirumurthy, Chia Chwee Imm Helen*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer]'s [loan from / loan to] [client] is permitted. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 23(1)(a) [(b) / (c)] PCR, which forbids a lawyer or law practice to enter into, or arrange for or knowingly allow an associated party to enter into, a prohibited borrowing transaction, unless an exception in r 23(2) or (3) applies [or: the loan to the client is improper under GN 7.4.2].
**R** A lawyer or law practice must not enter into a prohibited
borrowing transaction, which is any transaction by which money or valuable security is
borrowed, directly or indirectly, from a client by the lawyer, the practice or an associated
party; nor arrange for or knowingly allow an associated party (an immediate family member, or
a company in which they have an interest) to do so (r 23(1), (4) PCR). It is permitted only if
every other party received independent advice and the lawyer made full written disclosure of
their interest, or the lawyer did not act in the transaction and the client was separately
represented, or the Council so determines (r 23(2)–(3) PCR). Lending money to clients is also
discouraged: it creates a creditor–debtor conflict and, in pending litigation, risks
allegations of maintenance and champerty; clients needing welfare help should be referred to
appropriate organisations (GN 7.4.2).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 23(1) PCR applies, which states that, subject to paragraph (2), a legal practitioner or law practice must not
>   - (a) enter into a prohibited borrowing transaction;
>   - (b) instruct, procure, provide security for or arrange for an associated party to enter into a prohibited borrowing transaction; or
>   - (c) knowingly allow an associated party to enter into a prohibited borrowing transaction which the legal practitioner or law practice has the power to prevent.
> - Rule 23(4) defines a "prohibited borrowing transaction" as any transaction under or by virtue of which money or valuable security is borrowed (whether directly or indirectly, and whether with or without security) from a client (not being an excepted person) of a legal practitioner or law practice
>   - (a) by the legal practitioner or law practice; or
>   - (b) by an associated party of the legal practitioner or law practice.
> - An "excepted person" means any licensed or authorised bank, finance company or other similar entity the business of which includes lending money to members of the public. "Client" includes, in addition to a client as defined in s 2(1) LPA,
>   - (a) any person who has an existing lawyer-client relationship with the legal practitioner or law practice;
>   - (b) any person seeking to invest money through it; and
>   - (c) any person approached by or on its behalf to invest money through it.
> - An "associated party" of a legal practitioner means
>   - (i) any individual who is an immediate family member of the legal practitioner, or of the sole proprietor or any partner, director, consultant or employee of a law practice in which the legal practitioner practises; or
>   - (ii) any body corporate, partnership, syndicate, joint venture or trust in which the legal practitioner, or any such immediate family member, has any beneficial interest, whether vested or contingent; for a law practice it also includes its sole proprietor, partners, directors, consultants and employees themselves. "Immediate family member" means a spouse, child, grandchild, sibling, sibling's child, parent or grandparent (r 2(1)).
> - There are three exceptions.
> - Rule 23(2) states that r 23(1)(a) does not apply if
>   - (a) both
>     - (i) every party to the transaction (other than the legal practitioner or law practice) has received independent advice before the transaction was entered into, and
>     - (ii) the legal practitioner or law practice has made full disclosure of each of its interests in the transaction;
>   - (b) both
>     - (i) the legal practitioner or law practice does not act for any client in the transaction, and
>     - (ii) any client that is a party to the transaction is represented in it by a legal practitioner from a different law practice or by a different law practice; or
>   - (c) the Council determines (whether before or after the transaction was entered into) that r 23(1)(a) does not apply to that particular transaction.
> - Rule 23(3) gives the same three exceptions to r 23(1)(b) and (c), with independent advice needed by every party other than the legal practitioner, law practice or associated party, and full disclosure of the interests of both the legal practitioner or law practice and the associated party. "Independent advice" means advice given in relation to the transaction by a legal practitioner or law practice that
>   - (i) is not a party to the transaction and
>   - (ii) does not represent any associated party in it, and which that legal practitioner or law practice has certified in writing that it has given; "full disclosure" means such disclosure in writing as would be necessary for a trustee transaction (r 23(4)).
> - For loans the other way, GN 7.4.2 para 2 states that lending money to clients will put a lawyer in a position of personal conflict of interest, as the lawyer will have a creditor/debtor relationship with the client and the debt would be repaid only if the client's case was settled or paid, and that if the matter is pending litigation, allegations of maintenance and champerty could be made against the practice; para 3 states that law practices should direct clients who may require welfare assistance to appropriate organisations that can provide it.
> - A solicitor is subject to the law of maintenance and champerty like any other person (s 107(3) LPA).

**P** The rationale is that a client is vulnerable to a solicitor, who enjoys a position of influence, and may find it difficult to refuse a loan because of the trust and confidence reposed in the solicitor; the lawyer was suspended for 2 years (*Law Society of Singapore v Yap Bock Heng Christopher* [2014] 4 SLR 877). That the client suffered no loss is irrelevant to liability and is not mitigating, while non-repayment is aggravating (*Yap Bock Heng*). Borrowing from a client who received no independent advice was of sufficient gravity for disciplinary action even though the client carried on business as a moneylender, and substantial repayment did not matter (*Law Society of Singapore v Thirumurthy Ayernaar Pambayan* [2015] SGDT 2). Rule 23 applied to a lawyer without a practising certificate who borrowed from a client, and from the client's mother, while in a position of influence (*Law Society of Singapore v Chia Chwee Imm Helen* [2022] SGHC 214).

**A** First, is the lender a "client"? [The lender is a current client / a person approached to invest through the firm], and is not an excepted person [it is an individual, not a bank or finance company]. Second, who borrowed? [Lawyer] borrowed directly, so r 23(1)(a) applies [or: [Lawyer's spouse / a company in which Lawyer's spouse has a beneficial interest] is an associated party, and [Lawyer] [arranged the loan / knew of it and could have stopped it], so r 23(1)(b) [(c)] applies]. Third, the exceptions: [the client received no independent advice certified in writing and there was no written full disclosure], [Lawyer] [acted for the client in the transaction / the client was not separately represented], and there is no Council determination. [For a loan to a client: [Firm] is lending [foreign worker] clients living expenses to be repaid out of their claims, creating exactly the creditor/debtor conflict and champerty risk GN 7.4.2 warns against.] **C** This breaches r 23(1)[(a)]; the loan should be repaid and the client advised to take independent advice [/ the firm should stop lending and refer the clients to welfare organisations under GN 7.4.2 para 3].

*Source note: r 2(1) PCR p5; r 23(1)–(4) PCR p42–46 (definitions p44–46); s 107(3) LPA p266; GN 7.4.2 paras 2–3 p1; Yap Bock Heng rationale and suspension L3 p16, "no loss" and non-repayment points Cyril Master Notes §II.5 (secondary); Thirumurthy L3 p17, repayment point Cyril §II.5 (secondary); Chia Chwee Imm Helen L3 p18, SYL p4, borrowing from the mother Cyril §II.5 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-42 — Buying from a client
*Keywords: purchase from client, r 24, prevailing market price, reasonable price, independent valuation, where practicable, law on fiduciary relationships, undue influence, r 22(3)(a), adverse interest*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] may buy [asset] from [client]. **C** [Lawyer]'s purchase [complies with / breaches] r 24 PCR, which allows a lawyer to buy goods and assets from a client only at the prevailing market price or a reasonable price, with an independent valuation where practicable.
**R** Subject to the law on fiduciary
relationships, a lawyer or law practice may buy goods and assets from a client only at the
prevailing market price or a reasonable price, and must where practicable obtain an
independent valuation (r 24(1)–(2) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 24(1) PCR applies, which states that, subject to the law on fiduciary relationships, a legal practitioner or law practice may purchase goods and assets from a client only if the purchase is at the prevailing market price or at such price as is reasonable.
> - Rule 24(2) states that, where practicable, the legal practitioner or law practice must obtain an independent valuation of the goods and assets. Because a lawyer buying from a client has an interest adverse to the client as seller, r 22(3)(a) also applies, which states that the lawyer must withdraw from representing the client in the matter unless
>   - (i) the lawyer makes a full and frank disclosure of the adverse interest to the client;
>   - (ii) the lawyer advises the client to obtain independent legal advice;
>   - (iii) if the client does not obtain independent legal advice, the lawyer ensures that the client is not under an impression that the lawyer is protecting the client's interests; and
>   - (iv) the client gives informed consent in writing to the lawyer acting, or continuing to act.

**P** Rule 24 is expressly subject to the general law on fiduciary relationships, and the conflict rules in r 22 continue to apply, so a fair price alone does not cure a failure to disclose or to advise independent advice.

**A** On r 24(1), [Lawyer] agreed to buy [the client's car / flat / shares] for [$X], [below / at] the market value of [$Y], so the price [is / is not] the prevailing market price or a reasonable price. On r 24(2), an independent valuation [was / was not] obtained, and [it was practicable, since a valuer could easily be found / it was impracticable because [facts]]. On r 22(3)(a), [Lawyer] [was / was not] acting for the client in the sale, [did / did not] fully and frankly disclose the adverse interest, [did / did not] advise independent legal advice, [did / did not] ensure that the client, if not independently advised, was not under the impression that [Lawyer] was protecting the client's interests, and [did / did not] get informed written consent (r 22(3)(a)(i)–(iv)). **C** [The purchase complies with r 24 / [Lawyer] breached r 24(1) [and (2)] and r 22(3)(a)]; [Lawyer] should obtain an independent valuation and insist the client is separately advised.

*Source note: r 24 PCR p46–47, L3 p19; r 22(3)(a) PCR p41; Cyril Master Notes §II.6 (secondary). No case on r 24 in the sources.*

### eth-w-0-43 — Gifts from a client: r 25
*Keywords: gift, will, inter vivos, significant gift, r 25, immediate family member, partner director consultant or employee, law practice, must not act, independent legal advice, legacy, bequest, Wan Hui Hong James, Manjit Singh, Lee Suet Fern*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] could act on [client]'s intended gift to [Lawyer / family member / firm]. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 25(2) PCR, which requires a lawyer whose client intends to make a significant gift to the lawyer, the firm, its people or their immediate family not to act for the client in relation to the gift and to advise the client to obtain independent legal advice about it.
**R** Where a client intends to make a
significant gift, by will, while alive or otherwise, to the lawyer, any partner, director,
consultant or employee of the practice, any immediate family member of any of them, or the
practice itself, the lawyer must not act for the client in relation to the gift and must
advise the client to obtain independent legal advice about it (r 25(1)–(2) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 25(1) PCR applies
>   - (a) where a client of a legal practitioner intends to make a significant gift, whether by will or while the client is alive, or in any other manner, to
>     - (i) the legal practitioner;
>     - (ii) the sole proprietor, or any partner, director, consultant or employee, of the law practice in which the legal practitioner practises;
>     - (iii) any immediate family member of the legal practitioner or of any of those persons; or
>     - (iv) the law practice in which the legal practitioner practises; or
>   - (b) where a client of a law practice intends to make such a gift to the law practice, its sole proprietor, partners, directors, consultants or employees, or any immediate family member of any of them.
> - Rule 25(2) states that the legal practitioner or law practice
>   - (a) must not act for the client in relation to the gift; and
>   - (b) must advise the client to obtain independent legal advice in relation to the gift. "Immediate family member" means a spouse, child, grandchild, sibling, sibling's child, parent or grandparent (r 2(1)).

**P** A gift is "significant" not only in absolute terms but also relative to the client's means and the reasonable expectations of other prospective beneficiaries, and the more significant the gift, the greater the need for independent advice (*Wan Hui Hong James* at [10]–[11]). Merely telling the client to seek independent advice is not enough (*Law Society of Singapore v Wan Hui Hong James* [2013] 3 SLR 221 at [15]). The lawyer should (1) explain why independent advice is needed, so the gift is a free exercise of the client's will, and that influence may exist even if the client does not perceive it; (2) ensure the adviser is another advocate and solicitor; (3) ask the client to nominate the adviser, and ensure the adviser has no connection compromising independence; (4) advise the client to obtain written confirmation from the adviser that a full explanation was given; (5) where the transaction is complex, give the adviser the information needed without influencing the advice; (6) not suggest the advice is a formality, though the decision is ultimately the client's; and (7) if the client declines, insist with some force, and absent such advice decline to accept the gift (at [18](a)–(g)). Separately, a lawyer acting in respect of the gift must refuse to act and advise independent advice (at [19]–[22]). A $1.8m payment claimed as a "gift" with no independent advice was found to have been made for safekeeping and future fees (*Law Society of Singapore v Manjit Singh s/o Kirpal Singh* [2015] 3 SLR 829). Even without a solicitor-client relationship, a lawyer who made representations on a critically important legal document when in no position to do so, knowing she would be believed and relied on and that her husband was a significant beneficiary, let the will be executed knowing the testator's own lawyer was excluded, and did not clarify the testator's queries, showed patent divided loyalties and was guilty of misconduct (*Law Society of Singapore v Lee Suet Fern* [2020] SGHC 255).

**A** On r 25(1), [client], [age, circumstances], intends to leave [$X / a share] to [Lawyer's daughter], who is an immediate family member of the lawyer. The gift is significant: it is [a third of the estate], large relative to [client]'s means, and it displaces [other beneficiaries] who would reasonably expect to inherit. On r 25(2)(a), [Lawyer] [drafted the will / arranged its execution]; drafting the gift instrument is acting in relation to the gift. On r 25(2)(b), [Lawyer] [did not advise independent advice / told the client it was unnecessary]. [Lawyer]'s family interest is also adverse to the client, so r 22(3)(a) applies. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 25(2)(a)–(b), and also r 22(3)(a); [Lawyer] should stop and refer the client to an independent firm.

*Source note: r 2(1) PCR p5; r 25 PCR p47, L3 p20; Wan Hui Hong James L3 p21–22, pinpoints [10]–[11], [15], [18]–[22] read from the judgment ([2013] SGHC 85) on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026); Manjit Singh L3 p23, facts Cyril Master Notes §II.7 (secondary); Lee Suet Fern SYL p4 ([2020] SGHC 255), L3 p24–25 (cited there as [2020] 5 SLR 1151).*

---

## End of the retainer

### eth-w-0-15 — Withdrawal; termination clauses
*Keywords: withdraw, terminate, r 26, r 26(2), r 26(4), r 26(5), r 26(6), complete the work, reasonable notice, must withdraw, may withdraw, health, non-payment, misrepresentation, loss of confidence, good cause, foreseeable harm, reservation of rights, PD 7.4.2, PD 7.4.3 Part B, cease to act, client discharges, Chew Kim Kee, Alfons Tanumihardja, Chiong Chin May Selena*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] may [withdraw / rely on a clause allowing termination at any time]. **C** [Lawyer] [may / may not] withdraw, because r 26(5) PCR permits withdrawal only on the grounds it lists and r 26(6) requires reasonable care to avoid foreseeable harm to the client.
**R** A lawyer must complete the work agreed (r 26(2) PCR) and must
withdraw if discharged by the client or if embarrassed by improperly obtained documents
(r 26(4) PCR). A lawyer may withdraw on reasonable notice, on the client's informed consent
where no significant harm results, for non-payment, client misrepresentation, a serious
loss of confidence, to avoid breaching the Act or the Rules, or other good cause
(r 26(5)(a)–(h) PCR). On withdrawal the lawyer must take reasonable care to avoid
foreseeable harm, including reasonable notice, time to instruct new counsel and
cooperation with them (r 26(6) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 26(2) PCR applies, which states that, except as otherwise provided in r 26, a legal practitioner must complete the work which he or she agreed to undertake pursuant to the client's instructions.
> - Rule 26(4) states that a legal practitioner must withdraw if
>   - (a) the legal practitioner is discharged from the engagement by the client; or
>   - (b) having come into possession of a document belonging to another party by any means other than the normal and proper channels, and having read it, the legal practitioner would be embarrassed in the discharge of his or her duties by the knowledge of its contents.
> - Rule 26(5) states that a legal practitioner may withdraw from representing a client in a case or matter if
>   - (a) the legal practitioner gives reasonable notice of the withdrawal to the client;
>   - (b) the withdrawal will not cause any significant harm to the client's interests, the client is fully informed of its consequences, and the client voluntarily assents to it;
>   - (c) the legal practitioner reasonably believes that continuing is likely to have a serious adverse effect on his or her health;
>   - (d) the client
>     - (i) fails to pay the legal practitioner's invoice within the stipulated period, or
>     - (ii) otherwise breaches an agreement with the legal practitioner regarding
>       - (A) fees or expenses to be paid by the client or
>       - (B) the client's conduct;
>   - (e) the client makes material misrepresentations about the facts of the case or matter to the legal practitioner;
>   - (f) the withdrawal is necessary to avoid a contravention by the legal practitioner of
>     - (i) the Act,
>     - (ii) the Rules or other subsidiary legislation under the Act,
>     - (iii) practice directions, guidance notes and rulings issued under s 71(6) of the Act,
>     - (iv) practice directions, guidance notes and rulings on professional practice, etiquette, conduct and discipline issued by the Council or the Society, or
>     - (v) for a lawyer also authorised abroad, those issued by the foreign authority or professional body;
>   - (g) there is a serious loss of confidence between the legal practitioner and the client; or
>   - (h) any other good cause exists.
> - Rule 26(6) states that where a legal practitioner withdraws, he or she must
>   - (a) take reasonable care to avoid foreseeable harm to the client, including, where the circumstances permit,
>     - (i) by giving reasonable notice of the withdrawal to the client,
>     - (ii) by giving the client a reasonable amount of time to engage another legal practitioner to take over the case or matter, and
>     - (iii) by cooperating with the client's new legal practitioner; and
>   - (b) abide by the client's decision on whether to appoint another legal practitioner, and who to appoint, to take over the matter if not completed.
> - PD 7.4.2 (repeated in PD 7.4.3 Part B) states that difficulty in terminating a retainer may be averted by a reservation of right in the Warrant to Act or Letter of Engagement, to the effect that the lawyer may at any time discharge himself or herself on the grounds in r 26(5) while observing the r 26(6) obligation to take reasonable care to avoid foreseeable harm; and that without such a reservation, a lawyer who obtains a discharge may be exposed to a claim for damages if the withdrawal leads to the dismissal of the client's claim, or to judgment against the client when there is a valid defence.

**P** The duties of honesty, competence and diligence continue until the retainer ends (r 26(1) PCR). Whether a client has revoked a lawyer's instructions turns on whether the revocation was unequivocal: an email asking the firm to reassign the matter to another lawyer did not unequivocally revoke the firm's authority (*Law Society of Singapore v Chiong Chin May Selena* [2013] SGHC 5 at [28], [31]–[33]). A client may terminate the retainer at any time and for any reason, and the retainer ends as between lawyer and client once the client accepts the firm's withdrawal, even before the firm is discharged from the record (*Chew Kim Kee v Kertar & Co* [2004] SGHC 95 at [17], [22]–[23]). Closing a file does not by itself end the relationship, but it ends when the client, told the lawyer cannot act because of a conflict, goes to another lawyer (*Alfons Tanumihardja v Thio Su Mien* [2005] SGHC 54; [2005] 2 SLR(R) 445 at [8]–[9]).

**A** First, is withdrawal required under r 26(4)? Here [the client has / has not discharged [Lawyer]; [Lawyer] has / has not read an improperly obtained document]. Second, if not, which r 26(5) ground applies? Here [the client has not paid invoice X (d)(i) / the client misrepresented [fact] (e) / continuing would breach r [X] (f) / there is a serious loss of confidence (g)]. Third, under r 26(6), [Lawyer] must [give reasonable notice / allow time to instruct new solicitors / cooperate with them]; withdrawing [on the eve of trial] without that is a breach. A clause allowing termination "with immediate effect for any reason at any stage" goes beyond r 26(5) and ignores r 26(6); the PD 7.4.2 form of reservation is the proper course. Where withdrawal is required, as here [conflict / material witness / client insisting on improper conduct], [Lawyer] must still manage it to protect the client. **C** [Lawyer] [may withdraw under r 26(5)([x]) provided r 26(6) is observed / may not withdraw as proposed]; the clause is not enforceable as drafted and should be limited to the r 26(5) grounds with reasonable notice.

*Source note: r 26(1)–(6) PCR p48–50, L1 p14–16; PD 7.4.2 p1; PD 7.4.3 Part B p1; Chiong Chin May Selena, Chew Kim Kee, Alfons Tanumihardja SYL p4; pinpoints for Chiong Chin May Selena ([28], [31]–[33]), Chew Kim Kee ([17], [22]–[23]) and Alfons Tanumihardja ([2005] SGHC 54 at [8]–[9]) read from the judgments on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026); neither Chew Kim Kee nor Alfons decides a lawyer's fees on early termination. Tested PYP21 Q3.3, PYP23 Q2.1.*

### eth-w-0-16 — Lien over documents; handing over to the new lawyer
*Keywords: lien, unpaid costs, r 26(7), release documents, undertaking to hold subject to lien, reasonable security, new solicitors, transfer of file, r 7(6), r 7(7), undertaking, r 26(6), copies of documents, PD 7.3.1, lawyer leaving firm, GN 7.3.1, exiting legal practitioner, dissolution, r 26(3), PD 7.3.4*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] properly dealt with documents subject to [Firm X]'s lien for unpaid costs [and with the transfer of the client's file]. **C** [Lawyer] [breached / complied with] r 7(6) PCR, which requires a legal practitioner to honour every undertaking given to another legal practitioner [, and [Firm X] breached r 26(7), which requires release of liened documents to the new lawyer on an undertaking and security].
**R** A lawyer claiming a lien over a client's documents must release them to the new
lawyer if that lawyer undertakes to hold them subject to the lien and gives reasonable
security for the unpaid costs, or offers to pay or arrange payment of the costs on
completion and the old lawyer accepts (r 26(7)(a) PCR); otherwise the documents go to the
client against reasonable security (r 26(7)(b)). A lawyer who gives an undertaking to
another lawyer must honour it (r 7(6) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 26(7) PCR applies, which states that where a legal practitioner (A) claims to exercise a lien for unpaid legal costs over any document of the client relating to a matter,
>   - (a) where another legal practitioner (B) is acting for the client, A must release the document to B, and offer such assistance as may be necessary to enable B to take over the matter, if
>     - (i) B undertakes to hold the document subject to the lien and provides reasonable security for A's unpaid costs, or
>     - (ii) B offers to pay, or to enter into an agreement with the client to obtain payment of, A's costs on completion of the matter, and A accepts B's offer; and
>   - (b) in any other case, A must release the document to the client if A receives reasonable security for A's unpaid costs.
> - Rule 7(6) states that a legal practitioner must honour every undertaking he or she gives to another legal practitioner, and r 7(7) that a legal practitioner must not give an undertaking to another legal practitioner unless
>   - (a) he or she believes it is necessary; and
>   - (b) he or she knows, when giving it, that he or she is able to honour it. On withdrawal, the lawyer must cooperate with the client's new legal practitioner and abide by the client's choice of new lawyer (r 26(6)(a)(iii), (b) PCR).
> - PD 7.3.1 states that the outgoing lawyer may copy the documents before handing over, but must bear the cost of copying.
> - Where a lawyer leaves to join another practice, GN 7.3.1 applies.
> - Its principles (para 3) are that
>   - (a) both must act in the client's best interests and not prejudice them;
>   - (b) the client always decides which practice represents it;
>   - (c) the client must be promptly notified in a professional manner and given enough information to decide;
>   - (d) the client is the client of the current practice, which has a right to retain it; and
>   - (e) the exiting lawyer must honour contractual, fiduciary and confidentiality obligations to the current practice.
> - The exiting lawyer must tell the current practice before telling the client (para 5); must not remove client lists or other proprietary information unless agreed (para 6); the two should jointly and promptly notify the client, and any unilateral notice must not suggest the client is obliged to move or to stay (para 7); on the client's instruction to transfer, the current practice should acknowledge it and transfer the file under r 26 (para 8); without such instruction, the client is taken to stay, and the exiting lawyer must not take the file or undermine the relationship (para 9); and communications should be in writing where possible (para 10). On dissolution of a law practice, the lawyer-client relationship ends (r 26(3) PCR), all clients should be notified, client money refunded or dealt with on their instructions, and clients' instructions sought on the distribution of files (PD 7.3.4).

**P** A lawyer's undertaking to another lawyer binds regardless of later client instructions; r 7(6) admits no exception.

**A** First, [Lawyer] received the original [document] on an undertaking to protect [Firm X]'s lien, so r 7(6) required [Lawyer] to honour it. Second, [Lawyer] then passed it to [new firm] at the client's request without getting a matching r 26(7)(a)(i) undertaking and security from [new firm], and without telling the client of the arrangement. The client's instructions do not override an undertaking already given. [Lawyer] should have obtained [new firm]'s undertaking to hold the document subject to the lien before release, or kept it until [Firm X] was paid or secured. [If [Firm X] refused to release despite [new firm]'s undertaking and security, [Firm X] breached r 26(7)(a).] [If the matter is a lawyer moving firms: the client [did / did not] instruct a transfer, so under GN 7.3.1 paras 8–9 the file [should be transferred / must stay].] **C** [Lawyer] breached r 7(6), and should now ask [new firm] for the document back or for an undertaking, and tell [Firm X].

*Source note: r 26(3), (6)–(7) PCR p48–50; r 7(6)–(7) PCR p14; PD 7.3.1 p1; GN 7.3.1 p1–2; PD 7.3.4 p1; GN 7.3.1, PD 7.3.1, PD 7.3.4 listed SYL p4; summary Cyril §II.8.4–8.8 (secondary). Tested PYP22 (c).*

---

## Duties to the court

### eth-w-0-17 — Paramount duty to court; honest evaluation of the merits
*Keywords: officer of the court, s 82(1) LPA, paramount duty, administration of justice, merits, unmeritorious application, personal opinion, r 9(2)(e), not a mouthpiece, Public Trustee, Zhou Tong, BOI v BOJ, Miya Manik, r 9(1)(a), r 9(2)(h), reasonably and properly arguable, patently unmeritorious, satisfactory account*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] breached duties to the court in [bringing / running] [application]. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 9(2)(e) and r 9(2)(h)(ii) PCR, which forbid a lawyer conducting proceedings for a client to let personal opinion affect their professional assessment of the facts or the law or their duty to the court, or to draft any process, pleading or affidavit containing a contention the lawyer does not consider reasonably and properly arguable.
**R** Every advocate and solicitor is an
officer of the Supreme Court (s 82(1) Legal Profession Act 1966) and owes a paramount duty
to the court (r 4(a) PCR). A lawyer must assist in the administration of justice, must not
let personal opinion of the client affect professional assessment of the facts or law or
the duty to the court, and must not advance a contention they do not consider reasonably
arguable (r 9(1)(a), 9(2)(e), 9(2)(h)(ii) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Every person duly admitted as an advocate and solicitor is an officer of the Supreme Court (s 82(1)(a) Legal Profession Act 1966), and a legal practitioner has a paramount duty to the court, which takes precedence over the duty to the client (r 4(a) PCR).
> - Rule 9(1)(a) PCR states the guiding principle that a legal practitioner has a duty to assist in the administration of justice, and must act honourably in the interests of the administration of justice.
> - Rule 9(2) PCR applies, which states that when conducting any proceedings before a court or tribunal on behalf of a client, a legal practitioner must not
>   - (d) express the legal practitioner's personal opinion of the client's conduct;
>   - (e) allow the legal practitioner's personal opinion to affect
>     - (i) the legal practitioner's professional assessment of the facts or the law; or
>     - (ii) the legal practitioner's duty to the court or tribunal; or
>   - (h) draft any originating process, pleading, affidavit, witness statement or notice or grounds of appeal containing any of the following:
>   - (i) any statement of fact or contention which is not supported by the client or by the legal practitioner's instructions;
>     - (ii) any contention which the legal practitioner does not consider to be reasonably and properly arguable;
>     - (iii) any allegation of fraud, unless the legal practitioner has clear instructions to make such an allegation and has before the legal practitioner reasonably credible material which establishes a prima facie case of fraud; or
>     - (iv) in the case of an affidavit or a witness statement, any statement of fact other than the substance of any evidence which the legal practitioner reasonably believes, having regard to the legal practitioner's instructions, the witness making the affidavit or statement would give if that evidence was given orally.
> - Where proceedings are used to pressure the other side rather than to obtain relief, r 10(5) PCR may also apply, which states that a legal practitioner must not render to his or her client any advice which the legal practitioner knows or has reasonable grounds to believe is sought by the client for any dishonest, fraudulent or unlawful purpose.

**P** An officer of the court has obligations and responsibilities in upholding the legal framework (*Public Trustee v By Products Traders Pte Ltd* [2005] 3 SLR(R) 449 at [26]), and all solicitors have an absolute and overriding duty first and foremost to the court to ensure the proper and efficient administration of justice (at [30], [35]). All solicitors must carefully assess the merits of their clients' cases before engaging in court proceedings; the duty has two facets: to the client, since recklessly instituting proceedings improperly takes the client's money, and to the court, since pursuing proceedings without considering the merits misuses the court's time (*Zhou Tong v Public Prosecutor* [2010] 4 SLR 534 at [19]–[21]). Counsel are not the mere mouthpieces of their clients and must counsel them on what is permissible, because they owe a paramount duty to the court (*BOI v BOJ* [2018] 2 SLR 1156 at [3]–[4]). Filing an unmeritorious application may be professional misconduct, and the court considers (1) how patently unmeritorious the application is, and (2) whether counsel can offer a satisfactory account for filing it; dire consequences for the client, even a capital sentence, do not justify ill-considered and baseless applications (*Miya Manik v Public Prosecutor* [2021] 2 SLR 1169 at [82]).

**A** Here [Lawyer] [filed the application without evidence of an essential element, e.g. a real risk of dissipation]. Under r 9(2)(h)(ii), the question is whether [Lawyer] could honestly consider that contention reasonably and properly arguable on the material [he / she] had; with [no evidence of X] [Lawyer] could not. Under r 9(2)(e), [Lawyer]'s [admiration for / sympathy with] the client [affected the assessment of the facts / displaced the duty to the court]. Applying *Miya Manik*, the lack of merit was [patent], and the only account is [the client's insistence], which is not a satisfactory account, since counsel must counsel rather than act as a mouthpiece (*BOI v BOJ*). [If the purpose was to pressure the other side, that points to a collateral purpose under r 10(5).] **C** [Lawyer] breached r 9(2)(e) and r 9(2)(h)(ii) PCR, and should advise the client candidly and correct the position with the court (r 9(5) PCR).

*Source note: s 82(1) LPA p207; r 4 PCR p9; r 9 PCR p17–19; r 10(5) PCR p21; Public Trustee at [26] SYL p5, L4 p3, at [30] SYL p5 ("especially at [30], [53]"), at [35] L4 p5 (quote); Zhou Tong at [19]–[21] SYL p5, [19] L4 p11; BOI v BOJ [2018] 2 SLR 1156 at [3]–[4] L4 p8 (SYL gives [2018] SGCA 61); Miya Manik [2021] 2 SLR 1169 at [82] L4 p12 (SYL gives [2021] SGCA 90). Tested PYP21 Q2, PS2 Q1.*

### eth-w-0-18 — Misleading the court
*Keywords: mislead, r 9(2)(a), false statement, half-truth, omission, candour, knowingly or recklessly, Seah Zhen Wei Paul, Loh Der Ming, Ram Goswami, Public Trustee, Chung Ting Fai, false affidavit, drafting affidavit, extension of time, words in the client's mouth, r 9(2)(b), r 9(2)(c), r 9(3)(a), adverse authority, r 9(5), r 10(4), without belief in its truth, settled case*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] misled the court by [statement / omission]. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 9(2)(a) PCR, which forbids a lawyer conducting proceedings for a client to knowingly mislead or attempt to mislead the court in any way.
**R** A lawyer must not knowingly mislead or attempt to
mislead the court, must not fabricate facts, and must not include a statement known to be
false in any document used in proceedings (r 9(2)(a)–(c) PCR). If the lawyer unknowingly
breaches r 9, they must disclose and remedy it once aware (r 9(5) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 9(2) PCR applies, which states that when conducting any proceedings before a court or tribunal on behalf of a client, a legal practitioner must not
>   - (a) knowingly mislead or attempt to mislead in any way, whether by doing anything referred to in sub-paragraph (b) or (c) or otherwise,
>     - (i) the court or tribunal;
>     - (ii) any staff of the court or tribunal;
>     - (iii) any other legal practitioner; or
>     - (iv) any witness in, or any other person involved in or associated with, those proceedings;
>   - (b) fabricate any fact or evidence in any communication with, or representation or submission to, the court or tribunal; or
>   - (c) include, in any document used for those proceedings, any statement of fact which the legal practitioner knows to be false.
> - Rule 9(3)(a) PCR adds that the lawyer must inform the court or tribunal of every relevant decision, and every relevant legislative provision, of which the lawyer is aware, whether that decision or provision supports or rebuts the lawyer's contentions.
> - Rule 9(5) PCR states that, subject to rule 6, where a legal practitioner unknowingly contravenes any provision of this rule, the legal practitioner must, as soon as practicable after becoming aware of the contravention, disclose the contravention to each person affected by it, and take reasonable steps to remedy it.
> - Rule 10(4) PCR states that where a legal practitioner knows that the client is about to give, or has given, false evidence or false information to a court or tribunal, the legal practitioner must
>   - (a) cease to act for the client; or
>   - (b) if the legal practitioner continues to act, conduct the client's case in a manner that does not perpetuate the falsehood.

**P** Solicitors must never mislead the court either actively or passively, nor consciously furnish to the court erroneous or incomplete information (*Public Trustee v By Products Traders Pte Ltd* [2005] 3 SLR(R) 449 at [26]–[36], especially [35]). The mental element of a breach of r 9 is made out if a false statement is made to the court by a solicitor (a) knowing it is false; (b) without belief in its truth; or (c) recklessly, without caring whether it is true or false (*Law Society of Singapore v Seah Zhen Wei Paul* [2024] SGHC 224 at [69]); there, agreeing not to volunteer that the suit had settled unless asked, and to tell the Court of Appeal it was "held in abeyance", misled the court (at [109]–[110]). A statement that implies something the lawyer knows is untrue is dishonest: saying he had been "unable" to obtain instructions implied an attempt he never made (*Loh Der Ming Andrew v Koh Tien Hua* [2022] SGHC 84 at [64], [66]; three years' suspension). Submitting that bail money was the bailor's savings, knowing it was not his, earned six months' suspension (*Re Ram Goswami* [1988] 2 SLR(R) 183). A solicitor who drafted a false affidavit for a client to support an application for an extension of time deserved more than a censure: since solicitors draft most affidavits, the profession must be impressed not to put words into clients' mouths to mislead the court or to state untruths not borne out by the evidence (*Law Society of Singapore v Chung Ting Fai* [2006] 4 SLR(R) 587 at [48]).

**A** Here [Lawyer] told the court "[statement]" while knowing [fact]. Limb (a): the statement was made to [the court / the registrar / opposing counsel], each of whom r 9(2)(a) protects. Mental element (*Seah Zhen Wei Paul*): [Lawyer] [knew it was false / had no belief in its truth / did not care whether it was true]. Even if literally true (e.g. "my instructions are that she has not disposed of any assets"), it conveyed a false impression by withholding [the imminent transfer / the settlement / prior failed applications], which is passive misleading (*Public Trustee*) and the implied-assertion point in *Loh Der Ming*. A direct question from the court makes the omission more serious. [If the omission was an adverse authority, r 9(3)(a) is breached.] **C** [Lawyer] breached r 9(2)(a) PCR; before judgment [Lawyer] must correct the record (r 9(5) PCR) or, if the client forbids disclosure, cease to act without revealing confidences (r 10(4) PCR).

*Source note: r 9(2), (3), (5) PCR p17–19; r 10(4) PCR p20–21; Public Trustee at [26]–[36] SYL p5, [35] L4 p5; Seah Zhen Wei Paul at [69], [109]–[110] L4 p21 (L4 cites [2024] 5 SLR 915); Loh Der Ming at [64], [66] L4 p20 (L4 cites [2022] 3 SLR 1417); Ram Goswami L4 p19; Chung Ting Fai at [48] L4 p7 (quoted), SYL p5. Tested PYP21 Q2, PS2 Q1.*

### eth-w-0-19 — Applications without notice: full and frank disclosure
*Keywords: ex parte, without notice, full and frank disclosure, Mareva, freezing order, injunction, r 30(3), r 9(3)(b), material facts, foreign proceedings, de Souza, r 10(3), Supreme Court PD 2021 para 71, notice to other parties, defeat the purpose, search order undertaking*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] met the duty of candour on the without-notice application. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 9(3)(b) PCR, which requires a lawyer to disclose to the court every fact, item of evidence, item of information and other matter that the law requires to be disclosed in the proceedings.
**R** A lawyer must
disclose to the court every fact and item of evidence the law requires to be disclosed
(r 9(3)(b) PCR) and, on a without-notice application, comply with the practice directions
on such applications (r 30(3) PCR). An applicant without notice owes a duty of full and
frank disclosure of all material facts.

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 9(3)(b) PCR applies, which states that when conducting proceedings before a court or tribunal, a legal practitioner must disclose to the court or tribunal, and to every other person involved in or associated with those proceedings,
>   - (i) every fact, item of evidence, item of information and other matter which the legal practitioner is required by law to disclose in those proceedings to the court or tribunal and to that other person, respectively; and
>   - (ii) any procedural irregularity of which the legal practitioner is aware.
> - An applicant without notice owes a duty of full and frank disclosure of all material facts.
> - Rule 30(3) PCR states that a legal practitioner who initiates any communication with the court in relation to an application without notice filed by the legal practitioner must comply with all relevant practice directions relating to applications without notice; and r 30(4) PCR states that, save for that, r 30(1)–(2) do not apply to such a communication.
> - For injunctions, para 71 of the Supreme Court Practice Directions 2021 states that
>   - (1) under O 13 r 1(3) Rules of Court 2021 a party may apply for an injunction without notice, supported by an affidavit stating the urgency, explaining why the defendant should not be informed of the application, and the merits;
>   - (2) the applicant must nonetheless give notice to the other parties before the hearing (by email or, in extreme urgency, by telephone), at least two hours before the hearing, stating the date, time and place and the nature of the relief sought, with a copy of the originating process and application if possible and the supporting affidavit in draft as soon as it is ready, and, if the other parties are absent at the hearing, the applicant's solicitors should tell the court
>     - (a) what attempts were made to notify them,
>     - (b) what documents were given to them and when, and
>     - (c) whether they consent to the application being heard without them; and
>   - (3) those directions need not be followed if notice would or might defeat the purpose of the application, but the reasons must then be clearly set out in the supporting affidavit.
> - Rule 10(3) PCR also applies, which states that, to the extent that a legal practitioner is able, the legal practitioner must prevent the client from, must not be a party to, and must not assist the client in
>   - (a) suppressing evidence; or
>   - (b) giving false evidence or false information to a court or tribunal.

**P** In *Law Society of Singapore v de Souza Christopher James* [2023] SGHC 318, on a without-notice application to use documents obtained under search orders for reports to law enforcement agencies, the client had a duty to disclose its prior breach of the search-order undertaking; the majority held that the supporting affidavit did disclose the breach (at [139]), while Ramesh JAD, concurring, held the disclosure objectively inadequate because it left out the excerpts given to the authorities and the extent of that disclosure (at [157], [206]–[207]); both held that the lawyer had no subjective intention to assist suppression, so r 10(3)(a) was not breached (at [91], [126], [220], [222]). An inadvertent or negligent failure to make full and frank disclosure breaches that duty but not r 10(3)(a) (at [88]). The Court of Three Judges nevertheless found that the lawyer had no subjective intention to suppress relevant information (at [207]).

**A** Here [Lawyer] knew of [earlier identical proceedings / failed freezing applications abroad / a prior breach], which plainly bear on whether the order should be granted. Under r 9(3)(b)(i) the law (the duty of full and frank disclosure) required that to be disclosed, and partial disclosure is not enough (*de Souza* at [207], per Ramesh JAD). [Lawyer] omitted it [on the client's instruction], so [Lawyer] was a party to suppressing evidence under r 10(3)(a); unlike *de Souza*, the omission here was deliberate. A client cannot instruct a lawyer to breach the duty of disclosure: [Lawyer] had to advise that disclosure was required and, if the client refused, decline to file. Under r 30(3) and para 71, [Lawyer] also had to [give notice to the other side / explain in the affidavit why notice would defeat the purpose], and did not. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 9(3)(b), r 10(3) and r 30(3) PCR; the order is liable to be set aside, and [Lawyer] should disclose now or cease to act.

*Source note: r 9(3) PCR p19; r 10(3) PCR p20; r 30(3)–(4) PCR p52; Supreme Court PD 2021 para 71(1)–(3) L7 p5–7, checked against the e-PD text on judiciary.gov.sg (3 Nov 2026; para 71 is headed "Applications without notice for injunctions" and does not itself use the words "full and frank disclosure"; para 73(1)(g) requires the affidavit to set out any other material facts the court should be aware of); de Souza listed SYL p6 ([160]–[168], [207], which are in Ramesh JAD's concurrence) and L7 p9, facts L4 p30; majority [88], [91], [126], [139] and concurrence [157], [206]–[207], [220], [222] read from the judgment on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026; L4's [2024] 3 SLR 1570 report cite not confirmed online). Full and frank disclosure duty stated in PYP21 p4 (question note). Tested PYP21 Q2.*

### eth-w-0-20 — Attestation of documents
*Keywords: attest, attestation, witness signature, Commissioner for Oaths, affidavit, not in presence, false attestation, PD 1.6.1, Thirumurthy, personally appear, grossly improper conduct, r 9(2)(c), r 32, supervision*

**I** The issue is whether the [affidavit / document] was properly attested. **C** [Lawyer] breached PD 1.6.1, which warns against attesting a signature without the signatory personally appearing before the attesting practitioner and states that false attestation may amount to grossly improper conduct in the discharge of a practitioner's professional duty.
**R** A signatory must personally
appear before the person attesting the signature; false attestation may amount to grossly
