improper conduct in the discharge of professional duty (PD 1.6.1). A lawyer must not present any evidence known to be false (r 9(1)(d) PCR), or include in any document used in proceedings a statement of fact known to be false (r 9(2)(c) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - PD 1.6.1 applies.
> - Part A warns members of the dangers of attesting the signature of documents without the signatory having personally appeared before the legal practitioner, and particularly advises members who are Commissioners for Oaths to heed the warning.
> - Part B states that false attestation of documents may amount to grossly improper conduct in the discharge of a legal practitioner's professional duty and a breach of the Legal Profession Act, and that legal practitioners should be mindful of the serious and obvious dangers of this practice.
> - That is due cause under s 83(2)(b) Legal Profession Act 1966, which covers an advocate and solicitor who has been guilty of fraudulent or grossly improper conduct in the discharge of his or her professional duty.
> - If the document is used in proceedings, r 9(2)(c) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner conducting proceedings on behalf of a client must not include, in any document used for those proceedings, any statement of fact which the legal practitioner knows to be false; r 9(1)(d) PCR states the principle that a legal practitioner must not present, or permit to be presented, any evidence or information which the legal practitioner knows to be false.
> - Rule 32 PCR states that a legal practitioner must, regardless of the legal practitioner's designation in a law practice, exercise proper supervision over the staff working under the legal practitioner in the law practice.

**P** A lawyer who falsely attested that a power of attorney was signed in his presence, when it was not, was suspended for nine months (*Law Society of Singapore v Thirumurthy Ayernaar Pambayan* [2022] SGHC 79).

**A** Here [client] signed and left before [the Commissioner] arrived, so the signatory did not personally appear before the attesting practitioner (PD 1.6.1 Part A). [Lawyer] assured the client this was fine and arranged for the Commissioner to sign later [in front of staff], so the jurat states something untrue: a false attestation (Part B), and [Lawyer] procured it, as in *Thirumurthy*. That may be grossly improper conduct under s 83(2)(b). As supervising lawyer, [Lawyer] is also responsible for the staff involvement (r 32). If the affidavit is filed, it is a document used in proceedings containing a statement of fact [Lawyer] knows to be false (r 9(2)(c)). **C** [Lawyer] breached PD 1.6.1 [and r 9(2)(c) PCR]; the affidavit must not be used and should be re-sworn properly.

*Source note: PD 1.6.1 Parts A–B p1; s 83(2)(b) LPA p217; r 9(1)(d), (2)(c) PCR p17–18; r 32 PCR p53; Thirumurthy SYL p5, facts L4 p22 (L4 cites [2022] 4 SLR 462). Tested PS2 Q1.*

### eth-w-0-21 — Client's improper conduct; advice for a dishonest purpose
*Keywords: r 10, client misleading court, suppress evidence, dishonest purpose, unlawful purpose, undertaking to court, assist client, cease to act, Narindar Singh, Bachoo Mohan Singh, Leong Pek Gan, r 10(2), r 10(5), r 10(6), commensurate with control, without foundation, face value, proper foundation*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] assisted the client to [mislead the court / evade an undertaking / act dishonestly]. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 10(5) PCR, which forbids a lawyer to give the client any advice the lawyer knows or has reasonable grounds to believe is sought for a dishonest, fraudulent or unlawful purpose.
**R** A lawyer must
not give advice they know or have reason to believe is sought for a dishonest, fraudulent
or unlawful purpose, and must not knowingly assist or permit the client to mislead the
court or do anything dishonest (r 10(5)–(6) PCR). So far as able, the lawyer must prevent
the client suppressing evidence or giving false evidence (r 10(3) PCR); if the client has
done so, the lawyer must cease to act or continue without perpetuating the falsehood
(r 10(4) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 10(1) PCR states the guiding principles that
>   - (a) a legal practitioner's duty to assist in the administration of justice includes a responsibility, commensurate with the amount of control the legal practitioner has over the client, to prevent the client from misleading a court or tribunal in any manner and from otherwise acting improperly;
>   - (b) a legal practitioner must exercise professional judgment over the substance and purpose of any advice given and any document drafted; and
>   - (c) a legal practitioner must not engage in any conduct which would be unlawful, unethical or otherwise improper, whether or not it would promote the client's cause.
> - Rule 10(5) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must not render to his or her client any advice which the legal practitioner knows or has reasonable grounds to believe is sought by the client for any dishonest, fraudulent or unlawful purpose.
> - Rule 10(6) PCR states that a legal practitioner must not knowingly assist or permit the client
>   - (a) to mislead a court or tribunal; or
>   - (b) to do any other thing which the legal practitioner considers to be dishonest.
> - Rule 10(2) PCR states that a legal practitioner whose client is a party to proceedings must inform the client of the client's responsibilities to the court and to every other party, including the client's duties
>   - (a) to be truthful towards the court; and
>   - (b) to comply with every legal requirement concerning the conduct and presentation of the client's case.
> - Rule 10(3) PCR states that, to the extent that the legal practitioner is able, the legal practitioner must prevent the client from, must not be a party to, and must not assist the client in,
>   - (a) suppressing evidence; or
>   - (b) giving false evidence or false information to a court or tribunal.
> - Rule 10(4) PCR states that where the legal practitioner knows the client is about to give, or has given, false evidence or false information to a court or tribunal, the legal practitioner must
>   - (a) cease to act; or
>   - (b) if continuing to act, conduct the client's case in a manner that does not perpetuate the falsehood.

**P** A lawyer who acted with his client to corruptly solicit a gratification from a co-accused's family, in exchange for the client exonerating the co-accused, was convicted and struck off (*Narindar Singh s/o Malagar Singh v Public Prosecutor* [1996] 3 SLR(R) 318 at [48]–[53]). On how far a lawyer may rely on the client's instructions, the court held (1) a solicitor should decline instructions, or doubt them, if they plainly appear to be without foundation, e.g. lacking logical or legal coherence; (2) a solicitor need not verify the client's instructions with other sources unless there is compelling evidence that they are dubious (both at [137(e)]); (3) whether a claim is "false" depends on whether the litigant's action has a proper foundation entitling him to seek judicial relief (at [94]); and (4) counsel was entitled to accept the client's instructions at face value (at [101]) (*Bachoo Mohan Singh v Public Prosecutor* [2010] SGCA 25; [2010] 4 SLR 137). A lawyer of over 30 years' standing who acted for both sides of a property "sale" that was in substance a high-interest loan by an unlicensed moneylender was wilfully blind to the illegal purpose and was guilty of advising a client she had reasonable grounds to believe sought advice to advance an illegal purpose, under the predecessor of r 10(5); reasonable grounds are judged objectively from the lawyer's position and experience, and the illegal purpose need not be completed (*Law Society of Singapore v Leong Pek Gan* [2016] SGHC 165 at [86]–[93]); she was suspended for two and a half years (*Law Society of Singapore v Leong Pek Gan* [2016] SGHC 250 at [19]).

**A** Here [client] asked how to [move assets despite an undertaking to court / hide proceedings], and [Lawyer] devised [a transfer to a nominee / a pre-signed affidavit to be filed later]. Under r 10(5), [Lawyer] knew, or at least had reasonable grounds to believe, the advice was sought to [breach an undertaking to court], an unlawful purpose. Under r 10(6)(a)–(b), [Lawyer] knowingly assisted the client to [mislead the court / do something dishonest]. These were not instructions [Lawyer] could take at face value under *Bachoo Mohan Singh*: they plainly lacked any proper foundation. [Lawyer]'s control over the client was [high, since Lawyer drafted the scheme] (r 10(1)(a)), and [Lawyer] did not tell the client of the duty to comply with the undertaking (r 10(2)). **C** [Lawyer] breached r 10(5) and r 10(6) PCR, and should have refused, warned the client, and ceased to act if the client persisted.

*Source note: r 10 PCR p20–21; Narindar Singh at [48]–[53] SYL p6, facts L4 p24; Bachoo Mohan Singh at [137(e)], [94], [101] L4 p26; the pinpoints are in the Court of Appeal's criminal reference decision [2010] SGCA 25 (read on eLitigation, 3 Nov 2026), not the procedural decision [2010] 1 SLR 966 ([2009] SGCA 59) that SYL cites; the [2010] 4 SLR 137 report cite for [2010] SGCA 25 is L4's and was not confirmed online; Leong Pek Gan SYL p6, facts, test and outcome read from [2016] SGHC 165 and [2016] SGHC 250 on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026; it applied r 22 of the 2010 rules, which the judgment does not itself map to r 10(5)). Tested PS2 Q1, PYP21 Q2.*

### eth-w-0-44 — Respect, courtesy and punctuality before the court
*Keywords: respect for court, courtesy, r 13(1)–(3), punctuality, PD 1.4.2, protracted arguments, PD 8.5.8, insolent, discourteous, turned his back, late, deadlines, Ravi Madasamy, Syed Suhail, standing dignity and authority, stood down, adjournment priority, open court*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer]'s conduct in court was disrespectful or discourteous. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 13(2) and r 13(3) PCR, which require a lawyer always to be respectful of a court and always to be courteous in the conduct of a case, to the court and to any other person involved in the proceedings.
**R** A lawyer must present a case and behave in a way
that respects the court and is consistent with its standing, dignity and authority, must
always be respectful of the court, and must be courteous to the court and everyone involved in
the proceedings (r 13(1)–(3) PCR). Lawyers must be punctual for all hearings (PD 1.4.2), and in
chambers should warn the court of long arguments and give priority to applications for adjournment, whether or not by consent (PD 8.5.8).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 13(1) PCR states the guiding principles that
>   - (a) a legal practitioner must present a case, and behave, before a court or tribunal in a manner which is respectful of the court or tribunal; and
>   - (b) the manner in which a legal practitioner conducts a case must be consistent with the standing, dignity and authority of the court or tribunal.
> - Rule 13(2) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must always be respectful of a court or tribunal, and r 13(3) PCR states that a legal practitioner must always be courteous in the conduct of a case before a court or tribunal, whether to the court or tribunal, or to any other person involved in the proceedings. "Court" means any court of competent jurisdiction in Singapore (r 2(1) PCR).
> - PD 1.4.2 states that all members should be punctual for all court hearings, and should make the appropriate estimation for lengths of adjournments to assist in the general administration of the court's time.
> - PD 8.5.8 states that legal practitioners appearing in chambers who anticipate that their arguments will be substantial should inform the court at the start of the hearing and/or ask for their case to be stood down, and, subject to the court's ruling, should where possible give priority to other practitioners making applications for adjournment, whether or not by consent, as an act of courtesy to practitioners waiting their turn.

**P** Turning his back to the judge while being addressed, walking away, remaining seated, accusing the judge of not addressing him properly, threatening to report the judge, and being insolent when told not to speak loudly to the prosecuting officer was misconduct (*Law Society of Singapore v Ravi Madasamy* [2007] 2 SLR(R) 300 at [25]). Lack of courtesy may be a minor infraction if it shows only poor manners and does not undermine the court's authority, but lack of respect for the court, especially in session and displayed publicly before court staff, counsel or the public, is more serious because it diminishes the court's standing and undermines its authority (at [26]). Arriving late, missing filing deadlines and making ad hominem attacks on persons not before the court that prolonged a hearing was improper (*Syed Suhail bin Syed Zin v Attorney-General* [2022] SGHC 184 at [11]–[12]).

**A** Here [Lawyer] [was late for the hearing / raised his voice at the judge / refused to accept the ruling / attacked persons not before the court / argued at length in chambers without warning]. Under r 13(2), the question is whether this showed disrespect for the court itself; under *Ravi Madasamy* at [26], it is worse because it happened [in open court, before the public and court staff] and [in a contemptuous manner]. Under r 13(3), [the remarks to opposing counsel / witnesses] were discourteous to persons involved in the proceedings. [Lateness breaches PD 1.4.2, as in *Syed Suhail*; failing to warn of long arguments or to give way to adjournment applications breaches PD 8.5.8.] **C** [Lawyer] breached r 13(2) and r 13(3) PCR; [disrespect / discourtesy] of this kind is due cause under s 83(2)(b) or (h) LPA.

*Source note: r 2(1) PCR p4; r 13(1)–(3) PCR p24; PD 1.4.2 p1; PD 8.5.8 p1; s 83(2)(b), (h) LPA p217–218; Ravi Madasamy at [25] L4 p15, at [26] L4 p16; Syed Suhail at [11]–[12] SYL p6, facts Cyril §III.5.1 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-45 — Fictitious authorities and generative AI
*Keywords: generative AI, ChatGPT, fictitious authorities, fake cases, hallucinated citations, verify, competence, r 9(2)(b), r 9(2)(f), r 5(2)(c), r 4(h), Tajudin, Tan Hai Peng Micheal, Registrar's Circular No 1 of 2024, r 5(2)(d), r 9(5), r 32, supervision, non-delegable, personal costs*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] breached duties by citing [non-existent / misdescribed] authorities produced with a generative AI tool. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 9(2)(f) PCR, which forbids a lawyer to knowingly or recklessly cite the law out of context or advance any submission the lawyer knows or ought reasonably to know is contrary to the law, and r 5(2)(c) PCR, which requires a lawyer to act with reasonable diligence and competence.
**R** A
lawyer must not fabricate anything in a submission, nor knowingly or recklessly cite the law
out of context or advance a proposition they ought reasonably to know is wrong (r 9(2)(b), (f)
PCR), and must act with reasonable competence (r 5(2)(c)–(d), r 4(h) PCR). If the lawyer unknowingly misleads the court, they
must disclose and remedy it as soon as they become aware (r 9(5) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 9(2) PCR applies, which states that when conducting any proceedings before a court or tribunal on behalf of a client, a legal practitioner must not (b) fabricate any fact or evidence in any communication with, or representation or submission to, the court or tribunal; or (f) knowingly or recklessly cite the law out of context, interpret the law in a manner calculated to mislead the court or tribunal, or otherwise advance any submission, opinion or proposition which the legal practitioner knows or ought reasonably to know is contrary to the law.
> - Rule 5(2) PCR states that a legal practitioner must
>   - (c) act with reasonable diligence and competence in the provision of services to the client; and
>   - (d) ensure that the legal practitioner has the relevant knowledge, skills and attributes required for each matter undertaken on behalf of the client, and apply them in a manner appropriate to that matter.
> - Rule 4(h) PCR states the principle that a legal practitioner must keep up to date with all pertinent developments in the law in the legal practitioner's area of practice.
> - Rule 32 PCR states that a legal practitioner must, regardless of designation, exercise proper supervision over the staff working under the legal practitioner in the law practice.
> - Rule 9(5) PCR states that, subject to rule 6, where a legal practitioner unknowingly contravenes any provision of r 9, the legal practitioner must, as soon as practicable after becoming aware of the contravention, disclose it to each person affected and take reasonable steps to remedy it.

**P** The courts have held that a lawyer has a professional, non-delegable duty to ensure every authority cited exists and is accurate; it is impermissible to delegate research or drafting to GenAI without independently verifying the output, and blaming a junior is no answer, since the lawyer must supervise (*Tajudin bin Gulam Rasul v Suriaya bte Haja Mohideen* [2025] SGHCR 33 at [52], [54], [93], [101]: personal costs of $800 under O 21 r 6 Rules of Court 2021). The counsel who signed and the solicitor who let a paralegal draft were each ordered to pay $5,000 personally; AI is only ever the "handmaiden", and how the fake case arose is immaterial (*Tan Hai Peng Micheal v Tan Cheong Joo* [2026] SGHC 49 at [32], [44], [46], [66]). Both apply the Registrar's Circular No 1 of 2024 (GenAI Guide), and warn that grave cases go to discipline. The Guide, in force from 1 October 2024, does not prohibit generative AI, but the user assumes full responsibility for the output (para 3(3)), and lawyers remain bound to ensure that material put before the court is independently verified, accurate, true and appropriate (para 3(2)(a)). Users must fact-check and proof-read, verify that cited cases, legislation and texts actually exist and stand for the propositions attributed to them, and verify quotations (para 5(3)(a)–(c)); asking an AI tool to confirm that the materials exist is not enough (para 5(3)(e)). No pre-emptive declaration of AI use is required, but the user must be ready to identify the AI-generated portions and explain how they were verified, and may be required to declare compliance by affidavit (paras 3(3), 5(4), 5(8)). Breach may lead to costs, including personal costs against a lawyer, the material being disregarded or given less weight, and disciplinary action (para 9).

**A** Here [Lawyer / an associate / a paralegal] used [tool] to draft [submissions] and filed them without checking the cases, [three] of which do not exist. Under r 9(2)(f), filing authorities that were never checked is at least reckless, and [Lawyer] ought reasonably to have known the propositions were unsupported; a non-existent case presented as real is also a fabrication under r 9(2)(b). Under r 5(2)(c)–(d), reasonable competence required [Lawyer] to verify every citation, and the duty is non-delegable (*Tajudin*). Under r 32, [Lawyer] had to supervise the [associate / paralegal] who drafted it, as in *Tan Hai Peng Micheal*. If [Lawyer] did not know, r 9(5) requires disclosure and correction as soon as [Lawyer] becomes aware. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 9(2)(f) and r 5(2)(c) PCR, must withdraw and correct the submissions at once (r 9(5)), and may face personal costs and discipline.

*Source note: r 4 PCR p9; r 5 PCR p10; r 9 PCR p17–19; r 32 PCR p53; Tajudin and Tan Hai Peng pinpoints read from the judgments on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026); both listed SYL p5; Registrar's Circular No 1 of 2024 listed SYL p5; Guide paras 3, 5 and 9 read from the official PDF on judiciary.gov.sg (3 Nov 2026), not in the course materials.*

### eth-w-0-46 — Chambers practice: lawyer on record, client in chambers, third-party fees
*Keywords: lawyer on record, PD 7.3.2, change of solicitors, client in chambers, GN 1.1.1, chambers hearing closed, advance notice, third party fees, witness expenses, PD 8.8.1, r 7(5), foreign colleague, protracted arguments, PD 8.5.8, matrimonial, ancillary matters, professional agents, r 12(8)*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] handled [the change of solicitors / client attendance in chambers / third-party fees] properly. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach [PD 7.3.2, which forbids a lawyer to knowingly agree to act for a party in civil proceedings while another lawyer is on the record for that party / GN 1.1.1, which requires advance notice to the other party before seeking permission for a client to attend chambers / r 7(5) PCR, which requires a lawyer who appoints another lawyer to pay that lawyer's fees].
**R**
Where another lawyer is on the record for a party in civil proceedings, no other lawyer may
knowingly agree to act for that party in those proceedings, save in ignorance and urgency
(PD 7.3.2). Chambers hearings are closed by default; a client may attend only with the judge's
or registrar's permission, after advance notice to the other side (GN 1.1.1). A lawyer who
calls a witness must tell the witness the lawyer is not personally liable for their lawful
expenses, and should secure the client's ability to pay or take money in advance (PD 8.8.1).
A lawyer who appoints another lawyer must pay that lawyer's fees unless agreed otherwise
(r 7(5) PCR), but is not personally liable for the fees of non-lawyer professionals engaged for
the client (PD 8.8.1).

> **Rule in full**
> - PD 7.3.2 applies, which states that if in any civil proceeding the name of any legal practitioner appears on the record for any party, no other legal practitioner shall knowingly agree to act or continue to act for that party in that proceeding, unless he or she has, in ignorance that the name so appears on the record, already agreed to act and is unable, by reason of circumstances or urgency or the like, to refuse to act further without exposing himself or herself to a charge of breach of professional duty.
> - GN 1.1.1 para 2 states that
>   - (a) the default position is that chambers hearings are closed hearings;
>   - (b) the presence of clients in chambers is a matter of discretion for the hearing Judge or Registrar; and
>   - (c) if a client wishes to be present, the legal practitioner should give advance notice to the other party before applying to the Judge or Registrar for permission, which is decided case by case.
> - Para 3 explains that the notice avoids one party being present while the other is not, an issue particularly in matrimonial proceedings, and para 4 states that members should consider such an application where clients have a vital interest in the outcome, for instance in matrimonial ancillary matters.
> - PD 8.8.1 Part A states that where a legal practitioner calls a witness for the client, the practitioner shall, before calling the witness, make clear to the witness that the practitioner will not be personally responsible for the disbursements and expenses the witness is allowed or entitled to under the law, and should either satisfy himself or herself that the client is willing and able to pay them or obtain payment from the client in advance; and that a practitioner who directs a client to a foreign colleague is not responsible for that colleague's charges, nor entitled to a share of the fee.
> - Rule 12(8) PCR states that a legal practitioner
>   - (a) may pay, or offer to pay, a witness any disbursements and expenses which the witness is allowed or entitled to under the law; but
>   - (b) must not make, or offer to make, any payment contingent upon the nature of the evidence or the outcome of the case.
> - Rule 7(5) PCR states that a legal practitioner who appoints another legal practitioner must pay or ensure the payment of the latter's fees, unless both legal practitioners agree otherwise.
> - PD 8.8.1 Part B states that members are not professionally liable for the fees of other professional agents engaged on the client's behalf, except another legal practitioner; a practitioner is under no obligation personally to pay a non-lawyer professional's fees unless ordered by the court, responsibility falls on the client, and failure to meet them is not professional misconduct, though contractual liability to the agent is unaffected; it is good practice to tell the agent the client will pay directly, or to take sufficient money in advance.
> - PD 8.5.8 states that practitioners in chambers who anticipate substantial arguments should inform the court at the start and/or ask to be stood down, and should where possible give priority to practitioners making applications for adjournment, as a courtesy.

**P** These are aspects of courtesy to the court and to other practitioners, since a legal practitioner must always be courteous in the conduct of a case (r 13(3) PCR) and must treat other legal practitioners with courtesy and fairness (r 7(2) PCR).

**A** Here [Lawyer] [took over while X was still on record: under PD 7.3.2, did Lawyer know X was on record, and if not, can Lawyer show urgency that made refusal a breach of duty?] / [brought the client into chambers without notice: GN 1.1.1 para 2(c) required notice to the other side first and the judge's permission, and the matter (e.g. ancillary matters) did / did not give the client a vital interest] / [engaged counsel without agreeing who pays: r 7(5) makes Lawyer liable for counsel's fees unless agreed otherwise, but Lawyer is not professionally liable for the [expert / surveyor]'s fees under PD 8.8.1 Part B] / [called a witness without telling them Lawyer would not pay their expenses: PD 8.8.1 Part A]. **C** [Lawyer] should [wait for a notice of change / give notice and seek permission / pay counsel's fees as r 7(5) requires / tell the witness and secure the expenses from the client].

*Source note: PD 7.3.2 p1; GN 1.1.1 paras 2–4 p1; PD 8.8.1 Parts A–B p1; PD 8.5.8 p1; r 7(2), (5) PCR p13–14; r 12(8) PCR p23; r 13(3) PCR p24.*

### eth-w-0-47 — Discovery duties; suppressing evidence; reporting crime
*Keywords: discovery, disclosure, document production, explain obligations, supervise, r 10(3), suppress evidence, Teo Wai Cheong, PD 1.6.2, lay information, criminal offence, bigamy, Shahira Banu, de Souza, r 10(2), r 9(3)(b), relevance, review documents, subjective intention, partial disclosure*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] met the duties on disclosure of documents. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 10(3)(a) PCR, which requires a lawyer, so far as able, to prevent the client from suppressing evidence and not to be a party to or assist in it.
**R** So far as able, a lawyer must prevent the
client from suppressing evidence and must not assist in it (r 10(3)(a) PCR), and must disclose
everything the law requires (r 9(3)(b) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 10(3) PCR applies, which states that, to the extent that a legal practitioner is able, the legal practitioner must prevent his or her client from, must not be a party to, and must not assist the client in, doing either or both of the following:
>   - (a) suppressing evidence;
>   - (b) giving false evidence or false information to a court or tribunal.
> - Rule 10(2) PCR states that a legal practitioner whose client is a party to proceedings must inform the client of the client's responsibilities to the court and to every other party, including the client's duties
>   - (a) to be truthful towards the court; and
>   - (b) to comply with every legal requirement concerning the conduct and presentation of the client's case.
> - Rule 9(3)(b) PCR states that the legal practitioner must disclose to the court and every other person involved in the proceedings
>   - (i) every fact, item of evidence, item of information and other matter which the legal practitioner is required by law to disclose; and
>   - (ii) any procedural irregularity of which the legal practitioner is aware.
> - PD 1.6.2 states that a legal practitioner acting for a wife petitioning for divorce, who discovered the husband had committed bigamy, was under no obligation to lay information of the offence before proceeding with the petition, but in the petition was bound to disclose all the facts within his or her knowledge.

**P** The responsibility for proper discovery falls not only on the litigant but on the lawyers; as officers of the court, solicitors owe a special duty to the court to explain to their clients what the obligations are, and a duty of involvement in and supervision of the disclosure process (*Teo Wai Cheong v Crédit Industriel et Commercial* [2013] 3 SLR 573 at [43]). The solicitor must (1) explain to the client the meaning of relevance and the disclosure obligations (at [45]); (2) not necessarily be satisfied by the client's statement that there are no documents, or no more than the client chooses to disclose: if there are reasonable grounds for supposing there are others, the solicitor must investigate, but need not go beyond reasonable steps to ascertain the truth (at [45]); and (3) review the documents disclosed to consider whether relevant documents may have been omitted (at [47]). Suppressing evidence requires (1) a wrongful non-disclosure, which depends on there being a duty to disclose in light of the nature and purpose of the proceedings and the applicable law, and (2) that the non-disclosure was deliberate, with a subjective intention to keep the information from the court or the other party (*Law Society of Singapore v de Souza Christopher James* [2023] SGHC 318 at [163]–[165], per Ramesh JAD); for a lawyer charged under r 10(3)(a), there must be some subjective intention or deliberateness to assist the suppression, and mere involvement in the client's failure to disclose is not enough (at [166], [169]; and for the majority at [75]–[76], [83], [88]). Some disclosure does not discharge the duty of candour where the nature and purpose of the proceedings and the law call for more (*Attorney-General v Shahira Banu d/o Khaja Moinudeen* [2024] SGHC 111 at [32]–[37]).

**A** Here [Lawyer] [only forwarded the rules of court to the client / accepted the client's list without review / knew of a damaging email that was left out]. On *Teo Wai Cheong* (1), forwarding the rules is not explaining relevance; on (2), [the gap in the email chain / the client's earlier mention of X] gave reasonable grounds to suspect more documents, so [Lawyer] had to investigate; on (3), [Lawyer] did not review what was produced. On *de Souza*, the client had a duty to disclose [the email], and leaving it out [knowingly] was deliberate, so [Lawyer] was a party to suppression under r 10(3)(a). [If the issue is the other side's crime: there is no duty to report it first (PD 1.6.2), but the facts must be disclosed in the proceedings.] **C** [Lawyer] breached r 10(3)(a) PCR, and should explain the obligations properly, review the documents and file a supplementary list, or cease to act if the client refuses.

*Source note: r 9(3), r 10(2)–(3) PCR p19–20; PD 1.6.2 p1; Teo Wai Cheong at [43], [45], [47] L4 p28 (SYL p6 gives [43]–[49]), forwarding-the-rules point Cyril §III.2.5 (secondary); de Souza two-limb test at [163]–[166], [169] (Ramesh JAD, concurring) and majority [75]–[76], [83], [88], read from the judgment on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026; SYL p6 gives [160]–[168], [207]); Shahira Banu at [32]–[37] SYL p6, Cyril §III.2.4 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-48 — Relationship with client or court affecting independence: r 11(2)
*Keywords: r 11(2), professional independence, family relationship, personal relationship, commercial relationship, relative, friend, judge, relationship with the court, appearance of bias, r 11(1), Then Khek Khoon, interest in fees*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] may appear given [the relationship with the client / the judge]. **C** [Lawyer] [may not] appear, because r 11(2) PCR forbids a lawyer to appear where a relationship with the client makes professional independence difficult to maintain, or where a relationship with the court might or might appear to prejudice the impartial administration of justice.
**R** A lawyer must not appear before a court where it
would be difficult to maintain professional independence because of any commercial, family,
personal or other relationship with the client, or where the impartial administration of
justice might or might appear to be prejudiced by the lawyer's relationship with the court or
any member of it (r 11(2)(a)–(b) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 11(1) PCR states the guiding principle that a legal practitioner's duty to advance the client's interests, and to present the client's best possible case, is subject to the legal practitioner's duties as an officer of the court and to assist in the administration of justice.
> - Rule 11(2) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must not appear before a court or tribunal in a matter where
>   - (a) it would be difficult for the legal practitioner to maintain the legal practitioner's professional independence by reason of any commercial, family, personal or other relationship between the legal practitioner and his or her client; or
>   - (b) the impartial administration of justice might or might appear to be prejudiced by reason of the legal practitioner's relationship with the court or tribunal or any member of the court or tribunal.

**P** The concern is that the lawyer's objectivity may be unconsciously compromised. A firm's ordinary interest in its own fees creates no such conflict: where clients sued to recover legal costs they had paid as damages, the firm was not restrained from acting, as the fees had been paid long before, were not contingent on recovery, and any question of overcharging was for the firm to advise on, not for the defendants to raise (*Then Khek Khoon v Arjun Permanand Samtani* [2012] 2 SLR 451 at [30]–[36]).

**A** Under r 11(2)(a), here [Lawyer] [is the client's spouse / a close friend / a shareholder in the client's business]. [Lawyer] would have to give detached advice on [settlement / weak points] and present the case objectively, which that relationship makes difficult; unlike *Then Khek Khoon*, the interest goes beyond ordinary fees. Under r 11(2)(b), [Lawyer] [is related to / a close friend of] the presiding [judge / arbitrator], so the other side may reasonably perceive bias; the test is whether justice might appear to be prejudiced, not whether it actually is. **C** [Lawyer] [may not] appear; the firm may brief another lawyer, and should explain why to the client.

*Source note: r 11(1)–(2) PCR p21; Then Khek Khoon at [30]–[36] SYL p6, facts L4 p36; rationale Cyril §III.3.1–3.2 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-49 — Dealing with witnesses: r 12
*Keywords: witness, r 12, witness coaching, witness preparation, cross-examination, during cross-examination, scandalous questions, vilify, allegation not put, interview other side's witness, witness payment, contingent, Perez De La Sala, Wong Sin Yee, PD 8.8.1, order to attend court, group preparation, own judgment*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] dealt properly with [witness]. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 12[(x)] PCR, which [forbids a lawyer to discuss the evidence with their own witness during that witness's cross-examination without the court's permission / forbids scandalous questions or questions intended to vilify, insult or annoy a witness / forbids payment to a witness contingent on the evidence or outcome].
**R** A lawyer must deal with witnesses consistently with the administration of justice
and exercise their own judgment on questions (r 12(1) PCR). Without the court's permission, a
lawyer must not discuss evidence with their own witness between the start and the end of that
witness's cross-examination (r 12(2)); must not make an allegation against a witness in
submissions without putting it to the witness in cross-examination (r 12(3)); must not suggest
a witness is guilty of an offence unless material and supported by reasonable grounds (r 12(4));
and must not ask scandalous questions or questions meant to vilify, insult or annoy (r 12(5)).
A lawyer may interview any witness, but must tell the other side if that witness has been
called by them (r 12(6)–(7)), and must not pay a witness contingent on the evidence or outcome
(r 12(8)).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 12(1) PCR states the guiding principles that
>   - (a) a legal practitioner must act consistently with the administration of justice when dealing with any witness, regardless of the effect of the witness's evidence; and
>   - (b) must exercise their own judgment as to the substance and form of questions put or statements made to a witness.
> - Rule 12(2) PCR states that a legal practitioner must not, except with the permission of a court or tribunal, interview or discuss, with a witness whom the legal practitioner has called, at any time after the start and before the end of the cross-examination of that witness, the evidence given or to be given by that witness or any other witness.
> - Rule 12(3) PCR states that a legal practitioner must not, by asserting in a statement to a court or tribunal, make any allegation against a witness whom the legal practitioner cross-examined or was given an opportunity to cross-examine, unless the witness was given an opportunity to answer the allegation during cross-examination.
> - Rule 12(4) PCR states that a legal practitioner must not suggest that a witness or any other person is guilty of any offence or conduct, or attribute to them any offence or conduct of which the client is accused, unless the suggestion or attribution relates to a matter in issue (including the credibility of the witness) which is material to the client's case and which appears to the legal practitioner to be supported by reasonable grounds.
> - Rule 12(5) PCR states that a legal practitioner must not make any statement, or ask any question, which is scandalous, is intended or calculated to vilify, insult or annoy a witness or any other person, or is otherwise an abuse of the function of the legal practitioner.
> - Rule 12(6) PCR states that a legal practitioner may interview and take statements from any witness (including a prospective witness) at any stage, whether or not that witness has been interviewed or called by another party or the prosecution; but r 12(7) PCR states that if the legal practitioner is aware that the witness has been called to appear in a court, or has been issued an order to attend court, by another party or the prosecution, the legal practitioner must inform that party's legal practitioner or the prosecution of the intention to interview or take statements from the witness.
> - Rule 12(8) PCR states that a legal practitioner
>   - (a) may pay, or offer to pay, a witness any disbursements and expenses which the witness is allowed or entitled to under the law; but
>   - (b) must not make, or offer to make, any payment to a witness which is contingent upon the nature of the evidence given or upon the outcome of a case.
> - PD 8.8.1 Part A adds that, before calling a witness, the lawyer must make clear that the lawyer is not personally responsible for those expenses.

**P** A witness's evidence must remain his or her own. A practice run is not wrong in itself, and the lawyer may point out contradictions with the witness's affidavit, tell the witness what questions opposing counsel may ask, and invite the witness to consider the true answer (*Ernest Ferdinand Perez De La Sala v Compania De Navegacion Palomar, SA* [2018] SGCA 16 at [136]). Three rules of thumb apply: (1) in preparing a witness, the lawyer must not allow anyone, including himself or herself, to supplant or supplement the witness's own evidence; (2) preparation must not be too lengthy or repetitive, as repetitive drilling is dangerous even if no one tells the witness to change the evidence; and (3) preparation must not be done in groups or together with other involved persons (at [137]–[140]). Breach may lead the court to give the testimony less or no weight, the rules are prophylactic and apply to innocent breaches, and intentional wrongdoing may lead to severe professional sanctions (at [137], [141]–[142]). Cross-examining an outrage-of-modesty victim on her breast size, to suggest the touching was accidental, was improper (*Law Society of Singapore v Wong Sin Yee* [2018] SGHC 196).

**A** Here [Lawyer] [rehearsed witnesses together and supplied the "right" answers: rules (1) and (3) in *Perez De La Sala* / spoke to the witness about the evidence over lunch during cross-examination without permission: r 12(2) / accused the witness in closing of lying about X without putting it to them: r 12(3) / suggested the witness committed the offence with no reasonable grounds: r 12(4) / asked degrading questions irrelevant to any issue, as in *Wong Sin Yee*: r 12(5) / interviewed the other side's witness without telling their lawyer: r 12(7) / offered a bonus if the client won: r 12(8)(b)]. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 12[(x)] PCR, which may be improper conduct under s 83(2)(b) LPA.

*Source note: r 12 PCR p22–23; PD 8.8.1 Part A p1; rule text L5 p3–10 (L5 says "leave" and "subpoena"; the 2022 text says "permission" and "order to attend court"); Perez De La Sala and Wong Sin Yee SYL p6; Perez De La Sala [136]–[142] read from the judgment on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026; Cyril's version of the three rules was inaccurate); Wong Sin Yee facts Cyril §III.4.4 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-50 — The lawyer's own undertakings to court; contempt and prejudicial publications
*Keywords: undertaking to court, r 13(4), r 13(5), solemn promise, honour, necessary, able to honour, contempt, publication, Facebook, pending appeal, r 13(6), Seow Francis, Marshall David, Naidu Priyalatha, Nalpon, forgery of court documents, GN 1.6.1, Ng Bock Hoh Dixon, Scheduled Conduct, report to Council, eight days*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] breached [an undertaking given to the court / the rule on publications about proceedings]. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 13(4) PCR, which requires a lawyer to honour every undertaking the lawyer gives to a court or tribunal [/ r 13(6) PCR, which forbids a lawyer to publish material about proceedings that amounts to contempt or is calculated to interfere with a fair trial or prejudice the administration of justice].
**R** A lawyer must honour
every undertaking given to a court and must not give one unless they believe it necessary and
know they can honour it (r 13(4)–(5) PCR). A lawyer must not publish, or help publish, material
about proceedings that amounts to contempt or is calculated to interfere with a fair trial or
prejudice the administration of justice (r 13(6) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 13(4) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must honour every undertaking given by the legal practitioner to a court or tribunal.
> - Rule 13(5) PCR states that a legal practitioner must not give an undertaking to a court or tribunal unless the legal practitioner
>   - (a) believes that the undertaking is necessary; and
>   - (b) knows (when giving the undertaking) that he or she is able to honour it.
> - Rule 13(6) PCR states that a legal practitioner must not publish, or take steps to facilitate the publication of, any material concerning any proceedings, whether on behalf of the client or otherwise, which
>   - (a) amounts to a contempt of court; or
>   - (b) is calculated to interfere with the fair trial of a case or to prejudice the administration of justice.
> - GN 1.6.1 para 3 defines "Scheduled Conduct" as any forgery, fabrication or alteration of court documents of the courts of Singapore or elsewhere, by a legal practitioner.
> - Para 4 recommends that a practitioner who knows or has reason to believe that another practitioner has committed Scheduled Conduct submit to the Council, as soon as practicable, a report with supporting documents giving particulars of the reporter, the subject and the conduct.
> - Para 6 states that the reporter should first write to the subject in confidence, stating the intention to report and inviting a written response within eight days, save where
>   - (a) delay in reporting, or
>   - (b) notifying the subject, is likely to adversely affect the due and proper administration of justice.
> - Para 7 states that the report must be made bona fide and not to secure any undue advantage for the reporter, the reporter's firm or clients, and para 8 states that where the report contains privileged information the reporter should seek and encourage the client's consent to disclosure.

**P** An undertaking is a solemn promise, not merely a contract, on which the court and others rely because it is given by a member of an honourable profession, and a deliberate breach is more serious than a negligent one (*Law Society of Singapore v Naidu Priyalatha* [2022] SGHC 224). A lawyer who undertook to the Attorney-General to hand over all relevant files, so the police did not search, but did not direct a search, was suspended for a year (*Re Seow Francis T* [1971–1973] SLR(R) 727); one who assured the Chief Justice that affidavits would not go to the press, then sent them two days later, was suspended for six months (*Re Marshall David* [1971–1973] SLR(R) 554). Posting a crucial point from a pending appeal on a Facebook group breached r 13(6) (*Law Society of Singapore v Nalpon Zero Geraldo Mario* [2022] SGHC 81). A lawyer who falsified a judgment, to placate a client rather than to deceive a court, was suspended for two years, as creating false court documents for any purpose shows a lack of respect for the court process (*Law Society of Singapore v Ng Bock Hoh Dixon* [2010] SGHC 69; [2010] 2 SLR 1000 at [3], [30]–[31], [37]).

**A** Here [Lawyer] undertook to [produce / hold / not publish] [X]. Under r 13(5)(a)–(b), [the undertaking was not necessary / Lawyer did not know, when giving it, that he could honour it because X was in the client's control]. Under r 13(4), [Lawyer] [did not check he could comply, as in *Seow Francis* / deliberately did the opposite, as in *Marshall David*], and a deliberate breach is more serious (*Naidu Priyalatha*). [Under r 13(6), the post about the pending [appeal / trial] was calculated to prejudice the administration of justice, as in *Nalpon*.] [If [Lawyer] learns that another lawyer forged a court document, GN 1.6.1 paras 4 and 6 recommend writing to that lawyer first, then reporting to the Council.] **C** [Lawyer] breached r 13[(4) / (6)] PCR and should remedy the breach at once and inform the court.

*Source note: r 13(4)–(6) PCR p24; GN 1.6.1 paras 3–8 p1; Seow Francis L5 p15; Marshall David L5 p16; Naidu Priyalatha SYL p7, solemn-promise and deliberate-breach points Cyril §III.5.2 (secondary; no pinpoint); Nalpon SYL p7, facts Cyril §III.5.4 (secondary); Ng Bock Hoh Dixon [2010] 2 SLR 1000 SYL p7, falsified-judgment facts and lack-of-respect point Cyril §III.5.4 (secondary); L5 p18 instead summarises the later striking-off case [2011] SGHC 242 and records the earlier two-year suspension for falsifying a judgment as [2010] SGHC 69 ([2010] SGHC 69 is reported at [2010] 2 SLR 1000, as [2011] SGHC 242 at [3] confirms; both read on eLitigation, 3 Nov 2026).*

### eth-w-0-51 — Defending in criminal proceedings: r 14
*Keywords: criminal defence, accused, r 14, every reasonable defence, bail, surety, personal opinion of guilt, absent from trial, confession, inconsistent evidence, plead guilty, give evidence, abide by decision, abscond, mitigation, antecedents, Iskandar bin Rahmat, r 10(4), false evidence, co-accused, joint defence, acting for two accused, third party pays fees, paymaster, who is paying, employer pays, referred by co-accused, r 39(2)*

**I** The issue is how [Lawyer] must conduct [accused]'s defence given [confession / bail request / wish to testify]. **C** [Lawyer] [may continue, but must not adduce evidence or make submissions inconsistent with the confession, under r 14(4) PCR / must not provide bail or surety, under r 14(3)(a) PCR].
**R**
Defence counsel has a fundamental duty to assist in the administration of justice and must
pursue every reasonable defence and raise every favourable factor in accordance with law
(r 14(1)–(2) PCR). Counsel must not stand surety or bail, express or act on a personal opinion
of guilt, or be absent from trial without reasonable grounds, and must keep assisting after
conviction (r 14(3)(a)–(e) PCR). After a confession, counsel may continue but must not adduce
evidence or make submissions inconsistent with it (r 14(4) PCR). Counsel must advise on plea
and on whether to give evidence, and must abide by the accused's decision (r 14(5) PCR). If the
accused absconds, counsel may apply to be discharged or carry on as if the accused chose silence
(r 14(6)). Mitigation must not be scandalous (r 14(7)); counsel need not disclose antecedents
but must not contradict them (r 14(8)).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 14(1) PCR states the guiding principles that a legal practitioner who represents an accused person
>   - (a) is under a fundamental duty to assist in the administration of justice; and
>   - (b) must present the accused's case consistently with the aims of the Rules and comply with the constitutional, evidential and procedural rules of a criminal trial.
> - Rule 14(2) PCR states that the legal practitioner must pursue every reasonable defence, and raise every favourable factor, on behalf of the accused in accordance with law.
> - Rule 14(3) PCR states that the legal practitioner
>   - (a) must not provide any surety or bail for the accused;
>   - (b) must not express a personal opinion as to whether the accused is guilty, or allow that opinion to affect the professional assessment of the facts or the law, the conduct of the proceedings, or the duty to the accused or the court;
>   - (c) must not be absent from a trial or hearing without reasonable grounds;
>   - (d) must, if absent,
>     - (i) communicate the grounds for the absence to the accused before the trial or hearing; and
>     - (ii) make reasonable efforts to arrange for another legal practitioner to take over; and
>   - (e) must continue to reasonably assist the accused after conviction and sentence.
> - Rule 14(4) PCR states that where the accused confesses to any offence to the legal practitioner, the legal practitioner
>   - (a) may continue to represent the accused; but
>   - (b) must not adduce any evidence or make any submission which is inconsistent with the confession.
> - Rule 14(5) PCR states that the legal practitioner
>   - (a) must advise the accused on
>     - (i) whether to plead guilty, or to claim trial, to a charge; and
>     - (ii) whether to give evidence or to remain silent; and
>   - (b) must abide by the accused's decision.
> - Rule 14(6) PCR states that if the accused absconds, the legal practitioner may
>   - (a) apply to be discharged; or
>   - (b) conduct the defence on the basis of instructions received as if the accused were present but elected to remain silent.
> - Rule 14(7) PCR states that in a plea in mitigation the legal practitioner must not make any allegation which is scandalous or is intended or calculated to vilify, insult or annoy any person.
> - Rule 14(8) PCR states that the legal practitioner is not required to disclose any previous conviction or antecedent of the accused, but must not adduce any evidence or make any submission inconsistent with it.
> - Where the accused insists on giving evidence the lawyer knows is false, r 10(4) PCR states that the lawyer must
>   - (a) cease to act; or
>   - (b) if continuing, conduct the case in a manner that does not perpetuate the falsehood.
> - Rule 39(2) PCR states that where there is reason to believe that a client is referred to a legal practitioner or law practice by any other person, the legal practitioner or law practice
>   - (a) must maintain the independence and integrity of the legal profession, by not permitting the referror to undermine the professional independence of the legal practitioner or law practice;
>   - (c) must not allow the referral to affect in any way the advice given to the client;
>   - (d) must advise the client impartially and independently;
>   - (e) must ensure that any wish to avoid offending the referror does not affect in any way the advice given to the client; and
>   - (g) must communicate directly with the client to obtain or confirm instructions when providing advice and at all appropriate stages of the transaction.

**P** Defence counsel is not at fault for failing to run a defence (there, diminished responsibility) that the accused's instructions never disclosed facts to support (*Iskandar bin Rahmat v Law Society of Singapore* [2020] SGHC 40 at [60], [80]–[85]).

**A** Here [accused] [confessed to the offence / asked counsel to stand bail / insists on denying the offence on oath / has antecedents]. Under r 14(4), after the confession counsel can still test whether the prosecution proves each element, but cannot call evidence or submit that the accused did not do it. Under r 14(5), counsel must advise on plea and on giving evidence, then abide by the accused's choice; if the accused will testify to something counsel knows is false, r 10(4) applies. [Under r 14(3)(a), counsel cannot stand bail however sympathetic. Under r 14(8), counsel need not reveal antecedents but cannot say the accused is of good character.] Under r 14(2) and *Iskandar*, counsel must run every reasonable defence the instructions support. Co-accused and third-party payer: if [co-accused / employer] [pays [accused]'s fees / brought him to the firm], the client is still [accused] alone. Under r 39(2)(a), (c)–(e) and (g) the referral and any wish not to offend the payer must not affect the advice, and counsel must take instructions directly from [accused]. Acting for both co-accused needs the r 20(3) steps before accepting; once their accounts diverge (one blames the other, or may plead and give evidence against the other), counsel must cease for both, keeping one only with the other's written consent under r 20(7). If the firm wants to keep the payer's business, that is its own interest under r 22(2). **C** [Lawyer] [must not stand bail / may continue but only by testing the prosecution case / must withdraw without revealing the confession].

*Source note: r 14 PCR p24–26; r 10(4) PCR p20–21; r 20(3), (7) PCR p35–38; r 22(2) PCR p41; r 39(2) PCR p61–62; Iskandar at [60], [80]–[85] SYL p7, facts Cyril §III.6.1 (secondary); rule text L5 p20–27.*

### eth-w-0-52 — Prosecuting: r 15
*Keywords: prosecution, prosecutor, r 15, fairly and impartially, disclose witnesses, inconsistent prior statement, antecedents, sentencing precedents, unrepresented accused, mitigating circumstances, other pending proceedings, assist court, Parti Liyani, ministers of justice, apparent error*

**I** The issue is whether [Prosecutor] met the duties of a prosecutor. **C** [Prosecutor] breached r 15[(x)] PCR, which [requires a prosecutor to present the evidence fairly and impartially / to tell the defence of a witness who will not be called or of a substantial conflict with a prior statement / to draw the court's attention to any apparent error].
**R** A prosecutor has a fundamental duty to assist in the administration of
justice and must present the evidence fairly and impartially, without malice, fear or favour
(r 15(1)–(2) PCR). Before closing the prosecution case, the prosecutor must tell the defence of
any person who may give relevant evidence but will not be called, and of any substantial
conflict between a prosecution witness's evidence and their prior statement (r 15(3)). After
conviction, the prosecutor must tell the court of known antecedents, relevant sentencing
factors and precedents, and, for an unrepresented accused, known mitigating circumstances
(r 15(4)). The prosecutor must not mention other pending proceedings save on bail, adjournment
or remand, in answer to an assertion that there are none, or where admissible (r 15(5)), and
must draw the court's attention to apparent errors, omissions or irregularities (r 15(6)).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 15(1) PCR states the guiding principles that a legal practitioner who prosecutes
>   - (a) is under a fundamental duty to assist in the administration of justice; and
>   - (b) must present the case consistently with the aims of the Rules and comply with the constitutional, evidential and procedural rules of a criminal trial.
> - Rule 15(2) PCR states that a legal practitioner who represents the prosecution must present the evidence against an accused person fairly and impartially, and without malice, fear or favour, in accordance with law.
> - Rule 15(3) PCR states that the prosecutor must, within a reasonable time before the close of the prosecution's case, inform the accused or the accused's legal practitioner of
>   - (a) the identity, and the location (if known), of any person who may be able to give relevant evidence, but will not be called as a prosecution witness; and
>   - (b) any substantial conflict between the evidence given by a prosecution witness on a material issue and any prior statement provided by that witness.
> - Rule 15(4) PCR states that the prosecutor must, within a reasonable time after conviction but before sentencing, inform the court of
>   - (a) any previous conviction or antecedent of the accused that the prosecution knows of;
>   - (b) any relevant factors, and any known precedents, relating to the sentence; and
>   - (c) if the accused is unrepresented, any known mitigating circumstances.
> - Rule 15(5) PCR states that the prosecutor must not inform the court of any other or pending proceedings against the accused, except
>   - (a) in relation to an application for an adjournment, remand or bail;
>   - (b) in response to an assertion that there are no other or pending proceedings; or
>   - (c) where evidence of the other or pending proceedings would otherwise be admissible under any law or rule.
> - Rule 15(6) PCR states that the prosecutor must assist the court at all times before the conclusion of a trial, including by drawing the court's attention to any apparent error (whether of fact or of law), any apparent omission of fact, and any procedural irregularity, which in the prosecutor's opinion ought to be corrected.

**P** The Prosecution is under a fundamental duty to assist in the administration of justice and must present the evidence fairly; under r 15(6) it was incumbent on the DPPs to flag a known problem with an exhibit (*Re Parti Liyani* [2020] 5 SLR 1080 at [38]). Prosecutors are "ministers of justice" who must act in the public interest, need not be strictly adversarial, and must be willing to disclose all relevant material even if unhelpful to their case (at [39]).

**A** Here [Prosecutor] [did not disclose a witness whose account favoured the accused: r 15(3)(a) / let the court rely on a witness's evidence that conflicted on a material issue with the witness's earlier statement without telling the defence: r 15(3)(b) / mentioned other pending charges at trial, which none of the r 15(5)(a)–(c) exceptions covers / did not tell the court of a known problem with an exhibit, as in *Parti Liyani*: r 15(6) / did not tell the court of mitigating circumstances for an unrepresented accused: r 15(4)(c)]. As a minister of justice, [Prosecutor] could not hold this back because it was unhelpful to the prosecution (*Parti Liyani* at [39]). **C** [Prosecutor] breached r 15[(x)] PCR.

*Source note: r 15 PCR p27–28; rule text L5 p29–34; Parti Liyani at [38]–[39] read from the judgment ([2020] SGHC 227) on eLitigation, 3 Nov 2026; SYL p7.*

---

## The lawyer as witness

### eth-w-0-22 — Lawyer will be a material witness: r 11(3)(a)
*Keywords: lawyer as witness, material witness, contested issue, r 11(3), PD 1.7.1, discharge, attendance notes, representations, drafted the agreement, Ho Kon Kim, material and disputed question of fact, formal or undisputed facts, appellate tribunal, r 26(6)*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] (and [associate]) may act when they are likely to give evidence. **C** [Lawyer] may not act, because r 11(3)(a) PCR forbids a lawyer who will be required to give evidence material to a contested issue to accept instructions from any party, and requires a lawyer already acting to discharge themselves or apply to be discharged.
**R** Where it is known or appears that a lawyer
will be required to give evidence material to a contested issue, the lawyer must not
accept instructions from any party and, if already acting, must discharge themselves or
apply to be discharged (r 11(3)(a) PCR). A lawyer should not act where they have reason to
believe they will be a witness on a material and disputed fact, but may swear an
affidavit on formal or undisputed facts (PD 1.7.1).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 11(3) PCR applies, which states that in any case where it is known or it appears that a legal practitioner will be required to give evidence which is material to the determination of any contested issue before a court or tribunal,
>   - (a) the legal practitioner
>     - (i) must not accept instructions from any party to that case; and
>     - (ii) must, if the legal practitioner was acting for any party to that case, discharge himself or herself, or, where the legal practitioner has represented that party in any proceedings relating to that case, apply to be discharged, from acting further for that party.
> - PD 1.7.1 states that a legal practitioner shall not represent any party in any case in which he or she has reason to believe that he or she will be a witness in respect of a material and disputed question of fact, and if while appearing it becomes apparent that he or she will be such a witness, should discharge himself or herself and take all reasonable steps to ensure that the client's interests are not jeopardised; that a legal practitioner shall not appear before an appellate tribunal if, in the case under appeal, he or she has been a witness on a material and disputed question of fact in the court below; and that nothing in the PD prevents a legal practitioner from swearing an affidavit as to formal or undisputed facts in matters in which he or she acts or appears. On withdrawal, r 26(6) PCR states that the legal practitioner must
>   - (a) take reasonable care to avoid foreseeable harm to the client, including, where the circumstances permit,
>     - (i) by giving reasonable notice of the withdrawal;
>     - (ii) by giving the client a reasonable amount of time to engage another legal practitioner; and
>     - (iii) by cooperating with the client's new legal practitioner; and
>   - (b) abide by the client's decision on whether, and whom, to appoint to take over.

**P** The rationale is that a witness has a paramount duty to tell the truth, the whole truth and nothing but the truth, while a lawyer has a duty to present the case in the client's best interests, and a lawyer should not be placed where those duties could conflict; the duty to advance the client's case is subject to the duties as an officer of the court (r 11(1) PCR). Where the firm's conveyancing partner would be a material witness in a claim arising from the conveyancing transaction, he should not have acted (*Ho Kon Kim v Betsy Lim Gek Kim* [2001] SGCA 64 at [61]–[63]).

**A** First, is it known or does it appear that [Lawyer] will be required to give evidence? Here [the other side pleads that Lawyer said X at the meeting / Lawyer's attendance notes are the client's key evidence / the opponent intends to call Lawyer]. Second, is the evidence material to a contested issue? What [Lawyer] said or recorded goes to [the misrepresentation / the terms agreed], which is disputed on the pleadings and which only [Lawyer] and [associate] can speak to. An opponent's mere assertion that the lawyer will be a witness is not enough, but here the dispute is on the pleadings. It is not a formal or undisputed fact on which PD 1.7.1 would allow an affidavit. [If already acting in the proceedings, Lawyer must apply to be discharged (r 11(3)(a)(ii)).] **C** [Lawyer] and [associate] may not act; they must decline or discharge themselves, with reasonable notice and care for the client (r 26(6) PCR).

*Source note: r 11(1), (3) PCR p21–22; PD 1.7.1 p1; r 26(6) PCR p50; Ho Kon Kim at [61]–[63] SYL p6 ([63] L4 p35), facts L4 p35; rationale L4 p34. Tested PYP22 (a), PYP23 Q2.1.*

### eth-w-0-23 — Can a colleague in the same firm take over or act instead? r 11(3)(b)
*Keywords: r 11(3)(b), take over, take over conduct, taking over, colleague takes over, another lawyer in the firm, same firm, law practice may continue, continue to act, lawyer as witness, material witness, colleague, partner takes over, prejudice the administration of justice, firm's own conduct in issue, Ho Kon Kim, r 22, r 22(2), r 22(4), law practice's own interest, informed consent in writing, independent legal advice*

**I** The issue is whether [Firm] may continue to act through [colleague] once [Lawyer] steps aside. **C** [Firm] [may / may not] continue to act, because r 11(3)(b) PCR allows the law practice or a member of it to represent the party unless doing so would prejudice the administration of justice.
**R** Even where a lawyer is a
material witness, the firm or a member of it may represent the party unless doing so
would prejudice the administration of justice (r 11(3)(b) PCR). Separately, the firm must
not act where its own interests conflict with the client's (r 22(2), (4) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 11(3) PCR applies, which states that where it is known or it appears that a legal practitioner will be required to give evidence material to the determination of a contested issue, the legal practitioner must not act, but (b) the law practice in which the legal practitioner practises, or a member of that law practice, may represent or continue to represent any party to that case, unless doing so would prejudice the administration of justice.
> - Separately, r 22(2) PCR states that, except as otherwise permitted by the rule, a legal practitioner or law practice must not act for a client if there is, or may reasonably be expected to be, a conflict between
>   - (a) the duty to serve the best interests of the client; and
>   - (b) the interests of the legal practitioner or law practice.
> - Rule 22(4) PCR states that where a law practice has an interest in any matter entrusted to it by its client,
>   - (a) where the interest is adverse to the client's interests, the law practice must withdraw unless
>     - (i) it makes a full and frank disclosure of the adverse interest to the client;
>     - (ii) it advises the client to obtain independent legal advice;
>     - (iii) if the client does not obtain independent legal advice, it ensures that the client is not under an impression that the law practice is protecting the client's interests; and
>     - (iv) despite (i) and (ii), the client gives informed consent in writing to the law practice acting or continuing to act; or
>   - (b) in any other case, the law practice must withdraw unless
>     - (i) it makes a full and frank disclosure of the interest to the client; and
>     - (ii) despite that, the client gives informed consent in writing.

**P** In *Ho Kon Kim v Betsy Lim Gek Kim* [2001] SGCA 64 at [61]–[63], neither the conveyancing partner nor the litigation partner should have acted: first, the conveyancing partner would be a material witness; second, the partners had an interest in the case, as it was their firm that had acted in the property transaction.

**A** The question under r 11(3)(b) is whether [colleague] acting would prejudice the administration of justice. Weigh [colleague]'s independence from the witness: [never involved in the transaction / was the witness's trainee and is junior to them / small two-partner firm]. Weigh whether the firm's own conduct is attacked: if the defence says the firm misled the other party, [colleague] would be cross-examining or relying on a partner's evidence about the firm's own conduct, with an interest in vindicating the firm. That risks the appearance of justice, as in *Ho Kon Kim*, and gives the firm an interest adverse to the client under r 22(2) and r 22(4)(a), so the firm could act only if it made full and frank disclosure, advised the client to get independent legal advice, ensured that the client, if not independently advised, was not under the impression that the firm was protecting the client's interests, and obtained the client's informed consent in writing. **C** On balance [Firm] [may not continue, and the client should be advised to instruct another firm / may continue only with full and frank disclosure, advice to obtain independent legal advice, steps to ensure that the client, if not independently advised, is not under the impression that the firm is protecting the client's interests, informed consent in writing under r 22(4)(a)(i)–(iv), and strict separation from [Lawyer]].

*Source note: r 11(3)(b) PCR p22; r 22(2), (4) PCR p41–42; Ho Kon Kim at [61]–[63] SYL p6, facts and two reasons at [63] L4 p35; r 11(3)(b) point L4 p34. No course source states factors for "prejudice the administration of justice"; the weighing in A is application, not a test. Tested PYP22 (b), PYP23 Q2.2.*

## Other lawyers and other persons

### eth-w-0-24 — Undertakings to other lawyers
*Keywords: undertaking, solicitor's undertaking, r 7(6), r 7(7), honour, necessary, able to honour, lien, Naidu Priyalatha, solemn promise, condition outside control, third party, Arjan Chotrani Bisham, deliberate breach*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] breached an undertaking given to [other lawyer]. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 7(6) PCR, which requires a legal practitioner to honour every undertaking the legal practitioner gives to another legal practitioner.
**R** A lawyer must honour every undertaking given to
another lawyer, and must not give one unless they believe it is necessary and know, when
giving it, that they can honour it (r 7(6)–(7) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 7(6) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must honour every undertaking the legal practitioner gives to another legal practitioner.
> - Rule 7(7) PCR states that a legal practitioner (A) must not give an undertaking to another legal practitioner unless
>   - (a) A believes that the undertaking is necessary; and
>   - (b) A knows (when giving the undertaking) that A is able to honour the undertaking.
> - Both are read with the guiding principle in r 7(1)(b) PCR, that a legal practitioner must deal with another legal practitioner in good faith and in a manner which is dignified and courteous.

**P** An undertaking by one lawyer to another is a solemn promise, not merely an agreement or contract, on which the other lawyer may rely by making a concession, agreeing to a request or taking or not taking a step (*Law Society of Singapore v Naidu Priyalatha* [2022] SGHC 224). A lawyer should therefore never give an undertaking whose fulfilment depends on a third party or on any other condition outside the lawyer's control. Breach of an undertaking is grave (*Law Society v Arjan Chotrani Bisham* [2001] SGHC 24), and a deliberate breach is more serious than a negligent one: in *Naidu Priyalatha* a lawyer who undertook to release a cashier's order to her client only once a full settlement was agreed, but let it be deposited into the client's account though no settlement was reached, was suspended for three months. **A** Undertaking: here [Lawyer] undertook to [hold the original documents subject to the lien / pay costs on completion], and [the other lawyer] [released documents / took a step] in reliance. Rule 7(7)(a)–(b): did [Lawyer] believe it necessary, and know when giving it that [he / she] could honour it, or did performance depend on [the client / a bank / a third party]? An undertaking that depended on the client's later co-operation breached r 7(7)(b) when it was given. Rule 7(6): [Lawyer] then [released the documents / failed to pay] without protecting [the other lawyer]'s position. That the client later instructed otherwise, or changed lawyers, does not release the undertaking: [Lawyer] had to get the new lawyer's matching undertaking first, or refuse. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 7(6) [and r 7(7)], and should immediately remedy it by retrieving the documents or securing the costs.

*Source note: r 7(1)(b), (6)–(7) PCR p13–14; "solemn promise", reliance, conditions outside the lawyer's control and Arjan Chotrani Bisham L6 p15; Naidu Priyalatha SYL p7, p8 (no pinpoint in sources); "not merely an agreement or contract", deliberate worse than negligent, facts and three-month suspension Cyril §IV.1.4 (secondary). Tested PYP22 (c).*

### eth-w-0-25 — Dealing with an unrepresented person
*Keywords: unrepresented, litigant-in-person, r 8(2), r 8(1)(c), no legal advice, independent legal advice, impression interests protected, "you don't need a lawyer", Ganesan Krishnan, Ahmad Khalis, implied retainer, power of attorney*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] dealt properly with [X], who had no lawyer. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 8(2) PCR, which requires a legal practitioner dealing for a client with an unrepresented person to decline to give that person legal advice and to take reasonable steps so the person does not think the legal practitioner protects their interests.
**R** When dealing for the client with someone not
represented by a lawyer, a lawyer must decline to give that person any legal advice other
than to get independent legal advice, if the lawyer knows or ought to know their
interests are or may be adverse to the client's, and must take reasonable steps so that
the person is not under the impression that the lawyer is protecting their interests
(r 8(2)(a)–(b) PCR). The lawyer must treat unrepresented persons fairly (r 8(1)(c) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 8(2) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner (A), when dealing on behalf of his or her client with any person who is not represented by another legal practitioner,
>   - (a) must decline to give to the person any legal advice (other than advice to obtain independent legal advice), if A knows or ought reasonably to know that the interests of the person are adverse, or potentially adverse, to the interests of A's client; and
>   - (b) must take reasonable steps to ensure that the person is not under the impression that the person's interests are protected by A.
> - It is read with the guiding principle in r 8(1)(c) PCR, that a legal practitioner must treat with fairness any person who is not represented by another legal practitioner.

**P** Where a lawyer acts for one party and other parties may reasonably believe the lawyer is advising or protecting them, the lawyer must clearly disabuse them of that impression and, if their interests are adverse or potentially adverse, decline to advise them and tell them to get independent legal advice; failing to tell the donors of a power of attorney, who were not clients, to get independent advice exposed them to significant risk (*Law Society of Singapore v Ganesan Krishnan* [2003] 2 SLR(R) 251). A lawyer introduced to the other beneficiaries as a friend there to "help", who reassured them that his client as sole administrator could not deal with the estate property without their authorisation, was held to have an implied retainer with them and to owe them a duty to keep them informed and explain the documents (*Law Society of Singapore v Ahmad Khalis bin Abdul Ghani* [2006] 4 SLR(R) 308 at [74]). **A** Limb (a): [X] was [the counterparty to the settlement / the other party to the agreement / a beneficiary], whose interests [Lawyer] knew or ought reasonably to have known were adverse or potentially adverse. Statements such as "you do not need a lawyer" or "the terms are fair" are legal advice other than advice to obtain independent legal advice. Limb (b): saying "I am looking after everyone's interests", or letting [X] sign without explanation, is the opposite of reasonable steps to dispel the impression, and a clause reciting that [X] "had the opportunity to obtain independent advice" does not cure oral assurances. If [Lawyer] gave them, they may also found an implied retainer, bringing the multiple-client conflict rules into play. **C** If the allegations are true, [Lawyer] breached r 8(2)(a) and (b), and the dispute makes [Lawyer] a likely witness.

*Source note: r 8(1)(c), (2) PCR p15; Ganesan Krishnan L2 p13, SYL p3; Ahmad Khalis at [74] SYL p3, L2 p12, facts L7 p24–25. Tested PYP22 (a), PYP23 Q2.1.*

### eth-w-0-53 — Courtesy and fairness to other lawyers
*Keywords: courtesy, fairness, r 7(1), r 7(2), r 27, good faith, offensive letters, disparaging remarks, social media, PD 8.5.9, draft documents, PD 8.5.3, professional conference, PD 8.5.7, Ravi s/o Madasamy 2023, Terence Tan Bian Chye, China Insurance, disagree without being disagreeable, K Jayakumar Naidu*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] treated [other lawyer] with courtesy and fairness. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 7(2) PCR, which requires a legal practitioner to treat other legal practitioners with courtesy and fairness.
**R** A lawyer must accord other lawyers proper respect, deal with
them in good faith and in a dignified and courteous way so matters can be resolved in the
clients' best interests, and avoid conduct that harms the profession's standing (r 7(1)(a)–(c)
PCR); must treat other lawyers with courtesy and fairness (r 7(2)); and, in court proceedings,
must relate to other lawyers in a way that promotes the administration of justice (r 27 PCR).
A lawyer must observe good manners towards other lawyers and their staff however bitter the
clients' dispute, and must not write offensive letters (PD 8.5.9). Amendments to drafts must
be shown clearly (PD 8.5.3), and a lawyer seeking a conference goes to the other lawyer's
office (PD 8.5.7).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 7(2) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must treat other legal practitioners with courtesy and fairness.
> - It is guided by r 7(1) PCR:
>   - (a) a legal practitioner must always accord to another legal practitioner the proper respect due to the latter as a member of a noble and honourable profession;
>   - (b) a legal practitioner must deal with another legal practitioner in good faith and in a manner which is dignified and courteous, so that the matters on which they have been instructed can be properly and satisfactorily concluded or resolved in the best interests of their respective clients; and
>   - (c) a legal practitioner must not deal with another legal practitioner in any manner that may adversely affect the reputation and good standing of the legal profession or the practice of law in Singapore.
> - In court proceedings r 27 PCR adds that
>   - (a) a legal practitioner involved in any court proceedings (whether as a counsel, a witness or otherwise) must relate to another legal practitioner in a manner which promotes the administration of justice; and
>   - (b) a legal practitioner is responsible to an opposing legal practitioner for maintaining due process and promoting the administration of justice.
> - PD 8.5.9 states that a legal practitioner must at all times maintain his or her personal integrity and observe the requirements of good manners and courtesy towards other members of the profession or their staff, no matter how bitter the feelings between clients; must not behave in a manner which is acrimonious or offensive or otherwise inconsistent with his or her position as a legal practitioner; and must not write offensive letters to members of the profession, whatever the situation between the respective clients.
