> - PD 8.5.3 states that a legal practitioner shall comply with the requirements of common courtesy in dealing with draft documents; amendments should be made on the draft clearly showing the original and the amendment (one method being to underline additions and cross out deletions); and this is not to be circumvented by delivering the amended version to the client to pass on to the other side.
> - PD 8.5.7 states that a legal practitioner seeking a professional conference with a colleague shall, unless otherwise agreed, call on the legal practitioner from whom it is sought, whether or not the one seeking it is senior in call.

**P** The duty does not conflict with the adversarial system, since "one can disagree and yet not be disagreeable" (*China Insurance Co (Singapore) Pte Ltd v Liberty Insurance Pte Ltd* [2005] 2 SLR(R) 509 at [64]). Letters accusing opposing counsel of deception and of misleading the court and threatening to report him, together with some 27 letters to the other side alleging crimes and threatening criminal proceedings, were misconduct (*Law Society of Singapore v Terence Tan Bian Chye* [2007] SGDSC 10); so was a public Facebook post saying opposing counsel needed psychiatric treatment (*Law Society of Singapore v Ravi s/o Madasamy* [2023] SGHC 65). Contrast a lawyer who refused to deal with another lawyer out of hurt feelings, to his own client's detriment: that was an abdication of responsibility to the client rather than discourtesy (*Law Society of Singapore v K Jayakumar Naidu* [2012] SGHC 200 at [75], [77]). **A** Here [Lawyer] [wrote that opposing counsel was "dishonest" / posted about them online / sent a clean draft hiding changes / insisted a colleague come to [Lawyer]'s office for a conference]. Test each act against r 7(1)(a)–(c) and the PDs: [the letter is acrimonious and offensive, contrary to PD 8.5.9; the clean draft hides the amendments, contrary to PD 8.5.3; the conference demand is contrary to PD 8.5.7]. Zeal for the client, or bitterness between the clients, does not justify it. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 7(2) [and PD 8.5.9 / 8.5.3], which may be misconduct unbefitting under s 83(2)(h) LPA.

*Source note: r 7(1)–(2) PCR p13; r 27 PCR p51; PD 8.5.9, 8.5.3, 8.5.7 (each p1); China Insurance L6 p8; Terence Tan L6 p10 (cited there as [2007] SGDC 10), L7 p30, SYL p8; Ravi 2023 SYL p8, facts Cyril §IV.1.2 (secondary); L6 p10 lists instead Ravi s/o Madasamy [2015] SGHC 120, facts not in sources; K Jayakumar Naidu L6 p11; s 83(2)(h) LPA p218.*

### eth-w-0-54 — Contacting a person who has a lawyer: r 7(3)
*Keywords: represented person, r 7(3), Rai Vijay Kumar, potential witnesses, subpoena, communicate directly, other side's client, consent of their lawyer, notify, severe prejudice, authorised by law, service on represented party, PD 8.5.10, three working days, corporate officers*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] could communicate directly with [X], who is represented by [other lawyer]. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 7(3) PCR, which forbids a legal practitioner to communicate about a matter with a person the legal practitioner knows is represented in it by another legal practitioner, unless one of four exceptions applies.
**R** A lawyer must not communicate about a
matter with a person the lawyer knows is represented in it by another lawyer, unless the other
lawyer consents expressly or impliedly; or there is a reasonable basis to communicate directly,
the other lawyer was notified first and did not respond within a reasonable time; or the
client's interests would be severely prejudiced by delay; or the law or a court authorises it
(r 7(3)(a)–(d) PCR). Personal service on a represented party is allowed only after asking
their lawyer whether they will accept service and getting no confirmation within three working
days, and contact must be kept to what service needs (PD 8.5.10).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 7(3) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner (A) must not communicate, about a matter in respect of which A represents a client, with any person whom A knows to be represented in the matter by another legal practitioner (B) unless
>   - (a) A has the prior consent (whether express or implied) of B to communicate directly with that person;
>   - (b) both of the following apply:
>     - (i) there is a reasonable basis for A to communicate directly with that person; and
>     - (ii) prior to the communication, A has taken reasonable steps to notify B of A's intention to communicate directly with that person, but B does not respond within a reasonable time after A's notification;
>   - (c) the interests of A's client will be severely prejudiced if the communication is delayed; or
>   - (d) A is authorised to communicate directly with that person by law or by an order of a court or tribunal.
> - The rule extends to officers of a corporate client. On service, PD 8.5.10 Part B para 1 states that where A has been in communication with B about the client's actual or contemplated proceedings, and the Rules of Court or other law require personal service but permit service on B as an alternative, A must not serve the documents on the represented party personally unless
>   - (i) A has enquired with B whether B has instructions to accept service; and
>   - (ii) B does not confirm within three working days (excluding a Saturday, Sunday or public holiday), or such other period as agreed, that B has those instructions; and where A then serves personally, A must inform B in writing without delay.
> - Para 2 states that in all cases of personal service A must
>   - (a) limit communication with the party (including persons associated with the party) to only such communication as is necessary to effect service; and
>   - (b) comply with A's ethical duties to the party and to B under r 7 and r 8 PCR.
> - PD 8.5.10 is subject to any directions of the court and to r 7(3) PCR.

**P** Writing directly to potential witnesses known to be represented breached r 7(3), even though an associate sent the letters with the lawyer's knowledge; a subpoena is not "authorisation by law" for direct contact, and telling them they must give a statement and could not consult their insurers' lawyers took unfair advantage under r 8(3) (*Rai Vijay Kumar v Law Society of Singapore* [2025] SGHC 159 at [29]–[30], [38], [40]–[42]). **A** Knowledge: [Lawyer] knew [X] had instructed [Firm Y] [from correspondence / a notice of appointment]. Exception (a): Y gave no consent, express or implied. Exception (b): [Lawyer] [had no reasonable basis / did not first notify Y and wait a reasonable time]. Exception (c): no urgency existed such that delay would severely prejudice the client. Exception (d): no statute or order authorised direct contact, and [a subpoena / a right to serve documents] does not. Here [Lawyer] [called / emailed / met] [X] about the [settlement]; if [Lawyer] was serving documents, did [Lawyer] first ask Y and wait three working days, and keep contact to what service needed? **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 7(3), and should stop, tell [Firm Y], and route all further communication through it.

*Source note: r 7(3) PCR p13–14; extension to officers of a corporate client L6 p14; PD 8.5.10 Part B paras 1–2 pp1–3; Rai Vijay Kumar read from the judgment on eLitigation, 3 Nov 2026 (SYL p9); summary Cyril §IV.1.3.5, 1.3.8 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-55 — Second opinions; taking over another lawyer's matter
*Keywords: second opinion, r 7(4), take over, retainer determined, PD 7.3.3, lawyer on record, PD 7.3.2, advising a friend, PD 8.5.2, gross discourtesy, former client, PD 8.3.1, word is bond, poach client, influence client to terminate*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] may [give a second opinion to / take over from] [Firm Y]'s client. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 7(4) PCR, which allows a legal practitioner to give a second opinion to another legal practitioner's client but forbids improperly seeking to influence the client to end that relationship.
**R** A lawyer may give a second opinion to another lawyer's
client, with or without that lawyer's knowledge, but must not improperly seek to influence the
client to end that relationship (r 7(4) PCR). A lawyer should not act in place of another lawyer
known to be retained until the client has ended that retainer (PD 7.3.3), nor act where another
lawyer is on the record (PD 7.3.2). Asked informally by a friend who has a lawyer, a lawyer
must not comment on that lawyer's advice, which would be a gross discourtesy, though they may
suggest the friend raise points with their own lawyer (PD 8.5.2). The former lawyer should
release the file on the new lawyer's word and must not contact the former client to check
(PD 8.3.1).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 7(4) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner (A) may give a second opinion to a client of another legal practitioner (B), with or without the knowledge of B, but must not improperly seek to influence the client to terminate the relationship between the client and B.
> - PD 7.3.3 adds that a legal practitioner should not act in a matter in place of another legal practitioner whom he or she knows has been retained until that retainer has been determined by the client.
> - PD 7.3.2 states that if in any civil proceeding the name of any legal practitioner appears on the record for any party, no other legal practitioner shall knowingly agree to act or continue to act for that party in that proceeding, unless he or she has, in ignorance that the name so appears on the record, already agreed to act and is unable by reason of circumstances or urgency or the like to refuse to act further without exposing himself or herself to a charge of breach of professional duty.
> - PD 8.5.2 states that if a friend discusses a matter with A, A is not acting for any party and is told the friend is represented by B, it would be a gross discourtesy for A to comment on the advice tendered by B; it is not improper for A to suggest that the friend discuss certain aspects of the matter with B so that B can advise on them; but it is not proper for A to discuss the matter further than that, as otherwise the relationship of mutual confidence and trust between the friend and B would be disturbed.
> - PD 8.3.1 states that as between members of the profession one's word should be one's bond, and a legal practitioner's word should be accepted unless there is strong ground to doubt that practitioner's integrity; in normal circumstances it is not necessary to obtain confirmation from a former client before parting with the former client's money, property or documents to the new legal practitioner; and it is improper for the former legal practitioner to communicate with the client who has left, which would breach r 7 PCR, even if only to confirm what the new legal practitioner has written.

**P** These are manifestations of the duty of courtesy and fairness to other lawyers (r 7(2) PCR). **A** Second opinion: giving one is permitted, with or without [Firm Y]'s knowledge. Improper influence: here [Lawyer] [criticised Firm Y's advice and offered a discount to switch], which goes beyond an opinion. Taking over: [Lawyer] [began work / agreed to act] before the client [discharged Firm Y / changed solicitors on the record], contrary to PD 7.3.3 and PD 7.3.2. Friend: if [Lawyer] was only advising a friend informally, commenting on Firm Y's advice is a gross discourtesy under PD 8.5.2. Former lawyer: if [Firm Y] phoned the client to confirm the new firm's instructions before releasing the file, it breached r 7 as PD 8.3.1 explains. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 7(4) [and PD 7.3.3], and should wait until the retainer is ended in writing.

*Source note: r 7(2), (4) PCR p13–14; PD 7.3.3, 7.3.2, 8.5.2, 8.3.1 (each p1); manifestations of r 7(2) L6 p12–14; Cyril §IV.1.3.7–1.3.11 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-56 — Asking another lawyer for their authority to act
*Keywords: authority to act, r 7(8), r 7(9), warrant to act, written representation, good reason to suspect, PD 7.4.3, give reasons, no privilege, privileged material expunged, Tung Hui Mannequin, aspersions, breach of warranty of authority*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] may insist on seeing [other lawyer]'s warrant to act. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 7(9) PCR, which requires a legal practitioner to accept another legal practitioner's written representation of authority to act unless there is good reason to suspect that it is false.
**R** A lawyer
may ask another lawyer whether they have authority to act for a person, and must accept their
written representation of authority unless there is good reason to suspect it is false (r 7(8)–(9)
PCR). A firm that still wants to see the warrant must give its reasons, and the other firm,
once given reasons, should as a matter of course produce it; no privilege attaches to a warrant
to act once proceedings start (PD 7.4.3 Part C).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 7(8) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner may ask another legal practitioner (B) whether B has the authority to act for a person.
> - Rule 7(9) PCR states that a legal practitioner (A) must accept a written representation by another legal practitioner (B) that B is authorised to act for a person, unless A has good reason for suspecting that the representation is false.
> - PD 7.4.3 Part C states that a law practice
>   - (A) must accept another law practice's (B) written representation that it is authorised to act for a particular client on the face value of the representation, unless there are good reasons for suspecting that it has been falsely made; should A insist that B disclose its Warrant to Act despite a written representation, A should give B its reasons for suspecting that the representation has been falsely made; the practitioner asked is entitled to ask for those reasons, and once they are given should, as a matter of course, disclose the Warrant to Act; and where an action has been commenced in court, no privilege attaches ipso facto to a Warrant to Act.
> - PD 7.4.3 Part F adds that where the Warrant to Act contains privileged material it may be disclosed after expunging that material, or the solicitor should obtain a further brief warrant without it.

**P** A lawyer should not view a request to produce his warrant to act as casting aspersions on his professionalism and integrity (*Tung Hui Mannequin Industries v Tenet Insurance Co Ltd* [2005] 3 SLR(R) 184 at [44]–[45]), and courtesy is subject to the duty to the client to challenge the other side's authority when appropriate. Acting without authority exposes a lawyer to damages for breach of warranty of authority, liability for wasted costs and disciplinary action. **A** Asking: [Lawyer] was entitled under r 7(8) to ask [Firm Y] whether it acted for [named client]. Written representation: [Firm Y] [gave / did not give] one. Good reason: [Lawyer] [had none / had a real reason, such as the named client denying instructions or rival directors each claiming to instruct for the company]. If [Lawyer] demanded the warrant without giving reasons, that breached r 7(9) and PD 7.4.3 Part C; once reasons are given, [Firm Y] should produce it, expunging any privileged material. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 7(9), and [should accept the written assurance / is entitled to see the warrant, giving reasons].

*Source note: r 7(8)–(9) PCR p14; PD 7.4.3 Part C p1, Part F pp4–5; Tung Hui Mannequin at [44]–[45] and courtesy subject to the duty to the client L6 p16, SYL p8; consequences of acting without authority L1 p11–12.*

### eth-w-0-57 — Entrapment to obtain evidence of another lawyer's misconduct
*Keywords: entrapment, private investigator, agent provocateur, touting evidence, PD 8.5.5, illegal or improper means, Procurer, r 7(2), complain to Law Society, Wong Keng Leong Rayney, Tan Guat Neo Phyllis, evidence not excluded*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] could gather evidence of [rival lawyer]'s suspected [touting] by [using an investigator posing as a client]. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 7(2) PCR, as PD 8.5.5 applies it, which makes it improper for a legal practitioner to obtain evidence of another legal practitioner's misconduct by entrapment or by illegal or improper means.
**R** Entrapment means luring or instigating a lawyer to commit an offence
or breach of the rules that they would not otherwise have committed (PD 8.5.5, adopting *Wong
Keng Leong Rayney v Law Society of Singapore* [2007] 4 SLR(R) 377 at [27]). A lawyer who obtains
evidence by entrapment or illegal or improper means is subject to the same standards and the
ordinary processes of law (*Law Society of Singapore v Tan Guat Neo Phyllis* [2008] 2 SLR(R) 239
at [59]); instigating a colleague's breach breaches the duty of courtesy and fairness (r 7(2)
PCR) and derogates from the dignity of the profession (PD 8.5.5). A lawyer aware of another's
misconduct should report it, not manufacture it (PD 8.5.5).

> **Rule in full**
> - PD 8.5.5 applies, which adopts the definition in *Wong Keng Leong Rayney v Law Society of Singapore* [2007] 4 SLR(R) 377 at [27]: entrapment involves luring or instigating the legal practitioner to commit an offence or a breach of the rules of professional conduct which otherwise, or in ordinary circumstances, he or she would not have committed, in order to prosecute him or her; it invariably entails unlawful conduct by an agent provocateur, by abetment by instigation or intentionally aiding the offence or breach, whereas obtaining evidence illegally or improperly does not necessarily involve any instigation or inducement.
> - PD 8.5.5 then states that where a legal practitioner (the Procurer) obtains such evidence, directly or indirectly,
>   - (a) the Procurer is subject to "the same standards of conduct under the disciplinary code and also the law" (*Law Society of Singapore v Tan Guat Neo Phyllis* [2008] 2 SLR(R) 239 at [59]) and, if also the agent provocateur and guilty of wrongdoing, to the ordinary processes of the law including disciplinary proceedings (*Rayney* at [52]);
>   - (b) instigating or intentionally aiding another legal practitioner to commit an offence or breach is a breach of the obligation to treat colleagues with courtesy and fairness under r 7(2) PCR, and is as objectionable as the ensuing breach;
>   - (c) it derogates from the dignity of the profession and its standing in the public eye, and a legal practitioner who becomes aware of another's offence or breach should lodge a complaint with the Law Society instead; and
>   - (d) it is viewed as deceptive conduct in two foreign jurisdictions.
> - The Council's position is that obtaining such evidence by entrapment or illegal or improper means is improper, and the Procurer may be liable to disciplinary action under s 83 LPA.
> - Rule 7(2) PCR states that a legal practitioner must treat other legal practitioners with courtesy and fairness.

**P** Entrapment does not excuse the entrapped lawyer, who is equally liable under s 83 LPA: the law on entrapment evidence in criminal proceedings has no application to disciplinary proceedings, and the remedy is neither to exclude the evidence nor to stay the proceedings (*Tan Guat Neo Phyllis* at [59], quoted in PD 8.5.5); there is a higher public interest in disciplining errant lawyers than in letting them off, however the evidence is obtained (*Rayney* at [50]–[54]). In most past touting cases where investigators were hired, the court found the evidence had not been obtained by entrapment or improper means (PD 8.5.5). **A** Luring or instigation: here [investigator] [asked whether a referral fee would be paid / proposed a kickback / simply observed an existing practice of paying agents]. Observing an existing practice is not entrapment; proposing a breach the rival would not otherwise have committed is. Illegal or improper means: were the means used [describe] otherwise unlawful or improper, even without instigation? **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 7(2) and PD 8.5.5, and should report the suspected misconduct to the Law Society instead; [rival lawyer] remains liable for any touting proved.

*Source note: PD 8.5.5 pp1–2 (Rayney at [27], [52] and Tan Guat Neo Phyllis at [59] as quoted there); r 7(2) PCR p13; Rayney at [50]–[54] L6 p17; SYL p8, p11 (SYL p11 lists Rayney's 2006 High Court decision, [2006] 4 SLR(R) 934, and Tan Guat Neo Phyllis).*

### eth-w-0-58 — Entering default judgment against a represented party: r 28
*Keywords: default judgment, r 28, two working days, written notice, represented party, 4pm, working day, order of court, default of appearance, unfair advantage of oversight, warrior not assassin, Public Trustee*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] could enter default judgment against [defendant], who has a lawyer. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 28(1) PCR, which forbids a legal practitioner to enter default judgment against a represented party without first giving that party's legal practitioner written notice and letting at least 2 working days elapse.
**R** A lawyer must not enter default judgment under
the Rules of Court 2021 against a party represented by another lawyer unless written notice of
the intention has been given to that lawyer and at least two working days have passed (r 28(1)
PCR). Notice given after 4pm or on a non-working day counts from the next working day (r 28(2),
(4)). The rule does not extend any court deadline, and no notice is needed before acting on a
failure to comply with a court order within the time it sets (r 28(3)).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 28(1) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner (A) must not enter a default judgment under any provision of the Rules of Court 2021 against a party who is represented by another legal practitioner (B), unless
>   - (a) A has given B written notice of A's intention to enter the default judgment; and
>   - (b) at least 2 working days have elapsed after the notice is given to B.
> - Rule 28(2) states that any notice under paragraph (1) that is given on a working day after 4 p.m., or on a day other than a working day, is to be treated as given on the next working day.
> - Rule 28(3) states that, to avoid doubt,
>   - (a) the rule does not extend the time stipulated by an order of court, or by any provision of the Rules of Court 2021, for taking any action or step; and
>   - (b) a legal practitioner need not give any notice under paragraph (1) before taking any action or step on a failure to comply with an order of court within the time stipulated by the order of court.
> - Rule 28(4) states that "working day" means any day other than a Saturday, Sunday or public holiday.
> - The guiding principle in r 27(b) PCR is that a legal practitioner is responsible to an opposing legal practitioner for maintaining due process and promoting the administration of justice.

**P** The rule is one of courtesy and fairness, so that one lawyer does not take unfair advantage of another's oversight; as *Public Trustee v By Products Traders Pte Ltd* [2005] SGHC 103 put the lawyer's duty generally at [31], quoting Whyatt CJ, the arms a lawyer wields are to be those of a warrior, not an assassin. The notice can only be given after the time for entering an appearance or filing the defence has lapsed. Rule 28 no longer requires B to be "on record", and the better view is that notice should also be given before judgment in default of appearance where it is known from correspondence that a lawyer acts for the defaulting party. **A** Representation: [defendant] was represented by [Firm Y] [on record / to [Lawyer]'s knowledge from correspondence]. Notice: [Lawyer] [gave no written notice / emailed notice at 5pm on a Friday]. Timing: notice sent after 4pm on a Friday is treated as given on the Monday (r 28(2)), so judgment entered on [the Monday / the Tuesday] was entered before 2 working days had elapsed. Exception: r 28(3)(b) does not help unless the default was a failure to comply with a court order within the time it set. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 28(1), and should consent to setting the judgment aside.

*Source note: r 27–28 PCR p51–52; purpose, Public Trustee at [31], timing after lapse of time and default of appearance (Pinsler) L6 p19–21; Public Trustee SYL p8 and L6 p19 (both cite [2005] 3 SLR 449); neutral citation [2005] SGHC 103 and [31] read on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026): [31] is about the duty to the court versus the client and quotes Whyatt CJ; it does not deal with default judgment, so the r 28 application is L6 p19's; Cyril §IV.2 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-59 — Allegations against another lawyer in court documents: r 29
*Keywords: allegation, another lawyer, r 29, opportunity to respond, disclose response to court, affidavit, letters filed, PD 8.1.1, sufficient particulars, reasonable time, draft document, lead counsel, non-practising, criminal victim, firm's staff, Tan Beng Hui Carolyn, Imran bin Mohd Arip*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] could file [a document] alleging [misconduct] by [other lawyer]. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 29 PCR, which forbids a legal practitioner to permit an allegation against another legal practitioner in a document filed for the client in court proceedings unless that practitioner is given the opportunity to respond and, where practicable, the response is disclosed to the court.
**R** A lawyer must not permit an allegation against
another lawyer in any document filed for the client in court proceedings unless that lawyer is
given the opportunity to respond and, where practicable, the response is disclosed to the court
(r 29 PCR). The other lawyer must be given sufficient particulars, ideally the draft document;
the rule does not apply where the other lawyer is a party, where a criminal client accuses the
lawyer-victim, or where the lawyer is non-practising, and may apply to allegations about a
firm's staff that go to the firm's processes (PD 8.1.1).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 29 PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner (A) must not permit an allegation to be made against another legal practitioner (B) in any document filed on behalf of A's client in any court proceedings, unless
>   - (a) B is given the opportunity to respond to the allegation; and
>   - (b) where practicable, B's response (if any) is disclosed to the court.
> - PD 8.1.1 Part A states that the purpose is to give B, who as a non-party would not otherwise have an opportunity to respond, the chance to provide the court a full and balanced picture, and that after B replies A may withdraw or modify the allegation.
> - Part B states that r 29 does not apply
>   - (a) when B is a party to the proceedings;
>   - (b) where a client in a criminal suit makes allegations against B who is the victim; or
>   - (c) when an allegation is made against B who is a non-practising legal practitioner.
> - Part C states that r 29 may apply to an allegation against non-qualified staff of B's law practice, in particular one which goes towards the processes, oversight or management of the firm, unless the allegation is personal to the staff member.
> - Part D states that it is A's duty to give B sufficient particulars of the allegation to enable B to respond fully, which depends on the facts (for example, whether all the exhibits to an affidavit must be sent), and that it is prudent practice to forward B the draft document containing the allegation.

**P** "Document" includes affidavits sworn by A's client and letters filed for the client. Allegations against lead counsel may by implication be made against the lawyers assisting him, so r 29 extends to them; the opportunity must be real, with sufficient particulars and reasonable time to respond, and a letter sent only on the day the document is filed is not enough (*Imran bin Mohd Arip v Public Prosecutor* [2021] SGCA 91 at [16], [96], [98]). A lawyer who refused, when invited, to remove allegations against opposing counsel and the judge from an affidavit, and repeated allegations against another lawyer, was fined $10,000, her lack of remorse and the repetition being aggravating (*Tan Beng Hui Carolyn v Law Society of Singapore* [2023] SGCA 7). **A** Who is B: here [the client's former lawyer / opposing counsel] is a practising lawyer who is not a party, and no Part B exclusion applies. Document: [the client's affidavit / a letter to court] is filed on the client's behalf. Limb (a): [Lawyer] [did not send it / sent it the day of filing / sent only a vague summary], which gives neither sufficient particulars nor reasonable time. Limb (b): any response must, where practicable, be put before the court. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 29, and should give the lawyer the particulars and time to respond, and put any response before the court.

*Source note: r 29 PCR p52; PD 8.1.1 Parts A–D p1; documents covered L7 p13–15; lead counsel and Imran at [16], [96], [98] L6 p23, SYL p8; Tan Beng Hui Carolyn [2023] SYL p8, facts and fine Cyril §IV.3 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-60 — Communicating with the court and disclosing other lawyers' letters: r 30–31
*Keywords: communication with court, r 30, one-sided, ex parte communication, reasonable opportunity, copy to other parties, inform as soon as possible, without notice application, injunction, 2 hours' notice, Supreme Court Practice Directions para 71, r 31, without prejudice, off the record, communication between lawyers, letter to judge*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] could [write to the court / show the court correspondence with the other side]. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 30(1) [/ r 31] PCR, which forbids a legal practitioner to initiate a communication with the court about pending proceedings without giving the other parties' legal practitioners a reasonable opportunity to be present or reply [/ to disclose to the court communications with the other side's legal practitioner without their agreement].
**R** A lawyer acting in pending
proceedings must not initiate any communication with the court about the case unless every
other party's lawyer had a reasonable opportunity to be present or reply and, for written
communications, every other party gets a copy (r 30(1) PCR); a failure must be disclosed to the
other lawyers as soon as possible (r 30(2)). Communications about without-notice applications
are governed instead by the practice directions on such applications (r 30(3)–(4)). A lawyer
must not disclose to the court any communication about the matter with another party's lawyer
unless both lawyers agree (r 31 PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 30(1) PCR applies, which states that where a legal practitioner (A) acts for a party to any proceedings that the legal practitioner knows are pending, or are likely to be pending, before the court, the legal practitioner must not initiate any communication with the court about the facts, issues or any other matter in those proceedings, unless
>   - (a) A has given each legal practitioner acting for another party to those proceedings a reasonable opportunity to be present or to reply; and
>   - (b) if A's communication with the court was in writing, every other party to those proceedings is given a copy of the written communication.
> - Rule 30(2) states that if a legal practitioner who acts for a party to any proceedings fails to comply with paragraph (1) when communicating with the court about those proceedings, the legal practitioner must inform each legal practitioner acting for another party of the circumstances as soon as possible.
> - Rule 30(3) states that a legal practitioner who initiates any communication with the court in relation to an application without notice filed by the legal practitioner must comply with all relevant practice directions relating to applications without notice, and r 30(4) states that, except as provided in paragraph (3), paragraphs (1) and (2) do not apply to such a communication.
> - For injunctions sought without notice, para 71 of the Supreme Court Practice Directions 2021 requires the applicant to give the other parties notice of the application before the hearing (by email, or orally by telephone in extreme urgency), at least 2 hours before the hearing, stating the date, time and place and the relief sought, with a copy of the papers if possible and draft supporting affidavits as soon as they are ready; and, if they are absent at the hearing, to tell the court what attempts were made to notify them, what documents were given and when, and whether they consent to the hearing in their absence; but this need not be done where notice would or might defeat the purpose of the application, provided the reasons are set out in the supporting affidavit.
> - For ordinary correspondence, para 21(2) of the Practice Directions requires correspondence to the court about a pending matter to be copied to all other parties or their solicitors unless there are good reasons not to, and solicitors must not copy the court on correspondence between themselves.
> - Rule 31 PCR states that where a legal practitioner acts for a party in a matter, the legal practitioner must not disclose to the court any communication relating to the matter between the legal practitioner and a legal practitioner acting for another party in the matter, unless there is an agreement between the 2 legal practitioners to do so.

**P** Both rules protect due process and trust between lawyers (r 27 PCR). Rule 31 covers discussions both before and during proceedings, about the way to proceed, mutual concessions on procedure or the lawyers' views of the case; in practice lawyers also expressly reserve their rights with labels such as "without prejudice" or "off the record". **A** Rule 30: here [Lawyer] [emailed the judge's clerk about the merits / the adjournment] [without copying the other side / without giving them a chance to reply]. Was this instead a communication on [Lawyer]'s own without-notice application, so that r 30(3)–(4) and the practice directions govern? If r 30(1) was breached, r 30(2) requires [Lawyer] to tell the other side at once. Rule 31: [Lawyer] [exhibited the other side's settlement letter / told the court what counsel conceded on a call] without agreement. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 30(1) [/ r 31], must inform the other side at once (r 30(2)) and withdraw the material.

*Source note: r 27, 30–31 PCR p51–53; Supreme Court PD 2021 paras 21(2) and 71 checked against the e-PD text on judiciary.gov.sg (3 Nov 2026), para 71 also L7 p5–7; scope of r 31 L7 p19; Cyril §IV.4 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-61 — Unfair advantage, letters of demand and threats: r 8(3)–(5)
*Keywords: unfair advantage, r 8(3), deceit, wrongful reference, letter of demand, r 8(4), not recoverable, costs of letter, simple debt, mortgage arrears, instalments, PD 1.8.1, threat of criminal proceedings, police report, r 8(5), PD 9.1.2, statutory penalty, disciplinary complaint threat, offensive letter, copying bank, Terence Tan, Peter Pang, Carolyn Tan Beng Hui 1999, Wong Juan Swee, Chew Kia Heng*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer]'s [letter / conduct] towards [X] was proper. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 8(4) [/ r 8(5) / r 8(3)] PCR, which forbids a legal practitioner to demand in a letter of demand anything not recoverable by due process of law [/ to threaten criminal or disciplinary proceedings over an unsatisfied civil claim / to take unfair advantage of any person].
**R** A lawyer must not take unfair advantage of any person or act
towards anyone fraudulently, deceitfully or otherwise contrary to membership of an honourable
profession (r 8(3) PCR). A letter of demand must not demand anything not recoverable by due
process of law (r 8(4) PCR); so a lawyer collecting a simple debt must not demand the cost of the
letter, though instalment arrangements may provide for costs (PD 1.8.1). Where a client's civil
claim is unsatisfied, the lawyer must not threaten criminal or disciplinary proceedings
(r 8(5) PCR); saying the client "may consider lodging a police report" is improper, though it is
proper to say that non-compliance with an order or statute carries a stated penalty, and
offensive language is unbefitting (PD 9.1.2).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 8(3) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner
>   - (a) must not take unfair advantage of any person; and
>   - (b) must not act towards any person in a way which is fraudulent, deceitful or otherwise contrary to the legal practitioner's position as a member of an honourable profession.
> - Rule 8(4) PCR states that when a legal practitioner issues a letter of demand, the legal practitioner must not demand in the letter anything that is not recoverable by due process of law.
> - Rule 8(5) PCR states that where any civil claim made against a person, by a client of a legal practitioner, remains unsatisfied, the legal practitioner must not threaten the institution of any criminal or disciplinary proceedings against the person. These are guided by r 8(1)(a) PCR: a legal practitioner who deals with any person must be honest and courteous, and behave in a manner befitting the legal practitioner's professional standing.
> - PD 1.8.1 states that
>   - (A) a legal practitioner shall not issue a demand for anything that is not properly recoverable by due process of the law;
>   - (B) where instructed to collect a simple debt, it is improper also to demand the costs of the letter, because at that stage it cannot be said that those costs are properly recoverable in law;
>   - (C) that illustration does not apply where, after a motor accident, correspondence with the insurers or their lawyers results in an agreed settlement;
>   - (D) a mortgagee's lawyer demanding arrears must not at the same time demand the costs of the letter unless he or she explains that those costs can be added to the mortgage debt, for example where the mortgage so provides;
>   - (E) where a creditor wrongly demanded a debt from a third party, the third party's lawyer may write that he or she would advise the client to accept an apology for the libel provided his or her charges are paid; and
>   - (F) there is no objection to arranging payment of a simple contract debt or judgment debt by instalments on terms that the debtor pays the creditor's lawyer's costs, nor to agreeing to accept instalments only if the debtor's lawyers guarantee payment.
> - PD 9.1.2 states that a legal practitioner shall not threaten criminal proceedings against a person who has failed to admit or satisfy a civil claim; a letter stating that the client "may consider lodging a report with the police with the view of the arrest of any person for an offence under the Penal Code" is improper, since the disclosure of an offence is no justification for pressure to recover a civil debt; it is also improper to threaten criminal proceedings to achieve any stated objective, for example to compel a witness to attend to give or sign a statement; it is not improper to require a party to comply with a particular order, enjoinment or statutory provision and state, identifying it, the offence or penalty that failure will attract; and offensive or insulting language, and letters threatening criminal proceedings to coerce the other party, are unbefitting conduct.

**P** Some 27 letters alleging crimes, threatening criminal proceedings and containing offensive and scandalous statements were misconduct unbefitting an advocate and solicitor (*Law Society of Singapore v Terence Tan Bian Chye* [2007] SGDSC 10); threatening a colleague with a police report for theft, to stop him taking files from the lawyer's desk, was improper (*Law Society of Singapore v Peter Pang Xiang Zhong* [2006] SGDSC 21). Copying a letter of demand to the recipient's bankers to put undue pressure on him is an example of unfair advantage under r 8(3)(a) (*Carolyn Tan Beng Hui v The Law Society of Singapore* [1999] SGHC 23), and a lawyer who gave a creditworthiness reference while hiding a bankruptcy she knew of was fined for a wrongful reference, an example under r 8(3)(b) (*Wong Juan Swee v The Law Society of Singapore* [1994] 3 SLR(R) 619). A letter demanding that a party accept service within 5 minutes, with a veiled threat to tell his employers, drew a reprimand for offensive letters and unreasonable demands (*Law Society of Singapore v Chew Kia Heng* [2001] SGDSC 3). **A** Rule 8(4): here [Lawyer]'s letter [demanded $X "legal costs" for the letter on a simple debt], which is not recoverable at that stage unless [the contract provides for it / PD 1.8.1 Parts C–F apply]. Rule 8(5): [the letter warned of a police report for cheating unless paid within 7 days], which threatens criminal proceedings over an unsatisfied civil claim and is not a permitted statement of a statutory penalty. Rule 8(3): [copying the debtor's bank / employer] to pressure him takes unfair advantage. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 8[(4) / (5) / (3)], and should withdraw the demand or threat in writing.

*Source note: r 8(1)(a), (3)–(4) PCR p15; r 8(5) PCR p16; PD 1.8.1 Parts A–F p1; PD 9.1.2 p1; costs where contractually provided L7 p28; Terence Tan L7 p30, SYL p9; Peter Pang SYL p9, facts Cyril §IV.5.4 (secondary); Carolyn Tan Beng Hui [1999] and Wong Juan Swee L7 p26–27, Cyril §IV.5.2 (secondary); Chew Kia Heng L7 p32.*

### eth-w-0-62 — Personal misconduct; former mediator acting; dealings with public institutions
*Keywords: personal misconduct, private life, s 83(2)(h), s 83(2)(a), conviction, dishonesty, burglary, outrage of modesty, insult of modesty, abusive language, blog posts, mediator, r 8(6), prison visit, PD 4.3.1, misleading officials, Choy Chee Yean, Gopalan Nair, Ismail bin Atan, CNH, Kirpal Singh, Ong Ying Ping, Rai Vijay Kumar*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer]'s conduct [outside practice / after mediating] is misconduct. **C** [Lawyer]'s conduct [is / is not] due cause under s 83(2)(h) LPA, which covers misconduct unbefitting an advocate and solicitor as an officer of the Supreme Court or as a member of an honourable profession [, and [Lawyer] did breach r 8(6) PCR].
**R** A lawyer
must be honest and courteous to every person and behave in a way befitting their professional
standing and consistent with the public interest (r 8(1)(a)–(b) PCR). Misconduct unbefitting an
advocate and solicitor as an officer of the court or member of an honourable profession is due
cause, whether or not it occurs in practice (s 83(2)(h) LPA). A lawyer who acted as mediator
must not later act for any party on the subject of the mediation or anything discussed in it
(r 8(6) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Section 83(2)(h) LPA applies, which states that due cause may be shown by proof that an advocate and solicitor has been guilty of such misconduct unbefitting an advocate and solicitor as an officer of the Supreme Court or as a member of an honourable profession; s 83(2)(a) LPA adds proof that the advocate and solicitor has been convicted of a criminal offence, implying a defect of character which makes him or her unfit for his or her profession. On due cause, s 83(1) LPA makes the advocate and solicitor liable
>   - (a) to be struck off the roll;
>   - (b) to be suspended from practice for a period not exceeding 5 years;
>   - (c) to pay a penalty of not more than $100,000;
>   - (d) to be censured; or
>   - (e) to a penalty in addition to suspension or censure.
> - Rule 8(1) PCR guides the standard:
>   - (a) a legal practitioner who deals with any person must, regardless of whether that person is involved in any matter with which the legal practitioner is concerned, be honest and courteous, and behave in a manner befitting the legal practitioner's professional standing; and
>   - (b) must behave in a manner consistent with the public interest.
> - Rule 8(6) PCR states that a legal practitioner who has acted as a mediator in a mediation must not subsequently act for any party to the mediation in relation to
>   - (a) the subject of the mediation; or
>   - (b) any matter discussed during the mediation.
> - For prison visits, PD 4.3.1 directs lawyers to book interviews with inmates through the Singapore Prison Service's procedure and to follow its visit instructions for lawyers.

**P** Misconduct in a personal capacity counts: a non-practising lawyer convicted of using abusive words to police officers and disorderly behaviour in public, and of contempt for blog posts insulting judges, including posts breaching his undertaking to the court, was struck off, the court finding "contemptuous disrespect" for the judges (*Law Society of Singapore v Gopalan Nair* [2011] SGHC 191 at [5], [25]). A conviction in Hong Kong for burglary, an offence involving dishonesty, led to striking off, and work stress was no excuse (*Law Society of Singapore v Choy Chee Yean* [2010] 3 SLR 560). Misconduct without dishonesty may still justify striking off where it falls below the required standards of integrity, probity and trustworthiness, as with outraging the modesty of a legal executive in the lawyer's firm (*Law Society of Singapore v Ismail bin Atan* [2017] SGHC 190) and taking inappropriate photographs of a colleague (*Law Society of Singapore v CNH* [2022] SGHC 114); asking a client to belly dance during a professional visit to her premises was unbefitting and drew a reprimand (*Law Society of Singapore v Kirpal Singh s/o Hakam Singh* [2010] SGDT 5). Lawyers must comply with the legal requirements of public institutions: telling prison officers that a prisoner's wife was his assistant, when the approval covered only the lawyer, led to suspension (*Law Society of Singapore v Ong Ying Ping* [2005] 3 SLR(R) 583). Writing directly to represented doctors, and misleading them that they must give a statement, breached r 7(3) and r 8(3) (*Rai Vijay Kumar v Law Society of Singapore* [2025] SGHC 159; see 0-54). **A** Here [Lawyer] [was convicted of X / shouted abuse at / misled the officer / mediated the dispute and now acts for one side]. Section 83(2)(a): is there a conviction implying a defect of character? Section 83(2)(h): does the conduct, though outside practice, show dishonesty or a lack of integrity, probity or trustworthiness, or otherwise fall short of r 8(1)(a)–(b)? Rule 8(6): does the new matter concern the subject of the mediation or anything discussed in it? The rule contains no consent exception. **C** [Lawyer]'s conduct [is / is not] due cause under s 83(2)(h) [and [Lawyer] did breach r 8(6)].

*Source note: s 83(1), (2)(a) LPA p217; s 83(2)(h) LPA p218; r 8(1), (6) PCR p15–16; PD 4.3.1 p1; Gopalan Nair SYL p9 ([2011] SGHC 191, "abusive language and unruly behaviour"), L7 p36 (cited as [2011] 4 SLR 607: blog posts, struck off); charges, facts and sanction [5], [25] read from [2011] SGHC 191 on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026; it was the single 2011 decision on this respondent, but the [2011] 4 SLR 607 report cite was not confirmed online); Choy Chee Yean L7 p37, SYL p9; Kirpal Singh L7 p38, SYL p9; Ismail bin Atan, CNH SYL p9, facts Cyril §IV.5.6 (secondary); Ong Ying Ping SYL p9, facts Cyril §IV.5.7 (secondary); Rai Vijay Kumar read from the judgment, SYL p9.*

### eth-w-0-63 — Conduct in alternative dispute resolution: r 8A
*Keywords: ADR, mediation, arbitration, adjudication, r 8A, respectful, courteous, good faith, mislead mediator, client misleading mediator, r 17(2)(c), r 17(2)(e), r 8(6), GN 7.1.2, ADR advisory, Standard Advisory for All Potential Litigants, explain alternatives to litigation, small claims tribunal, neutral evaluation, never ask client to settle*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] behaved properly in [mediation / arbitration]. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 8A PCR, which requires a legal practitioner in an alternative dispute resolution process to be respectful, courteous and in good faith, and not to mislead, or knowingly let the client mislead, the adjudicator or mediator.
**R** In any ADR process a lawyer must be respectful of the process
and of the adjudicator or mediator, courteous in presenting the case, and act in good faith
throughout; must not knowingly mislead the adjudicator or mediator; and must not knowingly
assist or permit the client to do so (r 8A(1)–(5) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 8A PCR applies, which states that
>   - (1) a legal practitioner must always be respectful of the alternative dispute resolution process and the adjudicator, mediator or other person conducting the process;
>   - (2) a legal practitioner must always be courteous in the conduct or presentation of his or her client's case in an alternative dispute resolution process;
>   - (3) a legal practitioner must act in good faith throughout the alternative dispute resolution process;
>   - (4) a legal practitioner must not knowingly mislead or attempt to mislead in any way an adjudicator, a mediator or other person conducting the alternative dispute resolution process; and
>   - (5) a legal practitioner must not knowingly assist or permit his or her client to mislead an adjudicator, a mediator or other person conducting the alternative dispute resolution process.
> - GN 7.1.2 para 2 reminds members that under r 17(2)(e)(ii) PCR a legal practitioner must, in an appropriate case, together with the client evaluate the use of ADR processes.
> - Para 3 states that every legal practitioner is strongly encouraged to furnish and explain the "Standard Advisory for All Potential Litigants" (Annex A) to clients.
> - Para 4 states that, unless the client is already obliged by contract to engage in ADR, legal practitioners are strongly encouraged to mention
>   - (a) the options apart from litigation in court: mediation, arbitration, neutral evaluation or determination, and the small claims tribunal (for certain cases);
>   - (b) the eligibility criteria, process and consequence of each;
>   - (c) the benefits and disadvantages of each; and
>   - (d) a recommendation on which option is most suitable (if any).
> - Para 6 states that departing from paras 3 and 4 is not intended to result in disciplinary consequences; the binding duty is r 17(2)(e)(ii).

**P** These mirror the duties owed to a court not to knowingly mislead or attempt to mislead it (r 9(2)(a) PCR) and not to knowingly assist or permit the client to mislead it (r 10(6)(a) PCR). A lawyer must also explain clearly and properly to the client any proposal of amicable resolution or other offer or position taken by another party (r 17(2)(c) PCR) and, in an appropriate case, evaluate with the client the use of alternative dispute resolution processes (r 17(2)(e)(ii) PCR); GN 7.1.2 paras 3–4 strongly encourage explaining every alternative to litigation, so a lawyer who promises never to raise settlement, or discourages it in publicity, cuts against that duty. A lawyer who acted as mediator must not later act for a party on the subject of the mediation or anything discussed in it (r 8(6) PCR). **A** Respect and courtesy, r 8A(1)–(2): [Lawyer] [was rude to / walked out on the mediator]. Good faith, r 8A(3): [Lawyer] [attended only to extract information with no intention to settle]. Misleading, r 8A(4): [Lawyer] [told the mediator the client had no other assets, knowing otherwise]. Client misleading, r 8A(5): [Lawyer] [let the client misstate the figures]. Confidentiality of the mediation does not license misleading the mediator, and a lawyer who learns the client has misled the mediator must not continue on that footing. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 8A[(3) / (4) / (5)].

*Source note: r 8A PCR p16; r 8(6) PCR p16; GN 7.1.2 paras 2–4, 6 p1; r 9(2)(a) PCR p17; r 10(6) PCR p21; r 17(2) PCR p31; r 8A has no cases listed in SYL p9.*

---

## Staff and appointments

### eth-w-0-26 — Supervision of staff and paralegals
*Keywords: supervision, r 32, paralegal, legal executive, clerk, GN 3.7.1, constant supervision, unsupervised, no right of audience, PTC, signs off emails, deals with clients alone, periodic checks, Yeo Siew Chye Troy, Tan See Leh Jonathan*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] exercised proper supervision over [paralegal]. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 32 PCR, which requires a legal practitioner, whatever his or her designation, to exercise proper supervision over the staff working under him or her.
**R** A lawyer must, whatever their designation, exercise
proper supervision over the staff working under them (r 32 PCR). Paralegals are
unauthorised persons who may not practise law; the lawyer remains responsible for all
professional work, and must ensure paralegals do not act unsupervised in legal matters
(GN 3.7.1).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 32 PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must, regardless of the legal practitioner's designation in a law practice, exercise proper supervision over the staff working under the legal practitioner in the law practice.
> - GN 3.7.1 para 1 defines a "paralegal" as a legal executive, legal secretary or legal clerk and any other employee of a law practice who performs paralegal functions and assists a legal practitioner as a paralegal, who does not have in force a practising certificate, whatever the employee's designation.
> - Para 2 states that paralegals are "unauthorised persons" under ss 29, 32 and 33 LPA and are not allowed to practise law as an advocate and solicitor.
> - Para 4 states that
>   - (a) a legal practitioner shall ensure that he or she remains responsible for all professional actions of a paralegal and that the paralegal performs his or her duties, at all times, under the constant supervision of the legal practitioner in relation to any legal matter;
>   - (b) legal practitioners should take due care to ensure that paralegals make no unsupervised appearance before any court, including hearings in chambers and pre-trial conferences;
>   - (c) paralegals have no right of audience before any court, may attend chambers and open court to record notes of hearing only with the prior leave of the court, and where a paralegal accompanies the supervising lawyer to a hearing, the lawyer must ensure the court is told of the paralegal's status before proceedings begin, and the paralegal may not sit in seating reserved for lawyers without the court's permission;
>   - (d) legal practitioners must ensure that paralegals refrain from any form of unsupervised conduct in litigation matters and, in criminal matters, from unsupervised discussions with enforcement agencies, police officers or prosecutors, though paralegals may take statements from and interview clients or witnesses without the supervising lawyer provided no advice is rendered;
>   - (e) paralegals are subject to r 13(6) PCR (no publishing of material about proceedings that amounts to contempt or is calculated to interfere with a fair trial or prejudice the administration of justice), and lawyers must make them aware of it; and
>   - (f) under s 77 LPA no solicitor shall wilfully and knowingly enable an unauthorised person, which includes a paralegal, to practise law, and any action contrary to s 77 may warrant disciplinary proceedings.

**P** Allowing a conveyancing clerk to liaise with clients on payment matters, including collecting stamp duty, with no system of periodic checks on his dealings, was a failure of supervision; the clerk committed cheating and criminal breach of trust with the money, and the lawyer was suspended for four years (*Law Society of Singapore v Yeo Siew Chye Troy* [2019] SGHC 115). Allowing a paralegal, known to be without a practising certificate, to represent himself in emails as an advocate and solicitor, and agreeing to share fees with him, breached r 32 and led to a three-month suspension (*Law Society of Singapore v Tan See Leh Jonathan* [2020] SGHC 102). Letting non-lawyers run a branch office and deal alone with clients, without the effective supervision and monitoring of an advocate and solicitor, breached r 32 and was improper conduct under s 83(2)(b) LPA (*Law Society of Singapore v Chen Kok Siang Joseph* [2025] SGHC 44 at [89]–[90]; struck off on the totality of his misconduct, at [159]). A total absence of supervision over a conveyancing clerk for almost five years, during which the clerk misappropriated about $167,600 of a client's money, led to the maximum five years' suspension; that the lawyer was not dishonest and gained nothing was no mitigation (*Law Society of Singapore v Dhanwant Singh* [2025] SGHC 175 at [32], [36], [38], [55]). **A** Here [paralegal] [handles clients entirely alone / prepares all documents / signs letters in the firm's name without saying she is a paralegal / attends PTCs alone], while [Lawyer] is merely copied or signs when asked. Being copied on emails is not "constant supervision" under GN 3.7.1 para 4(a): [Lawyer] neither reviews the work nor communicates with the clients, and [attending a PTC alone / advising clients] is expressly barred by para 4(b) and (d). Inexperience or being a sole proprietor does not reduce the duty, which applies regardless of designation. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 32, and should take over the client relationships, review all work and make [paralegal]'s status clear.

*Source note: r 32 PCR p53; GN 3.7.1 paras 1–4(d) p1, para 4(e)–(f) p2; r 13(6) PCR p24; Yeo Siew Chye Troy L8 p18 (spelled "Tony" there), SYL p10, clerk's offences Cyril §V.1.2 (secondary); Tan See Leh Jonathan L8 p19, SYL p10; Chen Kok Siang Joseph and Dhanwant Singh listed SYL p10, facts and pinpoints read from the judgments on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026). Tested PS3 Q2.*

### eth-w-0-27 — Unauthorised persons; lending your name
*Keywords: unauthorised person, s 32 LPA, s 33 LPA, s 77 LPA, lending name, signing documents prepared by paralegal, holding out, PD 3.11.2, PD 3.11.1, signing the firm's name, signs as the firm, sign-off, designation, practising certificate, bank account, fee sharing, r 19, K V Sudeep Kumar*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] enabled [X], an unauthorised person, to practise law. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] contravene s 77(1) LPA, which forbids a solicitor to act as agent for, lend his or her name to, or do anything enabling, an unauthorised person to practise or purport to practise as a solicitor.
**R** A person without a
current practising certificate is an unauthorised person (s 32(2) LPA), and commits an
offence by acting as or pretending to be an advocate and solicitor (s 33 LPA). A solicitor
must not act as agent for an unauthorised person, permit their name to be used for the
unauthorised person's account or profit, or do anything enabling them to practise or
purport to practise as a solicitor; breach may lead to discipline (s 77(1), (3) LPA).

> **Rule in full**
> - Section 77(1) LPA applies, which states that a solicitor must not
>   - (a) wilfully and knowingly act as agent for any unauthorised person in any legal proceeding of any kind or in any matter which under the Act can be done only by a solicitor who has in force a practising certificate;
>   - (b) permit his or her name to be made use of in any such proceeding or matter upon the account or for the profit of any unauthorised person; or
>   - (c) send any process to any unauthorised person, or do any other act enabling any unauthorised person to appear, act or practise or purport to practise in any respect as a solicitor in any such proceeding or matter.
> - Section 77(2) states that a solicitor must not authorise any unauthorised person to operate any bank account in the name of the solicitor or the law practice maintained in connection with the practice.
> - Section 77(3) states that disciplinary proceedings may be taken against any solicitor who has acted in contravention of subsection (1) or (2), and s 77(4) makes the enabled unauthorised person guilty of an offence.
> - Under s 32(2) LPA a person is an unauthorised person unless he or she is
>   - (a) on the roll of advocates and solicitors with a practising certificate in force; or
>   - (b) on the roll of lawyers (NP) with a provisional practising certificate in force and, when carrying out the act in question, under the supervision of a solicitor.
> - Section 33(1) LPA makes it an offence for an unauthorised person
>   - (a) to act as an advocate or a solicitor or an agent for any party to proceedings, or as such to sue out any writ, summons or process, commence, carry on, solicit or defend any action in any court in Singapore, or draw or prepare any document relating to any proceeding in court; or
>   - (b) to wilfully or falsely pretend to be, or take or use any name, title, addition or description implying that he or she is, duly qualified or authorised to act as an advocate or a solicitor.
> - Section 33(2) LPA makes it an offence for an unauthorised person, directly or indirectly, to
>   - (a) draw or prepare any document or instrument relating to any movable or immovable property or to any legal proceeding;
>   - (b) take instructions for or draw or prepare papers to found or oppose a grant of probate or letters of administration;
>   - (d) write, publish or send for a claimant a letter or notice threatening legal proceedings, other than one saying the matter will be handed to a solicitor; or
>   - (e) solicit the right to negotiate, negotiate or settle any claim arising out of personal injury or death, unless he or she proves the act was not done for or in expectation of any fee, gain or reward; and s 33(3) makes it an offence for an unauthorised person, for or in expectation of any fee, gain or reward, to offer or agree to place the services of an advocate and solicitor at another person's disposal.
> - PD 3.11.2 states that a solicitor should not assist unauthorised persons to commit a breach of s 33 LPA by merely signing or "lending his name" to documents prepared by such unauthorised persons, including documents relating to the incorporation or formation of companies, and reminds members of their duty under r 32 PCR to supervise staff, which may include unauthorised persons.
> - PD 3.11.1 states that, generally, only a practising solicitor may sign the name of the law practice in a professional communication, but an unauthorised person can sign on behalf of a law practice so long as he or she does not sign in the name of the law practice; for instance, a manager, accountant or cashier may sign a letter or document on behalf of the law practice using his or her own name and proper designation.
> - Rule 19(1) PCR states that a legal practitioner must not share the legal practitioner's fees with, or pay a commission to, any unauthorised person for any legal work performed by the legal practitioner.

**P** Allowing a paralegal to hold himself out as an advocate and solicitor, and agreeing to share fees with him, led to a three-month suspension on charges under s 83(2)(b) and (h) LPA (*Law Society of Singapore v Tan See Leh Jonathan* [2020] SGHC 102); and letting a struck-off lawyer meet the client, give instructions and control a case, without telling the client he was not a practising lawyer, was characterised as allowing an unfit person to carry on the practice of law, not just a failure to supervise, and led to five years' suspension for one respondent and striking off for the other (*Law Society of Singapore v K V Sudeep Kumar* [2026] SGHC 9 at [36], [43], [52]). **A** Unauthorised person: [X] has no practising certificate (s 32(2)). Section 33: [X] [prepares the documents and runs the matters for her own contacts] and signs as "[Firm]" without disclosing her status, which is preparing documents under s 33(2)(a) and using a description implying she is qualified under s 33(1)(b). Section 77: [Lawyer] signs or lends his name when asked and shares the fees, which permits his name to be used for her profit (s 77(1)(b)) and enables her to practise in substance (s 77(1)(c)) [; if she operates the firm's bank account, s 77(2)]. PD 3.11.2: signing documents she prepared is exactly "lending his name", and the fee split breaches r 19(1). PD 3.11.1: signing as "[Firm]" rather than in her own name with her designation is itself improper, and the lawyer's r 32 duty is to stop it. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] contravene s 77(1)(b)–(c) and PD 3.11.2, alongside r 32 and r 19 PCR, which is due cause under s 83(2) LPA.

*Source note: s 32(2) LPA p92; s 33(1)–(3) LPA p92–94; s 77 LPA p202–203; s 83(2)(b), (h) LPA p217–218; PD 3.11.2 p1; PD 3.11.1 p1; r 19(1) PCR p35; Tan See Leh Jonathan L8 p19, SYL p10; K V Sudeep Kumar SYL p10, facts and sanctions read from the judgment on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026); summary of ss 32–33, 77 L8 p9–10, p15–17. Tested PS3 Q2, PYP20 Q2(b).*

### eth-w-0-28 — Executive appointments and outside businesses
*Keywords: executive appointment, director, business, r 34, First Schedule, Second Schedule, Third Schedule, Fourth Schedule, law-related services, non-executive director, independent director, r 38, s 83(2)(i) LPA, detracts from profession, estate agent, housing agent, debt collection, company secretary, PD 3.8.1*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] may hold [role] in [business]. **C** [Lawyer] [may / may not] hold the role, because r 34(1) PCR forbids a legal practitioner to accept an executive appointment associated with a prohibited business [and r 34(4) PCR allows one in a non-legal business only if every Second Schedule condition is met].
**R** A lawyer must not accept an executive appointment in a
business that detracts from or is incompatible with the dignity of the profession, that
materially interferes with their practice, availability to clients or representation of
clients, that is likely to unfairly attract legal business, or that shares fees with
unauthorised persons (r 34(1)(a)–(d) PCR). An appointment in a business that provides no
legal services is allowed if the Second Schedule conditions are met (r 34(4) PCR). An
"executive appointment" excludes non-executive and independent directorships (r 34(9)
PCR). Carrying on a business that detracts from the profession is due cause for
discipline (s 83(2)(i) Legal Profession Act 1966).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 34(1) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must not accept any executive appointment associated with any of the following businesses:
>   - (a) any business which detracts from, is incompatible with, or derogates from the dignity of, the legal profession;
>   - (b) any business which materially interferes with
>     - (i) the legal practitioner's primary occupation of practising as a lawyer;
>     - (ii) the legal practitioner's availability to those who may seek the legal practitioner's services as a lawyer; or
>     - (iii) the representation of the legal practitioner's clients;
>   - (c) any business which is likely to unfairly attract business in the practice of law;
>   - (d) any business which involves the sharing of the legal practitioner's fees with, or the payment of a commission to, any unauthorised person for legal work performed by the legal practitioner;
>   - (e) any business set out in the First Schedule, which lists a housing or estate agency business and a debt collection business; or
>   - (f) any business which is prohibited by the Act, the Rules or other subsidiary legislation made under the Act, or any practice directions, guidance notes and rulings issued under s 71(6) of the Act or by the Council or the Society.
> - Rule 34(3) states that, subject to paragraph (1), a legal practitioner may accept an executive appointment in a business entity which provides law-related services, which the Fourth Schedule lists as intellectual property, tax, trust business, company secretarial and Catalist continuing sponsor services.
> - Rule 34(4) states that, subject to paragraph (1), a legal practitioner (not being a locum solicitor) may accept an executive appointment in a business entity which does not provide any legal services or law-related services, if all of the conditions set out in the Second Schedule are satisfied. Those conditions are that
>   - (1) the business must not jeopardise the professional integrity, independence or competence of the legal practitioner, and must not detract from the standing and dignity of the legal profession;
>   - (2) the entity must not be held out or described so as to suggest that it is a law practice, or that any legal practitioner in it provides services, carries on business or is employed as a regulated legal practitioner;
>   - (3) the legal practitioner's involvement must not impair, and must not be in conflict with, the legal practitioner's duties in the law practice or to any client of that law practice;
>   - (4) the entity's files, records and accounts must be kept separate from and independent of the law practice's;
>   - (5) where a client of the law practice deals with or is referred to the entity, the legal practitioner must make full and frank disclosure to the client of
>     - (a) the relationship between the entity and the law practice and each legal practitioner in it with an interest in the entity,
>     - (b) their financial or other interests in the entity, and
>     - (c) that the statutory protection for clients of a law practice, including compulsory professional indemnity insurance and the Compensation Fund, may not be available to the entity's customers;
>   - (6) the law practice's client account and other client-money accounts must not hold money for the entity or its customers;
>   - (7) the entity's accounts must not hold money for the law practice or its clients; and
>   - (8) the entity must conduct its business at an address separate and distinct from the law practice's, though both may be in the same building and a registered office address alone does not count.
> - Rule 34(6)(b) states that, except as provided in paragraphs (2) to (5), a legal practitioner must not accept any executive appointment in a business entity, and r 34(7) states that nothing in the rule prohibits any appointment in a Third Schedule institution (the Society, the Academy, the Institute, NUS, SMU, SUSS and any registered charity).
> - Rule 34(9) defines "executive appointment" as a position associated with a business, or in a business entity or Singapore law practice, which entitles the holder of the position to perform executive functions in relation to it, but excluding any non-executive director or independent director; "business" includes any business, trade or calling in Singapore or elsewhere, whether or not for profit, but excludes the practice of law.
> - Rule 38 PCR states that a legal practitioner or law practice must not engage in any business, trade or calling which
>   - (a) derogates from the dignity of the legal profession;
>   - (b) is likely to unfairly attract business in the practice of law; or
>   - (c) is prohibited by the Act, the Rules or other subsidiary legislation, or the practice directions, guidance notes and rulings of the Council or the Society.
> - Section 83(2)(i) LPA makes it due cause that an advocate and solicitor carries on by himself or herself, or any person in his or her employment, any trade, business or calling that detracts from the profession of law or is in any way incompatible with it, or is employed in any such trade, business or calling.

**P** PD 3.8.1 states that (A) an executive partnership or executive directorship in an employment agency, firm or company may transgress s 83(2)(i) LPA; (B) a solicitor may properly be appointed company secretary for consideration, but acting for an external corporate secretarial firm's clients is the practice of law and must be done through the solicitor's law practice, with prior approval, and the resulting conflicts managed; (C) carrying on the business of a housing agent in tandem with practice is incompatible with the profession; and (D) a solicitor who in the course of practice agrees a finder's commission for securing a buyer or seller does not necessarily detract from the profession, but loses that benefit if he or she then acts in the conveyance, and being an estate agent in tandem with practice remains prohibited. **A** Executive or not: here [Lawyer] is [director and shareholder] of [a café] but takes no part in its operations. If the role is non-executive, it is not an "executive appointment" under r 34(9) and r 34 is not engaged. If executive, test r 34(1): a café does not detract from the profession (a), [does not materially interfere with practice] (b), does not attract legal work (c), and involves no fee-sharing (d) or First Schedule business (e). Then r 34(4) and the Second Schedule: separate premises, records and accounts, no holding out as a law practice, and disclosure to any firm client referred to it. Contrast a business that feeds legal work to the firm (r 34(1)(c)), an estate agency or a debt collection business (First Schedule; PD 3.8.1). **C** [Lawyer]'s role [is / is not] permissible [, subject to the Second Schedule].

*Source note: r 34(1) PCR p54; r 34(3)–(4) PCR p55; r 34(6)–(7), (9) PCR p56; r 38 PCR p60; First Schedule PCR p70; Second Schedule paras 1–8 PCR pp70–72; Third and Fourth Schedules PCR p72; s 83(2)(i) LPA p218–219; PD 3.8.1 Parts A–B p1, Parts C–D pp2–3; L8 p24–29, p32. Tested PS3 Q2.*

### eth-w-0-64 — Supervising practice trainees
*Keywords: practice trainee, supervision, r 32, supervising solicitor, eligible supervisor, unauthorised person, s 32 LPA, s 33 LPA, s 77 LPA, appear in court, watching brief, PD 3.9.2, Lun Yaodong, substantive work, r 36 non-examinable*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] properly supervised [trainee]. **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 32 PCR, which requires a legal practitioner to exercise proper supervision over the staff working under him or her, including practice trainees.
**R** A lawyer must exercise proper supervision over the staff working under them, whatever
their designation (r 32 PCR). A practice trainee receives supervised training and is an
unauthorised person who must not act as a solicitor (s 32–33 Legal Profession Act 1966).
Supervisors must ensure trainees do not hold themselves out as qualified; a trainee who sat on
a watching brief at a Coroner's Inquiry without saying he was not yet called prompted a reminder
(PD 3.9.2).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 32 PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must, regardless of the legal practitioner's designation in a law practice, exercise proper supervision over the staff working under the legal practitioner in the law practice.
> - A practice trainee is not on the roll and has no practising certificate, so is an unauthorised person under s 32(2) LPA; an unauthorised person who acts as an advocate or solicitor, or takes or uses any description implying that he or she is qualified to act as one, commits an offence (s 33(1)(a)–(b) LPA); and a solicitor must not do any act enabling an unauthorised person to appear, act or practise or purport to practise as a solicitor (s 77(1)(c) LPA).
> - PD 3.9.2 records that a practice trainee purported to appear on a watching brief for an insurer in a Coroner's Inquiry and, twice invited to ask questions, declined without telling the court he had not yet been called to the Bar, and reminds members of their responsibilities in supervising practice trainees. (Rule 36 PCR, on management's duty to provide supervised training by an eligible supervising solicitor, is non-examinable.)

**P** Directing a trainee to do substantive work, such as drafting a letter to court, preparing an affidavit of evidence-in-chief and legal research, knowing there was no eligible supervising solicitor, was misconduct; the lawyer, who did not meet the qualification under the Legal Profession (Admission) Rules 2011 to supervise trainees, was suspended for 18 months (*Law Society of Singapore v Clarence Lun Yaodong* [2022] SGHC 269 at [47]–[50]). **A** Eligible supervisor: [the trainee's supervisor] [had / lacked] the qualification to supervise. Supervision: [trainee] [drafted and sent letters to court / appeared alone / advised clients] without [any review]. Holding out: did the trainee make clear to the court and to clients that he is not yet called? If not, [Lawyer] has let an unauthorised person act or appear as a solicitor (PD 3.9.2; s 77(1)(c) LPA). **C** [Lawyer] [did / did not] breach r 32, and should put an eligible supervisor in place and review all the trainee's work.

*Source note: r 32 PCR p53; s 32(2), 33(1) LPA p92–93; s 77(1) LPA p202–203; PD 3.9.2 p1; r 36 non-examinable SYL p10; Lun Yaodong at [47]–[50] SYL p10, L7 p22, 18-month suspension and r 18(1)(b) Admission Rules L8 p20, substantive-work facts Cyril §V.1.1.3 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-65 — Titles and descriptions: r 33
*Keywords: title, description, r 33, advocate and solicitor, lawyer, legal consultant, Commissioner for Oaths, Notary Public, Senior Counsel, Queen's Counsel, foreign lawyer, approved designations, PD 3.5.2, business cards, calling cards, support staff, consultant, s 75D LPA, 10 years*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer / staff member] may use the description "[X]". **C** [The description] [is / is not] permitted under r 33(1) PCR, which allows an advocate and solicitor to describe himself or herself professionally only as an advocate and solicitor, a lawyer, a qualified legal consultant, an appointed Commissioner for Oaths or Notary Public, or a Council-approved description.
**R** An advocate and solicitor may describe himself or herself
professionally only as an advocate and solicitor, a lawyer, a legal consultant (if qualified),
a Commissioner for Oaths or Notary Public (if appointed), or another description the Council
approves; Senior Counsel may use that title; a regulated foreign lawyer only as a foreign
lawyer, a legal consultant or an approved description (r 33(1)–(3) PCR). The Council has
approved designations such as partner, associate, senior associate, consultant, counsel,
director and managing director; support staff's business cards must not give the impression
that they are lawyers (PD 3.5.2).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 33(1) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner who is an advocate and solicitor must not describe himself or herself in connection with his or her profession as anything other than
>   - (a) an advocate and solicitor;
>   - (b) a lawyer;
>   - (c) a legal consultant (if qualified to be one);
>   - (d) a Commissioner for Oaths (if appointed as one);
>   - (e) a Notary Public (if appointed as one); or
>   - (f) such other description as may be approved by the Council from time to time.
> - Rule 33(2) states that a legal practitioner who is a regulated foreign lawyer must not describe himself or herself in connection with his or her profession as anything other than
>   - (a) a foreign lawyer;
>   - (b) a legal consultant (if qualified to be one); or
>   - (c) such other description as may be approved by the Council from time to time.
> - Rule 33(3) states that paragraph (1) does not prevent a Senior Counsel appointed under s 30 LPA from describing himself or herself as a Senior Counsel, and r 33(4) that paragraph (2) does not prevent a Queen's Counsel, or the holder of an appointment of equivalent distinction of any jurisdiction, from so describing himself or herself.
