> - PD 3.5.2 states that the Council has approved these designations for Singapore advocates and solicitors: sole proprietor, partner, senior partner, managing partner, founding partner, legal assistant, associate, senior associate, consultant, senior consultant, counsel, director, managing director, senior executive director, executive director, senior associate director, associate director, chairman, adviser and senior adviser; that from 12 January 2001 the calling cards of directors of a law corporation must carry the description "advocate and solicitor" after their designation; that calling cards given to support staff employed in an executive capacity must contain
>   - (a) the name of the person for whose use the card is provided; and
>   - (b) the designation, stated in a manner as not to give the impression that he is a legal practitioner; and that the legal practitioner who provides the card must ensure the staff member does not use it without the firm's authority or in circumstances that breach the PCR.
> - Section 75D(1) LPA adds that a solicitor must not take or use the title of consultant unless he or she has, for not less than 10 years in the aggregate, been a solicitor in practice, a relevant legal officer, a full-time member of the academic staff of the law faculty or school of NUS, SMU or SUSS, or any combination of these; breach is an offence (s 75D(2)).

**P** The rule prevents the public being misled about who is qualified; an unauthorised person who takes or uses any name, title or description implying that he or she is qualified to act as an advocate or solicitor commits an offence (s 33(1)(b) LPA). **A** Here [X] is described as ["specialist", "legal adviser", "senior lawyer"], which is not within r 33(1)(a)–(e) and is not on the approved list (only "adviser" and "senior adviser" are); or [a lawyer of 6 years' standing calls himself "consultant"], which breaches s 75D(1) though "consultant" is an approved designation; or [a law corporation director's card omits "advocate and solicitor"]; or [a paralegal's card reads "Legal Executive" with nothing to show she is not a lawyer], which risks the impression PD 3.5.2 forbids. **C** [The description] [breaches / does not breach] [r 33 / PD 3.5.2 / s 75D LPA] and should be changed to an approved designation.

*Source note: r 33(1)–(2) PCR p53; r 33(3)–(4) PCR p54; PD 3.5.2 p1; s 75D LPA p202; s 33(1)(b) LPA p93; r 33(1) L8 p22; summary Cyril §V.2 (secondary).*

---

## Publicity and touting

### eth-w-0-29 — Publicity: expertise, past clients, success rates, comparisons
*Keywords: publicity, advertising, r 43, r 43(1), r 43(2), expertise, specialisation, success rate, 99%, comparison, criticism of other lawyers, "best of the best", "unlike other lawyers", past clients, confidentiality, judicial appointment, r 43(3), r 42, advertising agency, PD 6.2.1, TV commercial, complimentary advertising*

**I** The issue is whether [statement] complies with r 43 PCR. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 43(1)[(b)(ii) / (c) / (a)] PCR, which requires a legal practitioner publicising a practice to ensure that the publicity does not [mention the success rate of the lawyer or firm / compare or criticise the fees or quality of service of other lawyers] [/ that any claim to expertise can be justified].
**R**
When publicising a practice, a lawyer must ensure that any claim to expertise or
specialisation can be justified, that the publicity does not mention the lawyer's or
firm's success rate or any past client or case where that would breach confidentiality,
and that it does not compare or criticise the fees or quality of other lawyers
(r 43(1)(a)–(c) PCR). Expertise may be justified by qualifications, experience, proportion
of working time, success, complexity, significance of matters and peer assessment
(r 43(2) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 43(1) PCR applies, which states that when a legal practitioner publicises his or her practice, or that of a law practice of which he or she is a director, partner or employee, the legal practitioner must ensure that
>   - (a) any claim to expertise or specialisation can be justified;
>   - (b) the publicity does not make any direct or indirect mention of
>     - (i) any past case in which, or client for whom, the legal practitioner, law practice or any member of it had acted, if providing the information will breach any duty of confidentiality owed to a client or former client, or
>     - (ii) the success rate of the legal practitioner, the law practice or any member of it; and
>   - (c) the publicity does not make any comparison or criticism in relation to the fees charged, or the quality of the services provided, by any other legal practitioner or law practice.
> - Rule 43(2) states that, in justifying a claim to expertise or specialisation, the following factors may be taken into account:
>   - (a) academic qualifications;
>   - (b) experience;
>   - (c) proportion of working time involved;
>   - (d) level of success achieved;
>   - (e) complexity of law and practice;
>   - (f) significance of the matters involving the legal practitioner or the law practice; and
>   - (g) assessment by peers.
> - Rule 43(3) states that when a lawyer from a law practice is appointed to the judiciary, the practice's publicity must not refer to the appointment during the judicial tenure, though the name may appear if it is or forms part of the practice's name.
> - Rule 43(4) states that the Rules do not permit anything which may reasonably be regarded as touting. "Publicity" means any form of advertisement, including one printed in any medium or communicated through or retrievable from any mass medium or the Internet (r 2(1) PCR).
> - Rule 42(1) states that a legal practitioner must ensure that any publicity relating to his or her practice complies with the Rules, whether it is by the legal practitioner or by any other person on his or her behalf, and r 42(2) that a legal practitioner who becomes aware of any impropriety in such publicity must use best endeavours to procure its rectification or withdrawal and to prevent recurrence.

**P** PD 6.2.1 Part D states that a TV commercial is unbefitting the dignity of the profession under r 44(1)(b) if it suggests that other law practices overcharge or sets out price lists, though a general reference to fixed fees is not improper; Part E states that a law practice may accept complimentary newspaper advertising if it complies with r 43 and r 44, and in particular any description of specialisation must meet r 43(1)(a) and r 43(2).

**A** First, "[Unlike other lawyers / when other lawyers had failed / lower fees than other firms]" is a comparison with, and criticism of, other lawyers' quality or fees, breaching r 43(1)(c). Second, "[99% success rate]" is a direct mention of success rate, breaching r 43(1)(b)(ii) outright, and a small-print disclaimer cannot cure a prohibited statement. Third, naming [past client / case] breaches r 43(1)(b)(i) unless the client consented, so that no confidentiality is breached. Fourth, "[Expert litigator]" is permissible only if it can be justified on the r 43(2) factors, e.g. [25 years' disputes practice (b), most working time in litigation (c), complex cross-border cases (e)]. Under r 42(1)–(2), [Lawyer] is responsible even though [the advertising agency] drafted it, and must have it corrected. **C** [Statements X and Y] breach r 43(1) and may not be used; [Z] may be used if [justified under r 43(2) / reworded].

*Source note: r 2(1) "publicity" PCR p5; r 42 PCR p63, L9 p5; r 43 PCR p64–65, L9 p6–8; PD 6.2.1 Parts D–E p1–2, L9 p11. Tested PYP23 Q2.3, PS3 Q3.*

### eth-w-0-30 — Publicity: misleading, unverifiable or undignified; unjustified expectations
*Keywords: r 44, r 44(1), r 44(2), misleading, deceptive, inaccurate, false, unverifiable, material misrepresentation, material fact omitted, unjustified expectation of results, dignity of profession, disrepute, undesirable manner, "crush your enemies", "recover millions", testimonial, celebrity, clickbait, TV commercial, flyers, PD 6.2.1, PD 6.2.2, r 37*

**I** The issue is whether [statement] is misleading or unbefitting under r 44 PCR. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 44(1)(b) PCR, which forbids a legal practitioner to publicise his or her practice in a manner that may reasonably be regarded as misleading, deceptive, inaccurate, false or unbefitting the dignity of the legal profession.
**R** A lawyer must not publicise their practice in a way likely to
diminish public confidence in the profession, or that may reasonably be regarded as
misleading, deceptive, inaccurate, false or unbefitting the dignity of the profession
(r 44(1)(a)–(b) PCR). Publicity is misleading if it contains a material misrepresentation,
omits a material fact, contains unverifiable information, or is likely to create an
unjustified expectation about results (r 44(2)(a)–(d) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 44(1) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must not publicise his or her practice, or the practice of the law practice of which he or she is a director, partner or employee, in a manner which
>   - (a) is likely to diminish public confidence in the legal profession or otherwise bring it into disrepute;
>   - (b) may reasonably be regarded as being misleading, deceptive, inaccurate, false or unbefitting the dignity of the legal profession; or
>   - (c) the Council may determine to be an undesirable manner of publicising a practice.
> - Rule 44(2) states that publicity is misleading, deceptive, inaccurate or false if
>   - (a) it contains a material misrepresentation;
>   - (b) it omits to state a material fact;
>   - (c) it contains any information which cannot be verified; or
>   - (d) it is likely to create an unjustified expectation about the results that can be achieved by the legal practitioner or the law practice.
> - Part 5 is read in light of r 37: no publicity in circumstances that affect the dignity and standing of the profession.
> - Rule 42(1) makes the lawyer answerable for publicity by others on his or her behalf.

**P** PD 6.2.1 Part B states that press and TV advertisements are not touting and not, of themselves, unbefitting the dignity of the profession under r 44(1)(a)–(b). Part D gives examples of a misleading TV commercial: claiming to be a leading family practice without family expertise (a material misrepresentation); not stating that the practice handles only uncontested divorces (omitting a material fact); giving only a phone number without the practice's name (unverifiable); and saying the practice will recover party-and-party costs (an unjustified expectation). PD 6.2.2 Part A states that distributing flyers in public places is unbefitting the profession's dignity under r 44(1)(b), and Part C that a flyer saying only "Legal Services" with a phone number is unverifiable under r 44(2)(c).

**A** Take each statement against the limbs. "[Recover millions for you / these lawyers will change your life]" is likely to create an unjustified expectation about results (r 44(2)(d)). "[Never give up fighting for ten years]" or "[one secret technique]" cannot be verified and may suggest something that does not exist (r 44(2)(a), (c)). "[Crush your enemies / banks hate her]" is aggressive clickbait unbefitting the profession's dignity and likely to bring it into disrepute (r 44(1)(a)–(b)). A testimonial-style quote beside a celebrity the firm never acted for implies a client relationship that did not exist, which is a material misrepresentation (r 44(2)(a)). **C** [The statements] breach r 44(1)(b) read with r 44(2)[(a) / (c) / (d)], and [Lawyer] must have them withdrawn or rewritten in factual, verifiable terms under r 42(2).

*Source note: r 37 PCR p60; r 42 PCR p63; r 44 PCR p65–66, L9 p9–10; PD 6.2.1 Parts B, D p1–2; PD 6.2.2 Parts A, C p1. Tested PYP23 Q2.3, PS3 Q3.*

### eth-w-0-31 — Touting; rewarding referrers and reviews
*Keywords: touting, r 39, r 39(1), r 39(2), referral, referror, commission, consideration, discount for review, gift vouchers, s 83(2)(d), s 83(2)(e), s 83(2)(f), procuring employment, r 43(4), online referral scheme, GN 6.1.1, hyperlink, PD 6.1.2, flyers, direct solicitation, PD 6.2.2, Chong Wai Yen Michael, Wong Keng Leong Rayney, Yap Kok Kiong, Lillian Bay, Udeh Kumar, Lee Cheong Hoh*

**I** The issue is whether [arrangement] amounts to touting or rewarding a referrer. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 39(2)(b) PCR, which forbids a legal practitioner to reward a referror by any commission or other form of consideration [, and r 39(1), which forbids touting].
**R** A lawyer must
not tout, or do anything likely to lead to a reasonable inference of touting (r 39(1)
PCR). Where a client is referred, the lawyer must not reward the referrer by any commission
or other consideration, must advise independently, and must communicate directly with the
client (r 39(2)(b), (d), (g) PCR). Due cause for discipline includes giving or allowing the
retention, out of a fee, of any gratification for procuring legal business (s 83(2)(d)
LPA) or procuring employment through a person paid for it (s 83(2)(e) LPA).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 39(1) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner or law practice must not tout for business or do anything which is likely to lead to a reasonable inference that it was done for the purpose of touting.
> - Rule 39(2) states that, without prejudice to paragraph (1), where there is reason to believe that a client is referred by any other person, the legal practitioner or law practice
>   - (a) must maintain the independence and integrity of the profession by not permitting the referror to undermine its professional independence;
>   - (b) must not reward the referror by the payment of any commission or other form of consideration;
>   - (c) must not allow the referral to affect in any way the advice given to the client;
>   - (d) must advise the client impartially and independently;
>   - (e) must ensure that any wish to avoid offending the referror does not affect the advice;
>   - (f) must ensure that the referror does not influence any decision on the nature, style or extent of its practice; and
>   - (g) must communicate directly with the client to obtain or confirm instructions when providing advice and at all appropriate stages of the transaction.
> - Rule 43(4) states that the Rules do not permit anything which may reasonably be regarded as touting.
> - Section 83(2) LPA states that due cause is shown where an advocate and solicitor
>   - (d) has tendered or given or consented to retention, out of any fee payable for his or her services, of any gratification for having procured the employment in any legal business of himself or herself or any other advocate and solicitor;
>   - (e) has, directly or indirectly, procured or attempted to procure the employment of himself or herself or any advocate and solicitor through or by the instruction of any person to whom any remuneration for obtaining such employment has been given by him or her or agreed or promised to be so given; or
>   - (f) has accepted employment in any legal business through a person proclaimed a tout under any written law.
> - GN 6.1.1 paras 35–37 state that taking part in an Internet referral scheme which requires the practice to pay a fee or share fees for work referred breaches the LPA; that even where no fee is paid, taking part in an online introduction or referral service in a way that unfairly attracts work is improper under s 83(2)(b) or (h) LPA; and that demanding a referral fee from another practice for merely referring work is improper "brokering".
> - PD 6.1.2 states that third-party websites, such as property agents', may hyperlink to a law practice's website provided there is no form of financial arrangement between them, and the hyperlink's description must not suggest that the third party can give legal advice, that the practice was formed by it, or that the viewer must use the practice exclusively.
> - PD 6.2.2 Part A states that distributing flyers to the public in public places may reasonably be regarded as touting under r 43(4) unless the recipients asked for them, and that direct, in-person solicitation breaches r 39(1).

**P** Paying gratification to a company that procured conveyancing work through referral fees to estate agents is serious misconduct (*Law Society of Singapore v Chong Wai Yen Michael* [2012] 2 SLR 113). An agreement to pay a referral fee is in substance no different from touting for future work (*Wong Keng Leong Rayney v Law Society of Singapore* [2006] 4 SLR(R) 934). Gift vouchers sent to estate agents who referred clients were improper (*Law Society of Singapore v Yap Kok Kiong* [2006] SGDSC 14), as was paying an employee 10% of fees on files he brought in (*Law Society of Singapore v Lee Cheong Hoh* [2001] 1 SLR(R) 197). It is no defence that the "referror" was an investigator in a sting operation (*Law Society of Singapore v Lillian Bay Puay Joo* [2008] 2 SLR(R) 316). A lawyer must communicate directly with a referred client (*Law Society of Singapore v Udeh Kumar s/o Sethuraju* [2013] 3 SLR 875).

**A** First, is there a referral? Here [the contact / client] [referred the matter / is asked to share the website and post a review to bring in new clients]. Second, is the referror rewarded? A [5% payment to the contact / 5% fee discount / gift voucher] is a commission or "other form of consideration" under r 39(2)(b); a discount is also arguably a gratification retained out of the lawyer's fee under s 83(2)(d), given to procure further work. A paid review is also unverifiable and misleading publicity under r 44(2). Third, check the other r 39(2) duties: did [Lawyer] advise independently and communicate directly with the client (r 39(2)(d), (g))? Fourth, for a website link, is there any financial arrangement (PD 6.1.2)? **C** The arrangement breaches r 39(2)(b) and s 83(2)(d)[–(e)] LPA and must be ended.

*Source note: r 39 PCR p61–62, L9 p17; r 43(4) PCR p65; s 83(2)(d)–(f) LPA p218, L9 p23; GN 6.1.1 paras 33–37 p4; PD 6.1.2 p1, L9 p19; PD 6.2.2 Part A p1; Chong Wai Yen Michael, Wong Keng Leong Rayney, Lillian Bay, Lee Cheong Hoh, Udeh Kumar 2013 SYL p11; case summaries L9 p20–21 (Yap Kok Kiong only in L9 p21, not SYL); Lee Cheong Hoh facts Cyril §VI.2.2 (secondary). Tested PYP21 Q3.5, PS3 Q2.*

### eth-w-0-66 — Good causes, third-party publicity, free advice and publicity abroad: r 45–49
*Keywords: good cause, donation, sponsorship, r 45, acknowledgment, scholarship, prize, registered charity, third-party publicity, r 46, free legal advice, legal clinic, r 47, business cards, brochures, pro bono, publicity outside Singapore, r 48, r 49, jurisdiction, incidental receipt, PD 6.2.4, public appearances, seminars, non-legal publications, r 37*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer]'s [sponsorship / clinic / overseas advertising] complies with the PCR. **C** [Lawyer] [breached / complied with] r [45(2) / 47(3) / 48(2)] PCR, which [limits a public acknowledgment of a contribution to the lawyer's name, status and practice name / forbids distributing business cards at, or acting for a paying client met at, a free legal clinic / requires publicity abroad to obey local law].
**R** A lawyer or practice may contribute to good causes
and be publicly acknowledged, but must take reasonable steps to ensure the acknowledgment states
no more than the lawyer's name, that they are a lawyer and the practice's name (r 45(1)–(2)
PCR); prizes and scholarships may bear the endower's name (r 45(3)). A practice may be publicised
with a third party's publicity (r 46). A lawyer may give free advice through a facility for
public legal assistance, but must not publicise more than name, status and practice, must not
hand out business cards or brochures, and must not then act for that person except pro bono
(r 47 PCR). Publicity outside Singapore is not subject to r 43–47 but must obey local law
(r 48); publicity is conducted where it is reasonably expected to be received, not where receipt
is incidental (r 49). Media appearances and articles may identify the lawyer with relevant
qualifications (PD 6.2.4).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 45(1) PCR applies, which states that, subject to the rule,
>   - (a) any legal practitioner or law practice may contribute to good causes, whether by donation, sponsorship, subscription or the provision of free services; and
>   - (b) any contribution may be publicly acknowledged by the recipient.
> - Rule 45(2) states that the lawyer or practice must take reasonable steps to ensure that any public acknowledgment does not state any information about the lawyer or practice except
>   - (a) for a lawyer, the lawyer's name, the fact that he or she is a legal practitioner, and the name of the law practice of which he or she is a director, partner or employee; and
>   - (b) for a law practice, its name.
> - Rule 45(3) states that a lawyer or practice may endow prizes and scholarships at any school or educational institution and be identified in the prize's name as the endower.
> - Rule 45(4) states that "good cause" includes any registered charity and any other benevolent cause or cause concerned with the promotion of education, sports or the arts.
> - Rule 46 states that, subject to the Act and the Rules, a lawyer's or practice's practice may be publicised in, or in conjunction with, the publicity of any third party, whether or not a client.
> - Rule 47(1) states that a lawyer may give free legal advice to any person at or through any facility established to provide legal assistance to the public; r 47(2) that the lawyer must then take reasonable steps to ensure that no information about him or her is publicised except name, status as a legal practitioner, and the name of his or her law practice; and r 47(3) that the lawyer must not, in giving that free advice,
>   - (a) distribute business cards or any brochure, leaflet or pamphlet about his or her practice; or
>   - (b) act for any person to whom he or she gave such free advice, unless acting pro bono.
> - Rule 48(1) states that a lawyer may publicise his or her practice in a country other than Singapore, and r 43 to r 47 then do not apply; r 48(2) that the lawyer must ensure that such publicity is not conducted contrary to the laws of that country.
> - Rule 49 states that publicity
>   - (a) is conducted in a jurisdiction in which it is reasonably expected to be received or accessible in the normal course of events; and
>   - (b) is not conducted in a jurisdiction where its receipt there is incidental.
> - PD 6.2.4 Part A states that, subject to Part 5, a lawyer who appears on radio or TV, gives a talk or lecture, gives a press interview, writes an article or letter to the press, or edits or writes a book may be identified by name, status as a lawyer and practice name, with particulars of special qualifications or knowledge directly relevant to the subject; Part B that a practice may organise, advertise and charge for a public seminar at its premises if it complies with the PCR; and Part C that r 47 does not apply to answering legal questions in non-legal publications, which are not a "facility", though the same identification limits apply and a disclaimer is advisable.

**P** All publicity remains subject to r 37 (no publicity affecting the dignity and standing of the profession) and r 44.

**A** Take the activity. For a donation, the [banner / programme] [lists the firm's practice areas and phone number], which goes beyond name, status and practice name, so [Lawyer] failed to take reasonable steps under r 45(2) [unless the recipient added it despite those steps]. For a clinic, [Lawyer] [handed out cards / took on a paying client met there], which r 47(3)(a)–(b) forbids. For overseas advertising, ask under r 49 where it is reasonably expected to be received: [a Singapore-facing website is conducted in Singapore, so r 43–47 apply / an ad in a foreign newspaper reaching Singapore only incidentally is not], and r 48(2) requires compliance with local law. **C** [Lawyer] [breached r 45(2) / r 47(3)] and should correct it [/ complied with r 45–49].

*Source note: r 37 PCR p60; r 45–49 PCR p66–68, L9 p12–15; PD 6.2.4 Parts A–C p1; summary Cyril §VI.1.9–1.14 (secondary).*

### eth-w-0-67 — Conveyancing referral agreements: r 40
*Keywords: referral agreement, conveyancing, r 40, in writing, referror undertakes, terminate, must terminate, no further referrals, free conveyancing, property agent, bank, client's choice of lawyer, r 39, motor accident referrals, PD 7.4.3 Part D, Chong Wai Yen Michael, Wong Keng Leong Rayney*

**I** The issue is whether [the firm]'s arrangement with [referror] for conveyancing work complies with r 40 PCR. **C** [The firm] breached r 40(1) PCR, which requires any agreement for the referral of conveyancing work to be in writing and to contain the terms it prescribes [, and r 40(2), which requires the firm to terminate it on breach].
**R** Besides r 39, any agreement for referral of
conveyancing work must be in writing and must provide that the referrer undertakes to comply
with the Rules; that the firm may terminate if it believes the referrer is in breach; that the
referrer's publicity must not suggest the conveyancing is free, that charges differ depending
on whether the client uses the firm, or that the referrer's other services depend on using the
firm; and that the referrer must not impair the client's right to choose a lawyer (r 40(1)
PCR). The firm must terminate on breach, may finish existing matters, and must accept no further
referrals (r 40(2)–(3) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 40(1) PCR applies, which states that, in addition to r 39, when a legal practitioner or law practice enters into any agreement for the referral of any conveyancing service, it must ensure that the agreement is made in writing and contains the following terms:
>   - (a) the referror undertakes to comply with the Rules;
>   - (b) the legal practitioner or law practice is entitled to terminate the agreement if there is reason to believe that the referror is in breach of any of its terms;
>   - (c) any publicity of the referror which refers to any service that may be provided by the legal practitioner or law practice must not suggest
>     - (i) that the conveyancing service is free,
>     - (ii) that different charges are payable depending on whether the client instructs the legal practitioner or law practice, or
>     - (iii) that the availability or price of any other service offered by the referror, or a related party, is conditional on the client instructing the legal practitioner or law practice; and
>   - (d) the referror must not do anything to impair the client's right not to appoint the legal practitioner or law practice, and must not influence in any way the client's right to appoint a lawyer of the client's choice.
> - Rule 40(2) states that the legal practitioner or law practice must terminate the agreement if
>   - (a) the referror is in breach of any of those terms; or
>   - (b) there is reason to believe that the legal practitioner or law practice is in breach of any such term.
> - Rule 40(3) states that after termination it
>   - (a) may continue to act in matters instructed before termination; but
>   - (b) must not accept any further referrals from the referror.
> - Rule 39(2)(b) separately forbids rewarding the referror by any commission or other form of consideration.
> - PD 7.4.3 Part D para 2 applies the same requirements to agreements for referral of non-injury and personal injury motor accident work, adding an express term that the lawyer will neither accept nor pay any commission, referral fee or other consideration.

**P** Paying any commission to a referror is prohibited in any case (r 39(2)(b) PCR; s 83(2)(d)–(e) LPA; *Law Society of Singapore v Chong Wai Yen Michael* [2012] 2 SLR 113), and an agreement to pay a referral fee is in substance touting (*Wong Keng Leong Rayney v Law Society of Singapore* [2006] 4 SLR(R) 934).

**A** First, is the agreement in writing with all four terms? Here [the arrangement is oral / has no undertaking by the referror]. Second, does the referror's publicity breach r 40(1)(c)? Here [the agent's flyer says "free legal fees when you buy through us"], which suggests the conveyancing is free (i) [or that prices differ (ii) / other services depend on using the firm (iii)]. Third, is the client's choice impaired under r 40(1)(d)? Here [the agent tells buyers they must use the firm]. Fourth, is the referror paid anything, contrary to r 39(2)(b)? Once any term is breached, r 40(2) obliges the firm to terminate; it may finish existing matters but take no further referrals (r 40(3)). **C** The arrangement breaches r 40(1), and the firm must terminate it under r 40(2) and accept no further referrals from [referror].

*Source note: r 39–40 PCR p61–63, L9 p17–18; s 83(2)(d)–(e) LPA p218; PD 7.4.3 Part D para 2 p3–4; Chong Wai Yen Michael, Wong Keng Leong Rayney SYL p11, L9 p20–21; Cyril §VI.2.3–2.4 (secondary).*

---

## Fees and client money

### eth-w-0-32 — Overcharging; fixed and non-refundable fees
*Keywords: overcharging, r 17(7), r 17(8), reasonable legal practitioner in good faith, standing and experience, fixed fee, agreed fee, gross sum, non-refundable deposit, disproportionate, PD 5.2.2, s 111 LPA, s 113 LPA, unfair or unreasonable, declared void, reopen agreement, Syn Kok Kay, Ang Chin Peng, Low Yong Sen*

**I** The issue is whether the agreed fee of [$X] amounts to overcharging. **C** [Lawyer] [breached / would breach] r 17(7) PCR, which forbids charging a fee that constitutes overcharging even if a fee agreement permits it.
**R** A lawyer must charge fairly (r 17(1) PCR) and must not charge
a fee that is overcharging even if a fee agreement permits it (r 17(7) PCR). There is
overcharging if a reasonable lawyer could not in good faith charge the amount, given the
lawyer's standing and experience, the nature of the work, the time needed, the client's
instructions and other circumstances (r 17(8) PCR).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 17(7) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must not charge any fee or disbursements, or render a bill (whether or not subject to assessment) for an amount, which constitutes overcharging, even if there is a fee agreement that permits the charging of the fee, disbursements or amount.
> - Rule 17(8) states that there is overcharging if a reasonable legal practitioner cannot in good faith charge the fee, disbursements or amount, taking into account all of
>   - (a) the legal practitioner's standing and experience;
>   - (b) the nature of the legal work concerned;
>   - (c) the time necessary to undertake the legal work;
>   - (d) the instructions and requirements of the client concerned; and
>   - (e) any other relevant circumstances.
> - The guiding principle is that a lawyer must act in the client's best interests and charge the client fairly for work done (r 17(1)).
> - Section 111(1) LPA allows a solicitor to make an agreement in writing with a client on the amount and manner of payment of costs for contentious business, by a gross sum or otherwise, at the same rate as, or a greater or lesser rate than, the solicitor would otherwise be entitled to; s 111(2) requires it to be signed by the client and makes it subject to Part 8.
> - Under s 113, every question on the agreement's validity or effect may be examined and determined, and the agreement enforced or set aside, on application to the court (s 113(2)); it may be enforced if it is in all respects fair and reasonable (s 113(3)); if its terms are deemed unfair or unreasonable, it may be declared void (s 113(4)) and the costs assessed as if there were no agreement (s 113(5)); and within 12 months after payment the court may, if special circumstances require it, reopen the agreement, order assessment and order the whole or part of the amount received to be repaid (s 113(7)).
> - PD 5.2.2 Part A states that s 111 does not give solicitors a carte blanche to agree an unreasonable fee, and that overcharging, whether in a bill of costs or otherwise, may amount to professional misconduct; Part B states that a lawyer is not, in all cases, entitled to keep fees collected as a non-refundable deposit or retainer irrespective of the amount of work done, even if the client agreed, and that members must comply with ss 111–113.

**P** A fee agreement does not end the inquiry into whether the amount charged is fair; a fee fixed as a percentage of the estate's value, with no correlation to the complexity of the matter, time spent or documents prepared, was overcharging (*Law Society of Singapore v Ang Chin Peng* [2013] 1 SLR 946). Overcharging hidden by billing through an apparently independent conveyancing secretary connected to the lawyer's brother was misconduct (*Law Society of Singapore v Low Yong Sen* [2009] 1 SLR(R) 802). In assessing gravity the court considers (a) the extent of the overcharging, by quantum and disproportionality; (b) the solicitor's intention during the period of overcharging; (c) the solicitor's conduct after the overcharging; and (d) other offender-specific aggravating and mitigating factors, such as seniority, antecedents, dishonesty, restitution and willingness to offer the bill for assessment (*Law Society of Singapore v Syn Kok Kay* [2023] SGHC 7 at [38], discussed at [28]–[37]); there a $1.34m bill taxed down to $288,000 led to three years' suspension for the overcharging (at [52]).

**A** Here the fee of [$4m] is [two thirds] of the [$6m] claim, payable in full even if the case settles early. Walk through r 17(8): (a) [Lawyer] is [a senior partner / a junior], which [may / cannot] justify a premium; (b) the work is [a straightforward claim for the price of goods]; (c) the time needed is [modest, and nil if it settles early]; (d) the client [asked only for prompt recovery / required senior counsel and urgent work]; and (e) [other circumstances]. Unless complexity, seniority or the client's requirements justify it, no reasonable lawyer could charge this in good faith. Being an agreed gross sum under s 111 does not save it: the court may declare it void as unfair or unreasonable under s 113(4) and order assessment, and under PD 5.2.2 Part B a "non-refundable" fee may not be kept regardless of work done. **C** The term [breaches / risks breaching] r 17(7); the fee should be tied to work done or reduced.

*Source note: r 17(1), (7)–(8) PCR p31, p33, L10 p39–40; s 111 LPA p270; s 113(2)–(7) LPA p271–272; PD 5.2.2 Parts A–B p1; Ang Chin Peng and Low Yong Sen SYL p12, facts Cyril Master Notes §VII.4.5 (secondary); Syn Kok Kay SYL p12, gravity factors [38] and outcome [52] read from the judgment on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026). Tested PYP21 Q3.1.*

### eth-w-0-33 — Contingency and success fees
*Keywords: contingency fee, success fee, bonus, uplift fee, r 18, s 107(1)(b) LPA, payment only in the event of success, interest in the subject matter, proportionate to amount recovered, champerty, maintenance, s 107(3) LPA, conditional fee agreement, r 18A, s 115A, s 115B, prescribed proceedings, GN 5.6.1, PD 5.2.1, party and party costs, Kurubalan, Lau See Jin Jeffrey, Legis Point, impecunious client, equity in lieu of fees, shares in lieu of fees, PD 5.1.1, shares as security, no win less fee, discount if the case is lost, discount on conviction, criminal fees*

**I** The issue is whether the [success bonus / percentage fee] is a prohibited contingency fee. **C** The [bonus / percentage fee] breaches s 107(1)(b) LPA [and r 18 PCR], which prohibit an agreement for contentious proceedings that stipulates for or contemplates payment only in the event of success [and negotiating for remuneration proportionate to the amount the client recovers].
**R** A solicitor must not enter an agreement for contentious
proceedings that stipulates for or contemplates payment only in the event of success
(s 107(1)(b) Legal Profession Act 1966), and must not negotiate for an interest in the subject
matter or for remuneration proportionate to the amount the client recovers (r 18 PCR). The
only exception is a conditional fee agreement complying with Part 8A, which may provide an
"uplift fee" payable in specified circumstances (s 107(3AA), s 115A(1) LPA), but only in
prescribed proceedings: arbitration, certain SICC proceedings and related court and mediation
proceedings (GN 5.6.1 para 8).

> **Rule in full**
> - Section 107(1) of the Legal Profession Act 1966 applies, which states that a solicitor must not
>   - (a) purchase or agree to purchase the interest, or any part of the interest, of his or her client or of any party in any suit, action or other contentious proceeding brought or to be brought or maintained; or
>   - (b) enter into any agreement by which he or she is retained or employed to prosecute any suit or action or other contentious proceeding which stipulates for or contemplates payment only in the event of success in that suit, action or proceeding. Nothing in the Act gives validity to any such purchase or agreement (s 107(2)), and a solicitor is subject to the law of maintenance and champerty like any other person (s 107(3)).
> - Rule 18 PCR states that a legal practitioner or law practice must not enter into any negotiations with a client
>   - (a) for an interest in the subject matter of litigation or of any other contentious proceedings; or
>   - (b) except to the extent permitted by any applicable scale of costs, for remuneration proportionate to the amount which may be recovered by the client in the proceedings.
> - PD 5.2.1 Part C states that any fee arrangement providing for solicitor and client costs contingent on the amount of party and party costs recovered by the client breaches s 107 and r 18, because the solicitor would have an interest in the subject matter of the litigation.
> - There is one statutory exception, which the syllabus marks non-examinable.
> - Section 107(3AA) states that s 107 does not prevent a solicitor from entering into a conditional fee agreement that complies with Part 8A, and r 18A(1) states that rules 17 and 18 do not prevent entering into such an agreement or charging fees payable only in the circumstances it specifies; r 18A(2) keeps rules 17 and 18 applying in all other respects.
> - A "conditional fee agreement" is an agreement on the remuneration and costs of contentious proceedings that provides for them, or part of them, to be payable only in specified circumstances, and may provide for an uplift fee (s 115A(1)); it may be made only in "prescribed proceedings" (s 115B(1)), which in broad terms are international and domestic arbitration, certain SICC proceedings, and court and mediation proceedings related to them (GN 5.6.1 para 8); it must be in writing and signed by the client, and must not provide for remuneration payable as a percentage or proportion of the damages or other amounts recovered (s 115B(4)(a)–(b)).
> - GN 5.6.1 para 6 states that contingency fee agreements, where the lawyer takes an agreed percentage of the sum recovered, continue to be prohibited.
> - GN 5.6.1 paras 11–13 state that a conditional fee agreement may be "no win, no fee" or "no win, less fee"; in a "no win, less fee" agreement, a discounted professional fee is payable if the specified circumstances are not met.
> - PD 5.1.1 Part A(b)(i) states that a law practice cannot agree to accept equity in lieu of fees in a contentious matter where the agreement amounts to a contingency fee arrangement, and that an agreement where the acquiring of shares is contingent on the outcome of a contentious matter, or where the shares are themselves the subject matter of the litigation, amounts to a contingency fee arrangement under s 107 LPA; it also reminds members of s 107(3) on maintenance and champerty.
> - PD 5.1.1 Part A(b)(iii) states that the law practice must not put its commercial interests above the client's interest, nor allow its judgement, objectivity and loyalty to the client to be compromised by its equity involvement, and that rules 20–22 PCR on conflicts should be carefully considered.

**P** The traditional rationale is that such arrangements would tempt the champertous maintainer to subvert the course of justice, and effective representation needs the lawyer to keep enough detachment to discharge his duty to the court (*Law Society of Singapore v Kurubalan s/o Manickam Rengaraju* [2013] 4 SLR 91 at [42], [45]); there the agreement gave the lawyer 30% or 40% of the amount recovered (at [2]). It is permissible, and even honourable, to act for an impecunious client knowing that fees will likely be recovered only if the claim succeeds or a costs order is obtained (*Kurubalan* at [2]), provided the lawyer has examined the case and honestly concluded that there is a good cause of action or defence which, but for the client's impecuniosity, would likely be litigated. A fee of 20% of damages, rising to 25% if damages exceeded $5m, was a contingency fee; the court noted the lack of attendance notes and rejected a lower standard for small firms (*Law Society of Singapore v Lau See Jin Jeffrey* [2017] SGHC 30). Compare *Legis Point LLC v Tay Choon Ai* [2017] SGHC 325 at [51]–[56], where a cap of $28,000 on the firm's fees and disbursements applied whether the client won or lost, so fees were not payable only on success and the cap did not contravene s 107(1)(b) or r 18; the court contrasted an improper arrangement limiting fees to the costs recovered from the other side.

**A** First, the matter is a [court suit], which is contentious proceedings. Second, the extra [$500,000] is payable only if [the suit is won / settles above $X], so that part of the agreement stipulates for payment only in the event of success under s 107(1)(b); a fixed base fee does not save the success element. Third, if the fee is [a percentage of the amount recovered], it is also remuneration proportionate to the recovery under r 18(b). Fourth, the conditional fee agreement exception does not apply: [an ordinary court suit is not prescribed proceedings / the agreement is a percentage of damages, which s 115B(4)(b) forbids]. Fifth, this is not the impecunious-client situation, because [the client can pay and the fee is expressly conditional on success]. A discount if the case is lost [or the accused is convicted after trial] is the same thing seen from the other side: it is the "no win, less fee" form that GN 5.6.1 paras 11–13 treat as a conditional fee agreement, so outside prescribed proceedings (and criminal proceedings never are) part of the fee is payable only on success, within s 107(1)(b). Shares: if [Lawyer] is to take [shares in the client's company] in lieu of fees, or as security, and [their acquisition depends on the outcome / the shares are the subject of the suit], PD 5.1.1 Part A(b)(i) treats that as a contingency fee arrangement, and the shareholding also engages r 22. **C** The bonus breaches s 107(1)(b) [and r 18(b)] and should be removed.

*Source note: s 107 LPA p265–266; s 115A(1) LPA p275–276; s 115B(1), (4) LPA p276–277; r 18, r 18A PCR p34; PD 5.2.1 Part C p1; GN 5.6.1 paras 6, 8 and illustrations p1–2, paras 11–13 p2; PD 5.1.1 Part A(b)(i) p2, (iii) p3; Kurubalan L10 p45–47 (PD 5.2.1 cites it as [2013] SGHC 135), impecunious-client condition L10 p47; Lau See Jin Jeffrey L10 p48; Legis Point SYL p12 ("C.f."), holding [51]–[56] read from the judgment on eLitigation (3 Nov 2026; Cyril's "cap at party and party costs" gloss is wrong). SYL p13 marks r 18A conditional fee agreements non-examinable. No case decides a bonus on top of a fixed fee, or a discount payable on failure; the conclusion rests on s 107(1)(b), s 107(3AA), s 115B and GN 5.6.1. Tested PYP21 Q3.2, PYP18 Q1(b), PYP20 Q1(b), PYP20 Q2(c).*

### eth-w-0-34 — Sharing fees with unauthorised persons
*Keywords: fee sharing, r 19, unauthorised person, s 32(2) LPA, practising certificate, paralegal, GN 3.7.1, 50/50 split, commission, expired practising certificate, Tan See Leh Jonathan, debt collectors, PD 5.4.1, referral commission, r 39(2)(b)*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer]'s arrangement with [X] shares fees with an unauthorised person. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 19(1) PCR, which forbids a lawyer to share fees with, or pay a commission to, any unauthorised person for legal work performed by the lawyer.
**R** A lawyer or law practice must not
share fees with, or pay a commission to, any unauthorised person for legal work performed
by the lawyer or practice (r 19(1)–(2) PCR). An unauthorised person is anyone who is not on
the roll with a current practising certificate, which includes paralegals (s 32(2) Legal
Profession Act 1966; GN 3.7.1).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 19(1) PCR applies, which states that a legal practitioner must not share the legal practitioner's fees with, or pay a commission to, any unauthorised person for any legal work performed by the legal practitioner; r 19(2) imposes the same prohibition on a law practice. "Unauthorised person" has the meaning in s 32(2) LPA (r 2(1) PCR), which states that a person is an unauthorised person unless he or she is
>   - (a) a person whose name is on the roll of advocates and solicitors and who has in force a practising certificate; or
>   - (b) a person whose name is on the roll of lawyers (NP), who has in force a provisional practising certificate and who, when carrying out the act in question, is under the supervision of a solicitor.
> - GN 3.7.1 paras 1–2 state that a "paralegal" includes a legal executive, legal secretary or legal clerk and any other employee who performs paralegal functions without a practising certificate, and that paralegals fall within the category of unauthorised persons.
> - PD 5.4.1 states that lawyers and law practices are not to engage, directly or indirectly, the services of debt collectors (any person engaged in any business of collecting debts, or who regularly collects or attempts to collect debts owed to another) to recover outstanding legal fees and expenses, because
>   - (a) debt collectors may use abusive, deceptive and unfair practices and are not bound by professional standards;
>   - (b) their remuneration may breach the rules on fee sharing and commissions under r 19;
>   - (c) their use may breach confidentiality and derogate from the dignity and standing of the profession; and
>   - (d) lawyers owe fiduciary obligations to clients and the courts are the ultimate arbiters of the recovery of legal fees.
> - Separately, r 39(2)(b) states that where there is reason to believe that a client was referred by any other person, the lawyer must not reward the referror by the payment of any commission or other form of consideration.

**P** A lawyer who agreed to share about 50% of his fees with a paralegal whose practising certificate had lapsed, and who held himself out as a solicitor, committed serious misconduct: an unauthorised person who holds himself out commits an offence and cannot recover fees, the fee agreement facilitated the offences, and the law makes no distinction between types of unauthorised persons (*Law Society of Singapore v Tan See Leh Jonathan* [2020] SGHC 102).

**A** First, is [X] an unauthorised person? [The paralegal is not on the roll with a current practising certificate / his certificate has lapsed], so yes. Second, is there sharing of fees or a commission? [Lawyer] and [X] split [50/50] the fees on [non-contentious matters]. Third, is it for legal work performed by the lawyer or practice? [The fees are the firm's professional fees for that work.] That [X] does the work makes it worse, not better, since it shows [X] is practising in substance. The further [5%] paid to the referrer is a commission to an unauthorised person under r 19 and a reward for a referral under r 39(2)(b). [If the firm passes overdue bills to a debt collector paid a percentage of recoveries, that breaches PD 5.4.1 and may breach r 19.] **C** [Lawyer] breached r 19(1) [and the firm r 19(2)], and the 5% to the referrer also breaches r 39(2)(b).

*Source note: r 2(1) PCR p6; r 19 PCR p35; r 39(2)(b) PCR p61; s 32(2) LPA p92, L10 p52; GN 3.7.1 paras 1–2 p1; PD 5.4.1 p1, L10 p55; Tan See Leh Jonathan SYL p13, L10 p54. Tested PS3 Q2.*

### eth-w-0-68 — Client money and the Solicitors' Accounts Rules
*Keywords: client money, client's money, client account, r 16 PCR, LPSAR, Solicitors' Accounts Rules, pay in without delay, r 3(1), r 6, r 9 exceptions, agreed fee, office account, r 7 withdrawal, bill of costs, two working days, PD 3.3.1, r 8, two signatories, $5,000, book-keeper, $30,000, 3 years' practice, records, monthly reconciliation, 6 years, GN 3.3.1, Dhanwant Singh, Yong Wei Kuen Paul, Tay Choon Leng John, Lim Tean, Tan Chwee Wan Allan, Zulkifli*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] dealt properly with money received for [client]. **C** [Lawyer] breached r [3(1) / 7 / 8] of the Legal Profession (Solicitors' Accounts) Rules (LPSAR) and r 16 PCR, which require client's money to be paid without delay into a client account and to be drawn from it only as the rules permit.
**R** A lawyer must hold money
entrusted by a client in a way that protects the client, is accountable for it, and must
promptly notify the client of money received and render accounts on request (r 16(1)–(3) PCR).
Client's money — money held for a client, other than trust money, money belonging only to the
solicitor, and conveyancing money — must be paid without delay into a client account (r 2, r 3
LPSAR). Only permitted money may go into a client account (r 6), and money may be drawn only for
payments to or for the client, reimbursement, on the client's authority, or for costs after a
bill or written notice (r 7 LPSAR; for costs, after two working days' notice, PD 3.3.1).
Withdrawals over $5,000 need two solicitors' signatures, and no solicitor with less than three years' practice may sign any withdrawal (r 8(5), (7) LPSAR). Proper records must be kept and reconciled monthly (r 11).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 16 PCR applies.
> - The lawyer must ensure that money a client has entrusted to the lawyer, or made the lawyer responsible for, is held in a way that protects the client's interests (r 16(1)), is accountable to the client for it (r 16(2)), and must
>   - (a) promptly notify the client whenever the lawyer receives any money (including conveyancing money) or securities to be held on the client's behalf; and
>   - (b) expeditiously render statements of accounts when the client requests (r 16(3)); these duties apply to a law practice too (r 16(4)).
> - Under r 2(1) LPSAR, "client's money" is money held or received by a solicitor on account of a person for whom he is acting, as a solicitor or in connection with his practice, as agent, bailee, stakeholder or in any other capacity, other than
>   - (a) money held on account of the trustees of a trust of which the solicitor is solicitor-trustee;
>   - (b) money to which the only person entitled is the solicitor himself or, for a firm, one or more of its partners; or
>   - (c) conveyancing money or anticipatory conveyancing money.
> - A "client account" is a current or deposit account in the solicitor's name at a bank, or a deposit account with an approved finance company, with the word "client" in its title, and "solicitor" includes a Singapore law practice.
> - Rule 3(1) states that, subject to r 9, every solicitor who holds or receives client's money shall without delay pay it into a client account.
> - Rule 6(1) states that no money other than money which rules 3(1), 4 and 5(3) require or permit to be paid in shall be paid into a client account, and r 6(2) requires money paid in wrongly to be withdrawn without delay on discovery.
> - Rule 9 sets out the exceptions: there is no obligation to pay in client's money
>   - (a) received in cash and paid out without delay in cash to or for the client;
>   - (b) received as a cheque endorsed over to or for the client without passing through a bank account; or
>   - (c) paid into a separate account in the client's name or of a person the client designates in writing (r 9(1)); and the solicitor shall not pay into a client account money
>   - (a) which the client for his own convenience asks in writing to be withheld from it;
>   - (b) for payment of a debt due to the solicitor from the client or reimbursement of money spent for the client; or
>   - (c) expressly paid to him
>     - (i) on account of costs for which a bill or other written intimation has been delivered, or
>     - (ii) as an agreed fee, or on account of an agreed fee, for business undertaken or to be undertaken (r 9(2)).
> - Rule 7(1)(a) states that client's money may be drawn from a client account only if it is
>   - (i) properly required for a payment to or on behalf of the client;
>   - (ii) properly required to reimburse money the solicitor spent on the client's behalf;
>   - (iii) drawn on the client's authority;
>   - (iv) properly required for or towards payment of the solicitor's costs where a bill of costs or other written intimation of the amount of the costs incurred has been delivered to the client and the client has been notified that money held for him will be applied towards or in satisfaction of such costs; or
>   - (v) transferred to another client account; and r 7(2) states that the amount drawn must not exceed the money held for that client.
> - PD 3.3.1 states that before deducting costs the solicitor must have
>   - (a) delivered a bill or other written intimation of the costs;
>   - (b) notified the client that the amount will be deducted; and
>   - (c) allowed two working days to lapse after the notification before transferring the amount out of the client account.
> - Rule 8(1) states that, except as provided under r 7, no money may be drawn from a client account without the Council's specific written authority; r 8(4) and (4A) bar withdrawals by cash or bearer cheque (without the court's permission), by ATM or by telephone banking.
> - Rule 8(5) states that no sum exceeding $5,000 may be drawn except
>   - (a) on a cheque or other instruction signed by 2 solicitors; or
>   - (b) by an online digital payment authorised by 2 solicitors under the Council's practice directions; but r 8(6) disapplies this where the solicitor has engaged an approved book-keeper under r 11(8) and the sum does not exceed $30,000.
> - Rule 8(7) states that a solicitor must not sign or authorise any withdrawal from a client account if
>   - (a) he has been in practice in Singapore, or employed as a Judicial Service, Legal Service or PD Officer, for less than 3 years in aggregate;
>   - (b) he does not hold a current practising certificate; or
>   - (c) his practising certificate is subject to a condition prohibiting it.
> - Rule 11 requires properly written-up books showing all dealings with client's money separately for each client (r 11(1)), a reconciliation of the clients' cash book with the clients' bank statements at least once every month (r 11(4)), and preservation of all such records and bank statements for at least 6 years (r 11(6)).
> - GN 3.3.1 states that money belonging only to a sole proprietor, director or partner of the law practice is not client's money and must not be deposited in the client account, because a solicitor must separate his own money from client's money.

**P** The rules protect the public by imposing obligations on solicitors to keep client money safe, and a solicitor cannot contract out of them (*Law Society of Singapore v Dhanwant Singh* [2019] SGHC 290); they also maintain the legitimacy and standing of the profession (*Law Society of Singapore v Yong Wei Kuen Paul* [2020] SGHC 66). An erroneous deposit of client's money into the office account breaches r 3(1) (*Law Society of Singapore v Tan Chwee Wan Allan* [2007] 4 SLR(R) 699). Client's money paid into the office account was not excused as an "agreed fee" under r 9(2)(c)(ii) where there was no written evidence of a fee agreement and the oral evidence was unsatisfactory (*Law Society of Singapore v Tay Choon Leng John* [2012] 3 SLR 150). Ignorance of the rules is no defence (*Law Society of Singapore v Lim Tean* [2026] SGHC 78 at [55]–[63]). Supervising the client account is a non-delegable responsibility of the equity partners (*Law Society of Singapore v Zulkifli bin Mohd Amin* [2011] 2 SLR 620).

**A** First, is the money client's money? [The deposit was paid by the client for the purchase / the settlement sum was received for the client], so it is not the solicitor's own money and no r 9 exception applies [there is no written agreed fee and no prior bill]. Second, under r 3(1) it had to go into the client account without delay, but [Lawyer] [put it into the office account / held it for two weeks]. Third, on withdrawal, [Lawyer] [took fees without delivering a bill or written intimation and notifying the client / did not wait two working days under PD 3.3.1 / drew $[12,000] on one signature with no approved book-keeper / had it signed by an associate of [two] years' standing], contrary to r 7(1)(a)(iv) and r 8(5) or (7). Fourth, [Lawyer] [did not tell the client the money had arrived], contrary to r 16(3)(a), and [the monthly reconciliation was not done], contrary to r 11(4). **C** [Lawyer] breached r 3(1) [/ r 7 / r 8] LPSAR and r 16 PCR, and must restore the money to the client account at once.

*Source note: r 16 PCR p30, L10 p8; LPSAR r 2(1) p2–5, r 3 p5, r 4 p6, r 6 p7, r 7 p8–9, r 8 p9–11, r 9 p11–12, r 11 p12–15; PD 3.3.1 p1; GN 3.3.1 paras 1–3, 7 p1; Dhanwant Singh and Yong Wei Kuen Paul L10 p10, SYL p12; Tan Chwee Wan Allan L10 p9; Tay Choon Leng John SYL p12, facts Cyril Master Notes §VII.1 (secondary); Lim Tean SYL p12; Zulkifli L10 p25.*

### eth-w-0-69 — Anti-money laundering: customer due diligence and suspicious transactions
*Keywords: money laundering, terrorism financing, proliferation financing, AML Rules, Part 5A LPA, relevant matter, anonymous account, customer due diligence, CDD, verify identity, beneficial owner, politically-exposed individual, PEP, enhanced due diligence, source of wealth, source of funds, senior management approval, high-risk jurisdiction, FATF, inability to complete CDD, suspicious transaction report, STR, STRO, tipping off, records 5 years, PD 3.2.1, Chan Chun Hwee Allan*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] met the anti-money-laundering duties in [matter]. **C** [Lawyer] breached r [6 / 8 / 5 / 15] of the Legal Profession (Prevention of Money Laundering, Financing of Terrorism and Proliferation Financing) Rules 2015 (AML Rules), which require a lawyer to identify and verify the client and its beneficial owners before or while establishing the business relationship, and to report reasonable suspicions.
**R** Before or while establishing a business relationship, a lawyer must
identify and verify the client using reliable independent sources, take reasonable measures
to find out whether the client is a politically exposed person, identify and verify any
beneficial owner of an entity client, and understand its ownership and control (AML Rules
r 6, r 8, r 11). Enhanced due diligence is required in higher-risk cases, such as a foreign
politically exposed person or a high-risk jurisdiction (r 12–13). Where there are reasonable
grounds to suspect money laundering, the lawyer must file a suspicious transaction report, and
must consider whether to continue the relationship; if it continues, the reasons must be
documented and the risk managed (r 5). If due diligence would tip off the client, the lawyer
need not perform it but must file a report (r 16). Records must be kept for at least five
years (r 19).

> **Rule in full**
> - Part 5A LPA and the AML Rules apply when a lawyer prepares for or carries out a transaction concerning a "relevant matter" (PD 3.2.1 para 1.5), which s 70A(2) LPA defines as
>   - (a) the acquisition, divestment or any other dealing of any interest in real estate;
>   - (b) the management of a client's moneys, securities or other assets, or of bank, savings or securities accounts;
>   - (c) the creation, operation or management of any company, corporation, partnership, society, trust or other legal entity or legal arrangement;
>   - (d) the acquisition, merger, sale or disposal of any company, partnership, sole proprietorship, business trust or other business entity; or
>   - (e) any matter that is unusual in the ordinary course of business, having regard to
>     - (i) its complexity,
>     - (ii) the quantum involved,
>     - (iii) any apparent economic or lawful purpose, and
>     - (iv) the business and risk profile of the client.
> - A lawyer must not open or maintain any account for, or hold and receive moneys from, an anonymous source or a client with an obviously fictitious name (s 70B), and must perform the prescribed customer due diligence measures (s 70C).
> - Rule 6(1) AML Rules states that a lawyer must
>   - (a) ascertain the identity of the client;
>   - (b) verify the client's identity using objectively reliable and independent source documents, data or information;
>   - (c) take reasonable measures to determine whether the client is a politically-exposed individual, or a family member or close associate of one; and
>   - (d) identify and, if appropriate, obtain information on the purpose and intended nature of the business relationship; for an entity, r 6(2) requires its name, legal form, proof of existence, constitutional documents, senior management, registered office and principal place of business.
> - Rule 7 requires the lawyer to ascertain whether anyone purports to act for the client, or the client acts for another, and to verify that person's identity and authority.
> - Rule 8(1) states that for an entity or legal arrangement the lawyer must
>   - (a) ascertain whether it has any beneficial owner;
>   - (b) ascertain the identity of each beneficial owner;
>   - (c) take reasonable measures to verify that identity using objectively reliable and independent sources;
>   - (d) take reasonable measures to determine whether each beneficial owner is a politically-exposed individual or a family member or close associate of one;
>   - (e) understand the nature of the client's business; and
>   - (f) understand its ownership and control structure.
> - A "beneficial owner" is an individual who ultimately owns or controls the entity or arrangement, or on whose behalf it conducts the transaction, and includes one who exercises ultimate effective control (r 2).
> - Rule 9 requires ongoing scrutiny of transactions for consistency with the lawyer's knowledge of the client, its business, its risk profile and, where appropriate, the source of funds.
> - Rule 11 states that these measures must be performed before the start, or during the course, of establishing the business relationship, though verification may be completed as soon as reasonably practicable afterwards if that is necessary not to interrupt the normal conduct of business and the risks can be effectively managed.
> - Rule 12(4) states that the risks are raised if
>   - (a) the client is from or in, or the transaction relates to, a country for which the FATF has called for countermeasures or enhanced due diligence;
>   - (b) the client is from or in a country known to have inadequate anti-money-laundering measures;
>   - (c) the client is from or in, or the transaction relates to, a country under FATF increased monitoring; or
>   - (d) the lawyer has reason to believe that the client, a person acting for it, or the transaction presents a high risk.
> - Rule 13(1) requires enhanced due diligence where
>   - (a) the risks are raised under r 12(4);
>   - (b) the client or its beneficial owner is a foreign politically-exposed individual, or a family member or close associate of one; or
>   - (c) the relationship is assessed as higher risk and the client or beneficial owner is a domestic politically-exposed individual, a person with a prominent function in an international organisation, or a family member or close associate of either; and r 13(2) states that the enhanced measures are
>   - (a) senior management approval before establishing or continuing the relationship;
>   - (b) reasonable measures to establish the source of wealth and source of funds of the client and any beneficial owner;
>   - (c) enhanced ongoing monitoring; and
>   - (d) all other reasonable measures.
> - Rule 15 states that where the lawyer is unable to complete any due diligence measure, because it cannot obtain or verify the information or gets no satisfactory response, it
>   - (a) must not commence any new business relationship, and must terminate any existing one;
>   - (b) must not undertake any transaction for the client; and
>   - (c) must consider whether to file a suspicious transaction report.
> - Rule 5(1) states that where the lawyer has reasonable grounds to suspect that a client may be engaged in money laundering, the financing of terrorism or proliferation financing, the lawyer
>   - (a) must disclose the suspicion, or the information on which it is based, by filing a suspicious transaction report with a Suspicious Transaction Reporting Officer or a police officer or Commercial Affairs Officer; and
>   - (b) must consider whether it is appropriate to continue the relationship, or to establish a new one or undertake a new matter; under r 5(2), if the lawyer continues, it must
>   - (a) substantiate and document its reasons; and
>   - (b) subject the relationship to commensurate risk mitigation measures, including enhanced ongoing monitoring.
> - Rule 16 states that where the lawyer
>   - (a) suspects the client may be so engaged and
>   - (b) has reason to believe that performing due diligence will tip off the client or those acting for or through it, the lawyer
>   - (c) need not perform those measures but
>   - (d) must instead file a suspicious transaction report.
> - The lawyer must document the basis for its decision whether to file a report (r 25), and must keep records relating to each relevant matter for at least 5 years after the matter is completed (r 19) and due diligence records for at least 5 years after the business relationship ends (r 20).
> - PD 3.2.1 para 4.1 states that failure to make a required report is an offence punishable with a fine of up to $250,000, imprisonment of up to 3 years, or both, and that a report made in good faith does not breach any restriction on disclosure under law, contract or the rules of professional conduct; para 1.2(b) warns that telling anyone, including the client, that a report has been made may amount to tipping off.
> - A breach of Part 5A or the AML Rules amounting to improper conduct is due cause for discipline (s 83(2)(b)(ii) LPA).

**P** Law firms are attractive to money launderers, which puts an added imperative on lawyers to be particularly vigilant; a lawyer who remitted and transferred money for a client without further background checks, although the transactions were unusual in the ordinary course of business, risked exposing the profession to assisting criminal activity (*Law Society of Singapore v Chan Chun Hwee Allan* [2018] SGHC 21 at [38]).

**A** First, the matter is a relevant matter because [it involves managing the client's money through the client account / buying property / forming a company / it is unusual given its size and lack of economic purpose]. Second, on r 6 and r 11, [Lawyer] [did not verify the client's identity from independent sources before acting / did not check whether he is a politically-exposed individual]. Third, on r 8, [the client is a BVI company and Lawyer never identified or verified who ultimately owns or controls it]. Fourth, the risk is raised under r 12(4) because [the funds come from a FATF-listed jurisdiction / the client is a foreign minister's son], so r 13 required senior management approval, source of wealth and funds checks and enhanced monitoring, none of which [Lawyer] did. Fifth, when [the client refused to name the beneficial owner], r 15 required [Lawyer] to stop acting and consider a report. Sixth, [large cash sums paid by a third party with no underlying transaction] gave reasonable grounds to suspect, so r 5 required a report, and r 16 allowed [Lawyer] to skip further checks that would tip the client off but not the report itself. **C** [Lawyer] breached r [6 / 8 / 13 / 15 / 5] of the AML Rules and should file a suspicious transaction report without tipping off the client, documenting the basis under r 25.

*Source note: s 70A(2), 70B–70E LPA p174–176; s 83(2)(b)(ii) LPA p217–218; AML Rules r 2 p3–8, r 5 p9, r 6 p9–10, r 7 p11–12, r 8 p12–14, r 9 p14–15, r 11 p17–18, r 12 p18–19, r 13 p20–21, r 15 p23, r 16 p23–24, r 19 p28–29, r 20 p31, r 25 p35 (version in force from 1 July 2025, now titled "Prevention of Money Laundering, Financing of Terrorism and Proliferation Financing"); PD 3.2.1 relevant matter p5, paras 1.2 p11–12, 1.5 p13–14, 4.1 p45 (PD 3.2.1 cites the old CDSA section numbers; s 70D LPA now refers to s 45(1) CDSA); Chan Chun Hwee Allan L10 p30, SYL p12. L10 p28 says a lawyer with grounds for suspicion "must not establish any new business relationship"; that reflects the old r 5, and the current r 5(1)(b)–(2) is followed here. Cyril §VII.3 (secondary) also predates the 2025 amendment.*

### eth-w-0-70 — Fee information; taxation and review of bills
*Keywords: fee information, r 17(3), basis of charging, reasonably foreseeable payments, estimates, substantial variation, informed in writing, r 17(4), contentious matter, costs warnings, solicitor and client costs, adverse costs, r 17(5), assessment, taxation, review fee agreement, query about bill, r 17(6), larger amount on assessment, GN 7.1.1, Andre Ravindran, at their peril*

**I** The issue is whether [Lawyer] gave [client] the required information on fees and assessment. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 17[(3) / (4) / (5)] PCR, which requires a lawyer to [tell the client the basis of charging and keep to estimates / explain the costs risks at the start of a contentious matter / inform the client in writing of the right to have a disputed bill assessed].
**R** A lawyer must tell the client the basis of
charging and how fees and disbursements are paid, other foreseeable payments and when they
fall due, estimates if requested, and must not depart substantially from estimates without
telling the client in writing (r 17(3) PCR). At the start of a contentious matter, and at
appropriate stages, the lawyer must explain that the client pays their own solicitor-and-client
costs regardless of any costs order, may have to pay the other side's costs on losing, and may
not recover full costs on winning (r 17(4)). If the client disputes or queries a bill, the
lawyer must tell the client in writing of the right to have it assessed or the fee agreement
reviewed, unless the client knows or ought to know of it (r 17(5)); on assessment, the lawyer
must explain that a larger amount may be claimed and allowed (r 17(6)).

> **Rule in full**
> - Rule 17(3) PCR states that a legal practitioner must
>   - (a) inform the client of the basis on which fees for professional services will be charged, and of the manner in which those fees and disbursements (if any) are to be paid;
>   - (b) inform the client of any other reasonably foreseeable payments that the client may have to make, either to the legal practitioner or to any other party, and of the stages at which those payments are likely to be required;
>   - (c) to the extent reasonably practicable and if requested by the client, provide estimates of the fees and other payments in (a) and (b); and
>   - (d) ensure that the actual amounts do not vary substantially from those estimates, unless the client has been informed in writing of any changed circumstances.
> - Rule 17(4) states that the legal practitioner must explain to the client, at the beginning of a contentious matter and at other appropriate stages,
>   - (a) that in any event the client is personally responsible for paying the client's own solicitor and client costs in full, regardless of any order for costs made against any other party;
>   - (b) that if the client loses, the client may have to pay the client's own costs, and the court may order the client to pay the costs of one or more other parties as assessed or fixed by the court; and
>   - (c) that even if the client wins, the other party
>     - (i) may not be ordered to pay the full amount of the client's own costs; and
>     - (ii) may not be capable of paying what has been ordered.
> - Rule 17(5) states that if a client disputes or raises a query about a bill (whether or not in a contentious matter), the legal practitioner must inform the client in writing of the client's right to apply to the court to have the bill assessed or to review any fee agreement, unless the legal practitioner believes that the client knows, or reasonably ought to know, of that right.
> - Rule 17(6) states that if the client consents to assessment, or the court orders it, the legal practitioner must inform the client that
>   - (a) delivering a bill does not preclude the legal practitioner from presenting a bill for a larger amount, or otherwise, for assessment; and
>   - (b) on assessment the legal practitioner is entitled to any amount allowed by the Registrar, even if it is more than the amount in any bill previously delivered.
> - GN 7.1.1 para 3 repeats the r 17(5) duty; para 5 states that a solicitor who believes the client already knows of the right (for example, from a previous retainer) may decide not to inform the client, but should bear in mind that those who fail to do so act at their peril; and para 6 states that solicitors should seek to resolve costs disputes by negotiation or mediation.

**P** All solicitors should act on the basis that their bills can be taxed and have an obligation to inform clients of this option; a solicitor who offers taxation is unlikely to have the frame of mind or intention to overcharge, and even a prima facie excessive bill can usually be remedied by an offer of taxation, while those who fail to inform clients "do so at their peril" (*Law Society of Singapore v Andre Ravindran Saravanapavan Arul* [2011] 4 SLR 1184 at [32]–[33]).

**A** On r 17(3), [Lawyer] [never told the client the hourly rate or how disbursements would be billed / gave an estimate of $X but billed $3X without telling the client in writing of changed circumstances]. On r 17(4), this was a contentious matter, and [Lawyer] [did not explain that the client pays her own costs in full regardless of any costs order, that she may pay the other side's costs if she loses, or that she may not recover full costs even if she wins]. On r 17(5), [client] [queried the bill]; [Lawyer] [threatened to sue instead of telling her in writing of her right to have it assessed or the fee agreement reviewed], and there is nothing to suggest she already knew of the right. If assessment follows, r 17(6) requires [Lawyer] to warn her that a larger amount may be claimed and allowed. **C** [Lawyer] breached r 17[(3) / (4) / (5)] and should now inform the client in writing of the right to assessment and seek to resolve the dispute by negotiation or mediation.

*Source note: r 17(3)–(6) PCR p32–33, L10 p35–38; GN 7.1.1 paras 2–6 p1–2; Andre Ravindran at [32]–[33] quoted in GN 7.1.1 and L10 p37, SYL p12; summary Cyril Master Notes §VII.4.2–4.4 (secondary).*
